### [1] [Understanding Asynchronous Inference Methods for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08168)
*Ayoub Agouzoul*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper systematically compares four methods (IT-RTC, TT-RTC, VLASH, A2C2) for mitigating observation staleness in asynchronous VLA robot control, finding A2C2 best for high delays, TT-RCT most robust training-based method with zero inference overhead, and VLASH showing a delay trade-off.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models have inference latency causing observation staleness during asynchronous action execution, and the four proposed mitigation methods have only been evaluated independently with different codebases, base policies, and protocols, lacking a fair systematic comparison.

Method: The authors developed two unified codebases integrating all four methods with harmonized library/dataset versions, benchmarked on Kinetix suite (MLPMixer policies) and LIBERO manipulation benchmark (SmolVLA), sweeping inference delays up to d=20 control steps.

Result: A2C2's per-step residual correction is most effective on Kinetix (above 90% solve rate up to d=8) and leads on LIBERO from d=4 onward. IT-RTC competitive at low delays but degrades under long chunks (H=30) and high delays. TT-RTC is most robust training-based method, stable across d_max choices, generalizes beyond training delay, adds zero inference overhead. VLASH shows low-delay vs. high-delay trade-off governed by fine-tuning delay range.

Conclusion: Among the four methods for mitigating observation staleness in asynchronous VLA inference, A2C2 provides the best performance at higher delays, while TT-RTC offers robustness and zero overhead. The study provides the first controlled systematic comparison of these concurrent approaches.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models offer a promising path to generalist robot control, but their inference latency causes observation staleness when generated actions are executed asynchronously. Several methods have been proposed concurrently to mitigate this problem: inference-time inpainting (IT-RTC), training-time delay simulation (TT-RTC), future-state-aware conditioning (VLASH), and lightweight residual correction (A2C2). Each takes a fundamentally different approach, but they have so far been evaluated independently with different codebases, base policies, and protocols. We present a systematic comparison of these four methods under controlled conditions. We develop two unified codebases that integrate all methods with harmonized library and dataset versions, and we benchmark them on the Kinetix suite with MLPMixer policies and on the LIBERO manipulation benchmark with SmolVLA, sweeping inference delays up to $d=20$ control steps. A2C2's per-step residual correction is the most effective method on Kinetix, holding above 90% solve rate up to $d=8$, and also leads on LIBERO from $d=4$ onwards. IT-RTC is competitive at low delays but degrades sharply under long chunks ($H=30$) and high delays. TT-RTC is the most robust training-based method: stable across $d_\max$ choices, generalizes beyond its training delay distribution, and adds zero inference overhead. VLASH exhibits a clear low-delay vs. high-delay trade-off governed by the fine-tuning delay range $[0,d_\max]$. Code is available at https://github.com/TheAyos/async-vla-inference

</details>


### [2] [Anatomical Landmark-Guided Deep Reinforcement Learning for Autonomous Gastric Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08269)
*Haoxuan Wu,Sishen Yuan,Haitao Gao,Zhen Li,Xiuli Zuo,Hongliang Ren*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes AL-DRL, an anatomical landmark-guided deep reinforcement learning framework for autonomous gastric navigation in wireless capsule endoscopy, achieving 97%+ simulated coverage and 87% ex-vivo coverage with 53% faster procedure time than expert manual control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: WCE enables painless GI tract visualization, but is limited by incomplete mucosal coverage and poor transferability of existing navigation methods across different patient anatomies.

Method: AL-DRL framework with a lightweight edge-contour-depth fusion module that operates on stable, low-dimensional landmark coordinates instead of high-dimensional video streams; two-stage sim-to-real pipeline with adaptive dynamic programming controller for mitigating physical disturbances.

Result: Over 97% coverage within 50 seconds in simulations across eight patient-derived models, outperforming vanilla PPO, SAC, and DQN; 87% mean coverage ex-vivo with 53% reduction in procedure time vs. expert manual control.

Conclusion: Anatomical landmark-guided DRL with low-dimensional state representation effectively bridges sim-to-real gap and significantly improves autonomous WCE navigation performance and transferability across anatomies.

Abstract: Wireless capsule endoscopy (WCE) enables painless visualization of the gastrointestinal tract, but its diagnostic potential is limited by incomplete mucosal coverage and poor transferability of existing navigation methods across patient anatomies. We propose a transferable, anatomical landmarkguided deep reinforcement learning (AL-DRL) framework for autonomous gastric navigation. Leveraging a lightweight edgecontour-depth fusion module, our policy operates on stable, lowdimensional landmark coordinates rather than high-dimensional video streams, effectively bridging the sim-to-real gap. In simulations across eight patient-derived models, the method achieves over 97% coverage within 50 seconds, significantly outperforming vanilla PPO, SAC, and DQN agents. A two-stage sim-to-real pipeline with an adaptive dynamic programming controller actively mitigates physical disturbances. Ex-vivo experiments demonstrate a mean coverage of 87% and a 53% reduction in procedure time compared with expert manual control.

</details>


### [3] [Hierarchical Prompting with Dual LLM Modules for Robotic Task and Motion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08330)
*Karolina Źróbek,Tessa Pulli,Paweł Gajewski,Antonio Galiza Cerdeira Gonzalez,Bipin Indurkhya*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A hierarchical language-driven framework combining two LLM modules (high-level planning agent + low-level spatial reasoning sub-module) for robotic task and motion planning, achieving 86% task success across 24 test scenarios using YOLOX-GDRNet for perception and ReAct-style prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve natural, intuitive human-robot interaction in service and assistance scenarios by enabling robots to understand and execute natural language commands in real-world spatial environments.

Method: The system uses a two-level LLM architecture: (1) a high-level planning agent with ReAct-style prompt that processes commands and generates action sequences, interacting with object perception/manipulation tools; (2) a low-level spatial reasoning sub-module for 3D placement reasoning (e.g., "next to"). Integrates YOLOX-GDRNet for detection and pose estimation, plus a motion execution stub.

Result: Evaluated in 24 test scenarios (simple spatial commands, high-level instructions, infeasible requests) with an overall task success rate of 86%.

Conclusion: The hierarchical LLM-based approach effectively bridges natural language understanding and robotic spatial reasoning for task and motion planning, demonstrating strong performance in diverse service robot scenarios.

Abstract: We present a hierarchical language-driven framework for robotic task and motion planning to improve natural, intuitive human-robot interaction in service and assistance scenarios. The proposed system employs two large language model (LLM) modules: a high-level planning agent and a low-level spatial reasoning sub-module. The primary agent processes natural language commands and generates action sequences using a ReAct-style prompt, interacting with tools for object perception and manipulation (e.g., pick, place, release). For precise spatial placement, such as interpreting "place the mug next to the plate", a separate sub-prompting module handles 3D reasoning based on object geometry and scene layout. The system integrates YOLOX-GDRNet for object detection and pose estimation, along with a motion execution stub. We evaluated the system in 24 test scenarios, ranging from simple spatial commands to high-level instructions and infeasible requests. The system achieved an overall task success rate of 86%.

</details>


### [4] [Failing Forward: Adaptive Failure-Informed Learning for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08434)
*Meng Zheng,Samhita Marri,Anwesa Choudhuri,Benjamin Planche,Zhongpai Gao,Van Nguyen Nguyen,Terrence Chen,Girish Chowdhary,Ziyan Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AFIL enhances VLA robotic policies by training dual generators for success and failure trajectories, using failure data as adaptive negative guidance to steer actions away from failure modes, improving success rates and robustness across manipulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models trained solely on success demonstrations via behavioral cloning suffer from compounding errors during execution, as they lack corrective training signals from failure trajectories, leading to unrecoverable out-of-distribution failures.

Method: AFIL uses a pretrained VLA to generate online failure rollouts, then jointly trains Dual Action Generators (DAGs) for success and failure behaviors sharing a vision-language backbone. During sampling, the failure generator adaptively steers action generation away from failure regions, with guidance strength determined by per-step distribution distances.

Result: Experiments on in-domain and out-of-domain robotic manipulation tasks (short- and long-horizon) show AFIL consistently improves task success rates and robustness over existing VLA baselines.

Conclusion: AFIL effectively addresses VLA brittleness by incorporating failure-aware training without manual failure design or human intervention, demonstrating efficiency, generality, and improved robustness.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models provide a promising paradigm for scalable robotic manipulation, yet their reliance on success-only behavioral cloning leaves them brittle; lacking corrective training signals, minor execution errors rapidly compound into unrecoverable, out-of-distribution failures. To address this limitation, we propose Adaptive Failure-Informed Learning (AFIL), an end-to-end framework that leverages failure trajectories as adaptive negative guidance for diffusion- and flow-based VLA policies. AFIL uses a pretrained VLA to generate failure rollouts online, avoiding the need for handcrafted failure-mode design or human-in-the-loop recovery. It then jointly trains Dual Action Generators (DAGs) for successful and failed behaviors while sharing a common vision-language backbone, enabling efficient failure-aware policy learning with limited parameter overhead. During sampling, the failure generator adaptively steers action generation away from failure-prone regions and toward more reliable success modes, with guidance strength determined by the per-diffusion-step distance between success and failure distributions. Experiments across in-domain and out-of-domain robotic manipulation tasks, covering both short- and long-horizon settings, show that AFIL consistently improves task success rates and robustness over existing VLA baselines, demonstrating its effectiveness, efficiency, and generality.

</details>


### [5] [LE-PAVD: Learning-Enhanced Physics-Aware Vehicle Dynamics for High-Speed Autonomous Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08489)
*Musabbir Ahmed Arrafi,Malik Ali,Nicholas M. Stiffler,Krishna Bhavithavya Kidambi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LE-PAVD is a hybrid vehicle dynamics model that integrates physics priors (Pacejka tire forces, load transfer, actuator limits) with learned components, achieving 16.1% lower ADE, 20.6% lower FDE, 91.3% lower yaw-rate RMSE, 21.6% fewer FLOPs, and 1.5× faster inference than deep baselines, with 17.4% and 9.5% faster lap times on training and test tracks respectively in closed-loop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate nonlinear vehicle dynamics modeling is critical for high-speed autonomous racing at handling limits; model-based methods are interpretable but oversimplify, while purely learned models capture nonlinearities but lack physical consistency and generalization.

Method: LE-PAVD augments a physics-aware backbone with four learned components: load-sensitive Pacejka tire forces, longitudinal load transfer, lateral tire-force effects, and rate-limited actuator inputs; trained end-to-end on both simulation and real-world telemetry data.

Result: On an unseen track: ADE reduced by 16.1%, FDE by 20.6%, yaw-rate RMSE by 91.3%; uses 21.6% fewer FLOPs and achieves ~1.50× faster inference; closed-loop lap times improved by 17.4% (training track) and 9.5% (test track) with zero boundary violations.

Conclusion: LE-PAVD provides a compact, physics-grounded dynamics backbone that improves both predictive fidelity and closed-loop performance while reducing computational cost, outperforming purely learned baselines.

Abstract: Accurate modeling of nonlinear vehicle dynamics is essential for high-speed autonomous racing, where controllers operate at the handling limits. Model-based methods are interpretable but rely on simplifying assumptions, while purely learned models capture nonlinearities yet often lack physical consistency and generalization. We propose LE-PAVD (Learning-Enhanced Physics-Aware Vehicle Dynamics), a hybrid model that integrates physics priors with learned components. Our architecture adds four components: load-sensitive Pacejka tire forces, longitudinal load transfer, lateral tire-force effects, and rate-limited actuator inputs. Trained end-to-end on simulation and real-world telemetry, LE-PAVD enforces physical consistency while improving state prediction accuracy. On an unseen track, LE-PAVD reduces average displacement error (ADE) by 16.1$\%$, final displacement error (FDE) by 20.6$\%$, and lowers yaw-rate root mean squared error (RMSE) by 91.3$\%$ versus a deep dynamics baseline, while using 21.6$\%$ fewer FLOPs and achieving approximately 1.50$\times$ faster inference. In closed-loop simulations, LE-PAVD consistently outperforms the baseline by achieving faster lap times by 17.4$\%$ on a training track and 9.5$\%$ on a test track, without any track boundary violations. Overall, LE-PAVD offers a compact, physics-grounded dynamics backbone that improves predictive fidelity and closed-loop performance while reducing inference cost.

</details>


### [6] [Trajectory-Consistent Flow Matching for Robust Visuomotor Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08511)
*Riad Ahmed,Sujosh Nag,Moniruzzaman Akash,Mostafa Hussein,Momotaz Begum*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes four complementary remedies (auxiliary velocity regression, multi-step trajectory consistency training, velocity field regularization, and RK4 inference) to address the train-inference mismatch in flow matching policies for robot manipulation, achieving significant success on real-robot and simulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard flow matching training optimizes a pointwise velocity objective while inference requires numerical integration of that field, causing a train-inference mismatch that leads to compounding trajectory errors in robot manipulation.

Method: Four complementary remedies: (1) auxiliary rectified flow velocity regression for uniform temporal supervision; (2) multi-step trajectory consistency training to directly close the train-inference gap; (3) velocity field regularization for temporal smoothness; (4) fourth-order Runge-Kutta (RK4) inference to reduce discretization error. Paired with a dual-view 3D point cloud encoder using two independent PointNet encoders.

Result: On four real-robot tasks (Franka arm and Boston Dynamics Spot), achieves 70% and 60% success on two long-horizon multi-phase tasks where baselines score 0%, 100% on precision tool placement. Three MetaWorld simulation tasks confirm consistent improvements.

Conclusion: Trajectory-level supervision is essential for reliable policy execution; the four components are complementary and individually insufficient — RK4 without smooth velocity field fails, and smoothness without trajectory-level supervision still drifts.

Abstract: Flow matching policies learn continuous velocity fields that transport noise to actions, enabling fast deterministic inference for robot manipulation. However, standard training optimizes a pointwise velocity objective while inference requires numerical integration of that field -- a mismatch that causes compounding trajectory errors. We propose four complementary remedies: (1) auxiliary rectified flow velocity regression that provides uniform temporal supervision across the full time interval; (2) multi-step trajectory consistency training that supervises the integrated displacement of the velocity field over trajectory segments, directly closing the train-inference gap; (3) velocity field regularization that enforces temporal smoothness, preventing oscillations that destabilize integration; and (4) fourth-order Runge-Kutta (RK4) inference that reduces global discretization error by orders of magnitude over Euler methods. Critically, these components are not independently sufficient -- RK4 without a smooth velocity field fails, and smoothness without trajectory-level supervision still drifts, as our ablation study confirms. We further pair these with a dual-view 3D point cloud encoder using two independent PointNet encoders for complementary spatial perception. On four real-robot tasks across a Franka arm and a Boston Dynamics Spot, our method achieves 70% and 60% overall success on two long-horizon multi-phase tasks where both baselines score 0%, and reaches 100% on precision tool placement. Three MetaWorld simulation tasks confirm consistent improvements, validating that trajectory-level supervision is essential for reliable policy execution.

</details>


### [7] [Model-Reference Adaptive Flight Control of the 95-mg Bee++](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08525)
*Francisco M. F. R. Gonçalves,Conor K. Trygstad,Néstor O. Pérez-Arancibia*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A model-reference adaptive control architecture is introduced for high-performance positional tracking of a 95-mg insect-scale flapping-wing aerial vehicle (Bee++), validated through real-time flight experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve high-performance positional tracking for the Bee++, a lightweight (95-mg) insect-scale flapping-wing aerial vehicle, which presents significant control challenges due to its small scale and complex flight dynamics.

Method: A model-reference adaptive control (MRAC) architecture designed specifically for the Bee++ vehicle.

Result: Demonstrated suitability, functionality, and high performance of the MRAC approach via real-time flight experiments.

Conclusion: The proposed MRAC architecture is effective for high-performance positional tracking of insect-scale flapping-wing aerial vehicles.

Abstract: We introduce a model-reference adaptive control (MRAC) architecture for high-performance positional tracking of the Bee++, a 95-mg insect-scale flapping-wing aerial vehicle. The suitability, functionality, and high performance of the proposed approach are demonstrated using data from real-time flight experiments.

</details>


### [8] [BEACON: Cross-Domain Co-Training of Generative Robot Policies via Best-Effort Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08571)
*Antong Zhang,Han Qi,Heng Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: BEACON is a theory-driven framework for cross-domain co-training of generative robot policies that uses discrepancy-aware importance reweighting to effectively learn from abundant source and limited target demonstrations, achieving robust performance across sim-to-sim, sim-to-real, and multi-source settings without explicit feature alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable efficient cross-domain adaptation for robot policy learning when abundant source-domain demonstrations are available but only limited target-domain demonstrations exist, addressing the gap between source and target distributions without requiring explicit domain alignment.

Method: BEACON formulates cross-domain co-training as a discrepancy-aware importance-reweighting problem, jointly learning a diffusion-based visuomotor policy and per-sample source weights guided by target-domain generalization guarantees. It introduces scalable instance-level discrepancy estimators, stochastic alternating optimization, and multi-source extension for heterogeneous domains.

Result: Across sim-to-sim, sim-to-real, and multi-source manipulation benchmarks, BEACON outperforms target-only training, fixed-ratio co-training, and feature-alignment baselines in terms of robustness and data efficiency. It also achieves implicit feature alignment without an explicit alignment objective.

Conclusion: Discrepancy-aware importance reweighting provides a principled and effective alternative to explicit domain alignment for cross-domain robot policy learning, enabling best-effort adaptation with practical scalability to high-dimensional sequence policies.

Abstract: We introduce BEACON--Best-Effort Adaptation for Cross-Domain Co-Training--a theory-driven framework for training generative robot policies with abundant source demonstrations and limited target demonstrations. BEACON casts cross-domain co-training as a discrepancy-aware importance-reweighting problem, jointly learning a diffusion-based visuomotor policy and per-sample source weights that minimize an objective informed by target-domain generalization guarantees. To make best-effort adaptation practical for high-dimensional sequence policies, we develop scalable instance-level discrepancy estimators, stochastic alternating updates for policy and weights, and a multi-source extension that balances heterogeneous source domains. Across sim-to-sim, sim-to-real, and multi-source manipulation settings, BEACON improves robustness and data efficiency over target-only, fixed-ratio co-training, and feature-alignment baselines. Importantly, even without an explicit alignment objective, BEACON achieves feature alignment as an implicit result of discrepancy-aware cross-domain co-training.

</details>


### [9] [ATAAT: Adaptive Threat-Aware Adversarial Tuning Framework against Backdoor Attacks on Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08612)
*Kewei Chen,Yayu Long,Shuai Li,Mingsheng Shang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper identifies "Gradient Interference" as the core obstacle causing traditional backdoor attack failures in VLA models, and proposes an Adaptive Threat-Aware Adversarial Tuning (ATAAT) framework that uses a "Threat-Method Adaptive Mapping" mechanism to select optimal gradient decoupling strategies, achieving high attack success rates (>80% TASR) with only 5% poisoning rate while maintaining stealthiness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address escalating security vulnerabilities in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models by investigating and overcoming the failure of traditional backdoor attack paradigms, specifically the "Gradient Interference" phenomenon caused by conflicting optimization strategies during end-to-end training.

Method: The proposed Adaptive Threat-Aware Adversarial Tuning (ATAAT) framework employs a core "Threat-Method Adaptive Mapping" mechanism that intelligently selects the optimal gradient decoupling strategy based on the adversary's capabilities, enabling implicit decoupled attacks in data poisoning scenarios.

Result: ATAAT achieves a highly robust Targeted Attack Success Rate (TASR > 80%) with extreme stealthiness at only 5% poisoning rate, efficiently handles complex semantic-level triggers, and achieves implicit decoupled attacks in data poisoning scenarios for the first time.

Conclusion: This work reveals a critical security vulnerability in VLA models and provides theoretical and methodological support for future defense architectures.

Abstract: Addressing the escalating security vulnerabilities in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, this study investigates backdoor attacks targeting the visual pathway. We identify a core obstacle causing the failure of traditional attack paradigms: "Gradient Interference." This phenomenon represents an optimization failure triggered by conflicting strategies during end-to-end training. To resolve this, we propose an Adaptive Threat-Aware Adversarial Tuning (ATAAT) framework. Through its core "Threat-Method Adaptive Mapping" mechanism, ATAAT intelligently selects the optimal gradient decoupling strategy based on the adversary's capabilities. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ATAAT exhibits significant advantages, achieving a highly robust Targeted Attack Success Rate (TASR > 80%) while maintaining extreme stealthiness with merely a 5% poisoning rate. It efficiently handles complex semantic-level triggers and achieves implicit decoupled attacks in data poisoning scenarios for the first time. This work reveals a critical security vulnerability in VLAs and provides theoretical and methodological support for future defense architectures.

</details>


### [10] [Geometry Guided Self-Consistency for Physical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08638)
*Yinwei Dai,Zhuofu Chen,Lijie Yang,Ravi Netravali*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: KeyStone is an inference-time self-consistency method that improves diffusion-based physical AI action generation by sampling K candidate action chunks in parallel, clustering them in continuous action space, and returning the medoid of the largest cluster—boosting task success rates by up to 13.3% with no additional latency or training cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion-based physical AI models generate a single action trajectory per inference round, which is brittle due to inherent stochasticity; this brittleness compounds across sequential rounds in an episode, leading to degraded task performance.

Method: KeyStone proposes inference-time self-consistency: it draws K candidate action chunks in parallel from a shared model context (leveraging spare compute from memory-bandwidth-bound diffusion inference), clusters them using Euclidean distance in continuous action space, and selects the medoid of the largest cluster—no learned judge or additional model required.

Result: Across diverse VLAs and WAMs, KeyStone improves task success rates by up to 13.3% over single-trajectory sampling with negligible latency overhead, and achieves accuracy on par with model-based selectors at no training cost.

Conclusion: KeyStone demonstrates that simple geometric self-consistency in action space is a practical, training-free, and latency-efficient method to robustify diffusion-based physical AI action generation.

Abstract: State-of-the-art physical AI models generate a chunk of actions per inference through diffusion or flow matching, iteratively refining an initial noise sample into an action trajectory. Because this inference process is inherently stochastic, committing to a single trajectory per round is brittle, and this brittleness compounds across the many sequential rounds that comprise a complete episode. We introduce KeyStone, an inference-time self-consistency method for diffusion-based action generation that draws $K$ candidate action chunks in parallel from a shared model context, clusters them in continuous action space, and returns the medoid of the largest cluster -- no additional model required. Two properties make this practical. First, the compact nature of action trajectories makes diffusion inference memory-bandwidth bound, leaving spare compute capacity to run $K$ chains in parallel with no additional wall-clock latency. Second, unlike token or pixel spaces where distance carries no semantic meaning and selection requires a learned judge, action chunks are geometrically structured such that Euclidean distance directly reflects physical similarity, making selection principled and judge-free. Across diverse vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs), KeyStone improves task success rates by up to \textbf{13.3\%} over single-trajectory sampling with negligible latency overhead, while having on par accuracy with model-based selectors at no training cost. We open source KeyStone at https://github.com/dywsjtu/keystone.

</details>


### [11] [REAP: Reinforcement-Learning End-to-End Autonomous Parking with Gaussian Splatting Simulator for Real2Sim2Real Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08713)
*Changze Li,Zhe Chen,Shaoyu Chen,Lisen Mu,Yijian Li,Yuelong Yu,Qian Zhang,Qing Su,Ming Yang,Tong Qin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: REAP is an end-to-end autonomous parking method using asymmetric reinforcement learning (SAC), behavior cloning from rule-based planners, a soft predictive collision penalty, and a Real2Sim2Real 3DGS simulator to achieve successful parking in extreme scenarios like narrow mechanical slots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional parking methods use a multistage approach lacking holistic optimization, causing failures in extreme scenarios (mechanical/dead-end slots). End-to-end methods using imitation learning are limited by data cost/distribution coverage, while reinforcement learning suffers from inefficient exploration.

Method: REAP uses Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) in an asymmetric reinforcement learning framework, distills rule-based planner knowledge via behavior cloning, introduces a soft predictive collision penalty mechanism, and builds a Real2Sim2Real simulator using 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) for real-to-digital scene transformation and Sim2Real deployment.

Result: REAP successfully parks in various parking space types, particularly demonstrating feasibility of end-to-end RL parking in extremely narrow mechanical slots, both in simulation and on physical vehicles.

Conclusion: The asymmetric RL framework combined with behavior cloning, collision penalty, and 3DGS-based sim-to-real transfer effectively solves extreme parking scenarios where traditional methods fail.

Abstract: In recent years, autonomous parking has made significant advances, yet parking tasks still face challenges in extreme scenarios such as mechanical and dead-end parking slots, often resulting in failures. This is mainly due to traditional parking methods adopting a multistage approach, lacking the ability to optimize the parking problem as a whole. End-to-end methods enable joint optimization across perception and planning modules to eliminate the accumulation of errors, enhancing algorithm performance in extreme scenarios. Although several end-to-end parking methods use imitation or reinforcement learning, the former is limited by data cost and distribution coverage, while the latter suffers from inefficient exploration. To address these challenges, we propose a Reinforcement learning End-to-end Autonomous Parking method (REAP). REAP employs Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) within an asymmetric reinforcement learning framework to improve training efficiency and inference performance. To accelerate model convergence, we distill the capabilities of a rule-based planner into the end-to-end network through behavior cloning. We further introduce a soft predictive collision penalty mechanism to reduce collision rates by penalizing obstacle-approaching actions. To ensure that the trained reinforcement learning network can directly transfer to real-world scenarios, we have established a Real2Sim2Real simulator. In the Real2Sim step, we use 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) to transform real-world scenes into digital scenes. In the Sim2Real step, we deploy the end-to-end model onto the vehicle to bridge the Sim2Real gap. Trained in the 3DGS simulator and deployed on physical vehicles, REAP successfully parks in various types of parking spaces, especially demonstrating the feasibility of end-to-end RL parking in extremely narrow mechanical slots.

</details>


### [12] [HULK: Large-scale Hierarchical Coordination under Continual and Uncertain Temporal Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08722)
*Qingyuan Luo,Jie Li,Meng Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HULK is a hierarchical framework for coordinating large-scale multi-agent systems under continual, uncertain temporal tasks. It interleaves rolling assignment of tasks to subteams (within a horizon) with dynamic intra-subteam coordination during execution, improving computational efficiency and robustness over offline integer programming approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent coordination methods assume static, known tasks solvable offline via integer programming. However, real-world applications (e.g., delivery, search and rescue) involve online task generation with uncertain subtask counts, rendering offline solutions impractical due to constant re-computation and global communication overhead.

Method: A hierarchical framework (HULK) with two interleaved layers: (1) a rolling assignment layer that allocates currently known tasks to subteams within a specific horizon; (2) a dynamic coordination layer that manages intra-subteam execution based on detected subtasks during online operation. Tasks are specified as temporal logic formulas over collaborative actions.

Result: The method is rigorously validated on large-scale heterogeneous systems under various temporal tasks and environmental uncertainties, demonstrating improved computational efficiency and robustness compared to offline baselines.

Conclusion: Hierarchical coordination at different granularities and triggering conditions effectively handles continual, uncertain temporal tasks in large-scale multi-agent systems, offering a scalable alternative to global offline optimization.

Abstract: Multi-agent systems can be extremely efficient when working concurrently and collaboratively, e.g., for delivery, surveillance, search and rescue. Coordination of such teams often involves two aspects: selecting appropriate subteams for different tasks in various areas, and coordinating agents in the subteams to execute the associated subtasks. Existing work often assumes that the tasks are static and known beforehand, where an integer program can be formulated and solved offline. However, in many applications, the team-wise tasks are generated online continually by external requests, and the amount of subtasks within each task is uncertain, e.g., the number of packages to deliver or victims to rescue. The aforementioned offline solution becomes inadequate as it would require constant re-computation for the whole team and global communication to broadcast the results. Thus, this work tackles the large-scale coordination problem under continual and uncertain temporal tasks, specified as temporal logic formulas over collaborative actions. The proposed hierarchical framework, HULK, consists of two interleaved layers: the rolling assignment of currently known tasks to subteams within a certain horizon, and the dynamic coordination within a subteam given the detected subtasks during online execution. Thus, coordination is performed hierarchically at different granularities and triggering conditions, improving computational efficiency and robustness. The method is validated rigorously over large-scale heterogeneous systems under various temporal tasks and environment uncertainties.

</details>


### [13] [Latent Geometry Beyond Search: Amortizing Planning in World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08732)
*Hoang Nguyen,Xiaohao Xu,Xiaonan Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes replacing expensive online planning in latent world models with a lightweight Goal-Conditioned Inverse Dynamics Model (GC-IDM) that directly maps latent states to actions, achieving comparable or better performance at 100-130x lower cost across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When does a learned representation simplify control rather than merely enabling prediction? The authors investigate whether structured latent spaces (smooth, uniform) can amortize planning into learned inference instead of requiring expensive online search.

Method: Using a pretrained LeWorldModel with regularized latent geometry, the authors replace iterative planning (CEM, MPPI, iCEM, gradient-based methods) with a Goal-Conditioned Inverse Dynamics Model (GC-IDM) that predicts the next action given current latent, goal latent, and remaining horizon.

Result: Across four benchmarks (navigation, contact-rich manipulation, continuous control), GC-IDM matches or exceeds CEM in 7/8 settings while reducing per-decision cost by 100-130x. A sweep over multiple planners confirms the result is not optimizer-specific.

Conclusion: Sufficiently structured latent spaces can shift part of the planning burden from online optimization to learned inference, as much of the structure recovered by test-time planning is already locally encoded in the latent representation.

Abstract: Modern vision-based world models can represent observations as compact yet expressive latent manifolds, but fast goal-oriented planning in these spaces remains challenging. This raises a central question: when does a learned representation simplify control, rather than merely enabling prediction? We study this question in a pretrained LeWorldModel, whose latent geometry is regularized for smoothness and uniformity. Our key insight is that, under such geometry, planning can be amortized into a latent inverse-dynamics mapping instead of requiring online search. We therefore replace iterative planning with a lightweight Goal-Conditioned Inverse Dynamics Model (GC-IDM) that maps the current latent state, goal latent state, and remaining horizon directly to the next action. Empirically, across four benchmark environments spanning navigation, contact-rich manipulation, and continuous control, our controller matches or exceeds CEM in seven of eight environment-protocol settings while reducing per-decision cost by 100-130x. A broader sweep over test-time planners (CEM, MPPI, iCEM, and gradient-based methods) shows that this result is not specific to a particular optimizer. These findings suggest that much of the structure recovered by test-time planning is already locally encoded in the latent representation. More broadly, our results indicate that sufficiently structured latent spaces can shift part of the planning burden from online optimization to learned inference.

</details>


### [14] [A Visuo-Tactile Data Collection System with Haptic Feedback for Coarse-to-Fine Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08757)
*Yeseung Kim,Nayoung Oh,Jun Park,Teetat Thamronglak,Daehyung Park*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A visuo-tactile data-collection system with direct-drive gripper preserving haptic feedback, tactile arrays, and real-time temporal annotation via push button, designed to produce multimodal datasets for coarse-to-fine imitation learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional demonstration systems decouple operators from contact forces, hindering demonstration of subtle force modulation needed for contact-rich manipulation tasks.

Method: Direct-drive gripper actuated by operator fingers (preserving natural haptic feedback) + integrated visual sensors + custom tactile arrays + handle-mounted push button for real-time temporal annotation of task-critical regions.

Result: Multimodal datasets fusing in-hand force perception with temporal structure annotations, designed for coarse-to-fine learning algorithms.

Conclusion: The system enables development of high-quality manipulation policies by exploiting structural task knowledge through temporally structured, contact-rich demonstrations.

Abstract: We present a visuo-tactile data-collection system that generates temporally structured, contact-rich demonstrations for imitation learning. Conventional systems often decouple the operator from contact forces, which hinders the demonstration of subtle force modulation. Our system introduces a direct-drive gripper that the operator actuates with the fingers, preserving natural haptic feedback. Integrated visual sensors and custom tactile arrays capture image streams and contact geometry. A handle-mounted push button enables the operator to annotate the task's temporal structure in real time by marking task-critical regions. By fusing in-hand force perception with in-situ temporal annotation, the system produces multimodal datasets designed for coarse-to-fine learning algorithms that exploit structural task knowledge, enabling the development of high-quality manipulation policies.

</details>


### [15] [Omni-scale Learning-based Sequential Decision Framework for Order Fulfillment of Tote-handling Robotic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08758)
*Jiaxin Liu,Peng Yang,Yuping Li,Xinyue Xie*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes OLSF-TRS, a generalized sequential decision framework combining combinatorial optimization with multi-agent reinforcement learning for order fulfillment in tote-handling robotic systems, achieving near-optimal performance on small-scale (optimality gap <3.5%) and significant improvements on large-scale (8-30%+ reduction in tote movements) over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The shift from pallets to totes in intralogistics (driven by e-commerce and small-batch production) has increased the need for tote-handling robotic systems. However, existing order-fulfillment decision mechanisms are tailored to specific systems and lack generalizability, motivating a unified, scalable framework.

Method: OLSF-TRS combines structured combinatorial optimization with multi-agent reinforcement learning to coordinate order, tote, and robot decisions in a unified sequential decision framework.

Result: On small-scale systems, average optimality gaps below 3.5% across two configurations. On large-scale scenarios, reduces total tote movements by 8-12% and over 30% compared to SOTA rule-based approaches, while maintaining real-time responsiveness.

Conclusion: OLSF-TRS provides an efficient, unified order fulfillment decision-making framework for tote-handling robotic systems, delivering cost reduction, lower energy consumption, and enhanced throughput stability for e-commerce and industrial logistics.

Abstract: Driven by the rapid expansion of e-commerce and small-batch production, the size of the intralogistics load unit of finished goods, semi-finished goods and raw materials is steadily shrinking. Totes are gradually replacing pallets as the primary handling and storage container. This shift has propelled tote-handling robotic systems to the forefront of automation order fulfillment centers. The order-fulfillment decisions of tote-handling robotic systems share a common order-tote-robot sequential decision-making nature. Existing studies primarily focus on decision mechanisms tailored to particular systems, making it difficult to generalize or transfer them to other contexts. We propose an Omni-scale Learning-based Sequential Decision Framework for Order Fulfillment of Tote-handling Robotic Systems (OLSF-TRS), a generalized and scalable sequential decision framework that combines structured combinatorial optimization with multi-agent reinforcement learning to coordinate order,tote, and robot decisions. On small-scale tote-handling robotic systems, OLSF-TRS achieves near-optimal performance with average optimality gaps below 3.5% across two distinct system configurations. In large-scale scenarios, OLSF-TRS consistently outperforms heuristic baselines across two different system types, reducing total tote movements by 8-12% and over 30% compared to SOTA rule-based approaches, while maintaining real-time responsiveness. These improvements translate into tangible operational benefits, including cost reduction, lower energy consumption, and enhanced throughput stability. The proposed framework delivers an efficient and unified order fulfillment decision-making framework for widely deployed tote-handling robotic systems,supporting high-quality order fulfillment in both e-commerce and industrial logistics sectors.

</details>


### [16] [ProcVLM: Learning Procedure-Grounded Progress Rewards for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08774)
*Youhe Feng,Hansen Shi,Haoyang Li,Xinlei Guo,Yang Wang,Chengyang Zhang,Jinkai Zhang,Xiaohan Zhang,Jie Tang,Jing Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ProcVLM is a progress-aware VLM that learns dense reward signals grounded in procedural structure and intra-stage visual change for long-horizon robotic manipulation, trained on 60M annotated frames from 30 embodied datasets via a novel supervision synthesis pipeline, and outperforms baselines in procedural reasoning and reward modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reward models rely on trajectory-level success labels or time-based interpolation, which conflate elapsed time with true task progress and fail to capture unfinished steps, stagnation, and failure states.

Method: ProcVLM (1) grounds progress estimation in procedural structure and intra-stage visual change, (2) adopts reasoning-before-estimation (infers remaining atomic actions before estimating progress), (3) synthesizes frame-level subtask-semantic annotations with progress budgets assigned per subtask and distributed based on intra-subtask visual change, (4) builds ProcCorpus-60M (30 embodied datasets, 60M annotated frames) and ProcVQA for procedure-aware pretraining with progress estimation, action segmentation, and future planning.

Result: ProcVLM improves embodied procedural reasoning and yields more discriminative trajectory-internal progress estimates than representative baselines on ProcVQA and reward-model benchmarks, supporting its use as a dense reward model for policy optimization.

Conclusion: Procedure-grounded progress estimation with reasoning-before-estimation and large-scale procedural supervision synthesis enables effective dense reward signals for long-horizon manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Long-horizon robotic manipulation requires dense feedback that reflects how a task advances through its procedural stages, not merely whether the final outcome is successful. Existing reward models often rely on trajectory-level success labels or time-based interpolation, which can conflate elapsed time with true task progress and therefore fail to capture unfinished steps, stagnation, and failure states. We present ProcVLM, a progress-aware vision-language model that learns procedure-grounded progress as a dense reward signal for manipulation. Rather than deriving progress from terminal outcomes or temporal proxies, ProcVLM grounds progress estimation in procedural structure and intra-stage visual change, and further adopts a reasoning-before-estimation paradigm that infers the remaining atomic actions before estimating task progress. Specifically, we construct this supervision by synthesizing frame-level subtask-semantic annotations, assigning progress budgets according to subtask structure, and distributing each budget based on intra-subtask visual change. To train ProcVLM at scale, we build a standardized procedural supervision synthesis pipeline and construct ProcCorpus-60M from 30 embodied datasets with 60M annotated frames, from which we derive ProcVQA for procedure-aware pretraining, with progress estimation as the central task alongside action segmentation and future planning. Experiments on ProcVQA and reward-model benchmarks show that ProcVLM improves embodied procedural reasoning and yields more discriminative trajectory-internal progress estimates than representative baselines, supporting its use as a dense reward model for downstream reward-guided policy optimization. Project page: https://procvlm.github.io/

</details>


### [17] [ElasticFlow: One-Step Physics-Consistent Policy with Elastic Time Horizons for Language-Guided Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08799)
*Kewei Chen,Yayu Long,Shuai Li,Mingsheng Shang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ElasticFlow is a distillation-free, physics-consistent one-step policy framework that achieves efficient 71Hz inference by directly modeling the average velocity field via Mean Field Theory and introducing Elastic Time Horizons to address temporal heterogeneity, outperforming state-of-the-art methods like OpenVLA and π0 on long-horizon tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion policies suffer from high latency due to iterative denoising, and existing acceleration methods compromise physical consistency. The goal is to achieve fast, physically consistent action generation.

Method: ElasticFlow uses a distillation-free approach that models the average velocity field directly from Mean Field Theory for one-step noise-to-action mapping, and introduces Elastic Time Horizons to tackle Temporal Heterogeneity and Spectral Bias via explicit control granularity encoding.

Result: Achieves efficient 1-NFE inference at approximately 71Hz; outperforms OpenVLA and π0 on long-horizon tasks; validated on LIBERO, CALVIN, and RoboTwin benchmarks.

Conclusion: ElasticFlow demonstrates efficient, robust, and semantically aligned control for embodied AI, balancing speed and physical consistency without distillation.

Abstract: Diffusion policies have demonstrated exceptional performance in embodied AI. However, their iterative denoising process results in high latency, and existing acceleration methods often sacrifice physical consistency. To address this, we propose ElasticFlow, a distillation-free, physics-consistent one-step policy framework. We reconstruct the Mean Field Theory by directly modeling the average velocity field, enabling a direct single-step mapping from noise to action. Addressing the Temporal Heterogeneity of robotic tasks, we introduce the Elastic Time Horizons mechanism. This mechanism effectively overcomes Spectral Bias by explicitly encoding control granularity, achieving efficient alignment between semantic instructions and physical execution horizons. Experiments on benchmarks such as LIBERO, CALVIN, and RoboTwin demonstrate that ElasticFlow achieves efficient 1-NFE inference (approximately 71Hz). Furthermore, it outperforms state-of-the-art methods, including OpenVLA and $π_0$, on long-horizon tasks, highlighting its potential for efficient, robust, and semantically aligned control.

</details>


### [18] [Constraint-Aware Diffusion Priors for High-Fidelity and Versatile Quadruped Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08804)
*Jianhui Chen,Ruixin Zhan,Liu Liu,Yang Cai,Ziqiao Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Diff-CAST replaces GAN-based discriminators with diffusion models for multi-modal stylistic reward modeling, combined with Symmetric Augmented Command Conditioning (SACC) and Constrained RL to enable safe, drift-free, and scalable quadruped locomotion learning from massive heterogeneous datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling RL+imitation learning frameworks to massive, multi-source datasets faces bottlenecks: GAN-based discriminators suffer mode collapse on diverse uncurated motion data; existing kinematic priors cause OOD heading drifts; unconstrained priors on hardware ignore actuator dynamics, posing safety risks.

Method: Diff-CAST uses diffusion models as stylistic reward discriminators (replacing GANs) for robust data scaling; introduces Symmetric Augmented Command Conditioning (SACC) for drift-free tracking; integrates Constrained RL for hardware safety compliance within a Sim2Real architecture.

Result: On a quadruped robot, Diff-CAST mitigates mode collapse, enables seamless transitions between diverse skills, and ensures robust, hardware-compliant locomotion.

Conclusion: Diffusion-based discriminators effectively replace GANs for large-scale heterogeneous motion data; SACC and Constrained RL together resolve OOD drift and actuator safety issues, enabling reliable real-world deployment of learned locomotion policies.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning combined with imitation learning has significantly advanced biomimetic quadrupedal locomotion. However, scaling these frameworks to massive, multi-source datasets exposes fundamental bottlenecks. First, traditional GAN-based discriminators are prone to mode collapse, struggling to capture diverse motion distributions from uncurated datasets. Second, existing kinematic priors suffer from out-of-distribution (OOD) tracking conflicts, leading to severe unintended heading drifts during complex maneuvers. Furthermore, deploying unconstrained priors to physical hardware poses critical safety risks by disregarding actuator dynamics. To overcome these challenges, we propose Diff-CAST (Diffusion-guided Constraint-Aware Symmetric Tracking), a novel motion prior framework leveraging the multi-modal distribution modeling capabilities of diffusion models for stylistic rewards. Diff-CAST effectively replaces traditional GAN discriminators, unlocking robust data scaling on heterogeneous collections. To ensure high-fidelity intent execution and reliable real-world deployment, we introduce a comprehensive Sim2Re architecture integrating Symmetric Augmented Command Conditioning (SACC) for drift-free tracking, and Constrained RL for hardware safety. Experiments on a quadruped demonstrate that Diff-CAST mitigates mode collapse, enables seamless transitions between diverse skills, and ensures robust, hardware-compliant locomotion.

</details>


### [19] [AssemPlanner: A Multi-Agent Based Task Planning Framework for Flexible Assembly System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08831)
*Chenhao Zhang,Chaoran Zhang,Zhaobo Xu,Yongbo Yang,Pingfa Feng,Long Zeng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AssemPlanner is a multi-agent task planning framework for flexible assembly systems that converts natural language task descriptions into sequential production operations using specialized agents and a ReAct-based reasoning engine.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing task planning methods for flexible assembly systems require time-consuming multi-expert configuration when establishing a production line for a new product.

Method: A multi-agent framework (AssemPlanner) with specialized agents (SchedAgent, KnowledgeAgent, LineBalanceAgent, scene graph) using ReAct-based adaptive reasoning with multi-agent feedback.

Result: The framework autonomously resolves complex industrial process constraints by observing feedback from KnowledgeAgent, LineBalanceAgent, and the scene graph.

Conclusion: AssemPlanner enables natural language-driven task planning for flexible assembly systems without the need for traditional static pipelines or extensive expert configuration.

Abstract: In flexible assembly systems, existing task planning methods require a time-consuming configuration process by multiple experts to establish a production line for a new product. To address this challenge, we propose a multi-agent based task planning framework for flexible assembly systems, denoted as AssemPlanner. It takes tasks described in natural language as input, which are then converted into actionable sequential production operations. It comprises several specialized agents, including SchedAgent , KnowledgeAgent, LineBalanceAgent, and a scene graph. Within the proposed framework, SchedAgent serves as the central reasoning engine. Departing from traditional static pipelines, AssemPlanner utilizes a ReAct-based SchedAgent to adaptively adjust actions via multi-agent feedback. By observing the feedback from KnowledgeAgent, LineBalanceAgent, and the scene graph, it autonomously resolves complex industrial process constraints. To facilitate reproducibility, all code and datasets are released at https://github.com/chz332/Assemplanner.

</details>


### [20] [Preserving Foundational Capabilities in Flow-Matching VLAs through Conservative SFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08879)
*Tianyi Zhang,Shaopeng Zhai,Haoran Zhang,Fuxian Huang,Qi Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ConSFT is a conservative fine-tuning method for flow-matching VLA models that prevents catastrophic forgetting by dynamically scaling gradients based on model confidence, achieving over 20% better capability retention than vanilla SFT without requiring prior data or reference networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unconstrained fine-tuning of flow-matching VLA models causes dense parameter overwrites that degrade pre-trained capabilities due to catastrophic forgetting.

Method: ConSFT dynamically scales learning signals based on model confidence, suppressing excessive gradients from low-confidence samples to bound intrinsic parameter disruption risk, inspired by trust-region clipping from reinforcement learning.

Result: Outperforms vanilla SFT by over 20% absolute margin in capability retention on LIBERO and RoboTwin benchmarks, matching data-heavy Experience Replay without prior data; real-world deployments confirm preservation of physical skills while acquiring sequential tasks.

Conclusion: ConSFT effectively mitigates catastrophic forgetting in VLA fine-tuning via confidence-based gradient scaling, achieving prior-data-free conservative adaptation with sparse parameter updates.

Abstract: Unconstrained fine-tuning of flow-matching Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models drives dense parameter overwrites, degrading pre-trained capabilities. We present Conservative Supervised Fine-Tuning (ConSFT), an optimization objective that adapts to target distributions while mitigating catastrophic forgetting, requiring zero prior data or architectural overhead. By dynamically scaling learning signals based on model confidence, ConSFT suppresses excessive gradients from low-confidence samples to prevent disproportionate parameter updates, thereby bounding the intrinsic parameter disruption risk. Inspired by reinforcement learning's trust-region clipping, this formulation establishes a progressive learning dynamic to secure target convergence and prior capability retention, maintaining sparse parameter updates without relying on the parallel reference networks required by explicit regularization. We evaluate ConSFT on the LIBERO and RoboTwin benchmarks across state-of-the-art flow-matching VLAs ($π_0$, $π_{0.5}$, and GR00T-N1.6-3B). The method outperforms vanilla SFT in capability retention by an average absolute margin of over 20\%, matching the efficacy of data-heavy Experience Replay in a prior-data-free regime. Real-world robotic deployments confirm that ConSFT precludes spatial overfitting during downstream adaptation, preserving pre-trained physical skills while acquiring sequential target tasks.

</details>


### [21] [Raymoval: Raycasting-based Dynamic Object Removal for Static 3D Mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08937)
*Daebeom Kim,Seungjae Lee,Seoyeon Jang,Kevin Christiansen Marsim,Hyun Myung*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a raycasting-based module for dynamic object removal in static 3D mapping, using azimuth-elevation grid projection, raycast consistency testing, and spatial consistency validation to produce cleaner static maps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic objects leave residual traces and cause surface loss in static 3D mapping, reducing map consistency for long-term robot navigation.

Method: 1) Project each scan onto an azimuth-elevation grid; 2) Compare bin-wise minimum range with map's first-hit distance via raycasting; 3) Apply raycast consistency test to separate dynamic/static points; 4) Spatial consistency validation refines labels.

Result: Consistent static mapping results with lower residual dynamics and reduced over-removal, validated quantitatively and qualitatively on SemanticKITTI and a custom dataset.

Conclusion: The proposed raycasting-based dynamic removal module effectively improves static map consistency by reducing dynamic traces while avoiding over-removal of static structures.

Abstract: Static mapping is fundamental to robot navigation, providing a persistent geometric prior and a consistent reference for long-term autonomy. However, dynamic objects leave residual traces and cause surface loss, which reduces map consistency. We propose a raycasting-based module for dynamic object removal in static 3D mapping. Each scan is projected onto an azimuth-elevation grid, and for every viewing direction we compare the bin-wise minimum range with the map's first-hit distance computed by raycasting. Furthermore, we apply a raycast consistency test that separates dynamic from static points. Finally, a spatial consistency validation step refines labels, producing static maps with lower residual dynamics and reduced over-removal. We evaluate our approach quantitatively and qualitatively on SemanticKITTI and a challenging custom dataset, and show consistent static mapping results.

</details>


### [22] [A low-cost mockup to simulate robotic laser cutting in nuclear decommissioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08947)
*Frederico Fernandes Afonso Silva,Murilo Marques Marinho,Bruno Vilhena Adorno*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a low-cost experimental mockup for simulating laser cutting of containers in nuclear decommissioning. It uses a 3-axis table, a 6-DOF manipulator with an ultraviolet torch simulating the laser, and a camera-based visual system. A constrained task-space adaptive motion controller compensates for inaccurate parameters and enables reactive collision avoidance. By controlling the ultraviolet beam (4-DOF) instead of the full end-effector pose, tracking accuracy improves to 2.4 mm (sd 1.3) vs. 3.9 mm (sd 2.5).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a low-cost, uncalibrated experimental setup that simulates laser cutting for nuclear decommissioning, avoiding the high costs, hazards, and calibration burdens of real laser systems in radioactive environments.

Method: The system comprises a three-axis table with an ultraviolet-sensitive cuboid container, a six-DOF manipulator holding an ultraviolet torch, and a visual system using cameras and fiducial markers. A constrained task-space adaptive motion controller compensates for inaccurate parameters without calibration. The controller explicitly handles geometric constraints for reactive collision avoidance. The ultraviolet beam (4-DOF) is controlled instead of the full end-effector pose to improve trajectory tracking.

Result: Overall mean tracking accuracy of 3.9 mm (sd 2.5 mm) when controlling the end-effector pose, and 2.4 mm (sd 1.3 mm) when controlling the ultraviolet beam, all achieved without initial system calibration.

Conclusion: The proposed low-cost mockup with adaptive constrained control and beam-level control achieves accurate trajectory tracking for simulated laser cutting without requiring calibration, demonstrating feasibility for nuclear decommissioning applications.

Abstract: This paper introduces a low-cost experimental mockup to simulate the laser cutting process of containers in nuclear decommissioning. It is composed of a three-axis table supporting a cuboid container with ultraviolet-sensitive faces, a six-degree-of-freedom serial manipulator holding an ultraviolet torch that simulates the laser, and a visual system based on cameras and fiducial markers. The system employs a constrained task-space adaptive motion controller that compensates for inaccurate parameters and eliminates the need to calibrate the system. Furthermore, as the motion controller explicitly accounts for geometric constraints, the robot reactively avoids collisions with obstacles while handling the ultraviolet torch. To enhance tracking of the laser-cutting path, we control the ultraviolet beam, which requires only four degrees of freedom, instead of the full end-effector pose. Experiments show that, despite an initially uncalibrated system, the overall system is capable of tracking different trajectories with an overall mean accuracy of 3.9 (sd 2.5) mm when the end-effector pose is controlled and 2.4 (sd 1.3) mm when the ultraviolet beam is controlled.

</details>


### [23] [Towards Backdoor-Based Ownership Verification for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09005)
*Ming Sun,Rui Wang,Xingrui Yu,Lihua Jing,Hangyu Du,Zhenglin Wan,Xu Pan,Ivor Tsang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GuardVLA is the first backdoor-based ownership verification framework for Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs), embedding stealthy watermarks via secret messages in embodied visual data and using a swap-and-detect mechanism for post-release verification, preserving task performance while enabling reliable ownership verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As trained VLAs are increasingly shared and adapted, protecting model ownership becomes essential for secure deployment and responsible open-source usage.

Method: GuardVLA embeds a stealthy and harmless backdoor watermark during training by injecting secret messages into embodied visual data, and uses a swap-and-detect mechanism with a trigger projector and external classifier head to activate and detect the backdoor based on prediction probabilities.

Result: Extensive experiments across multiple datasets, model architectures, and adaptation settings demonstrate reliable ownership verification while preserving benign task performance; the embedded watermark remains detectable under post-release model adaptation.

Conclusion: GuardVLA enables effective ownership verification for VLAs while maintaining benign task performance and robustness to post-release adaptation.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) support generalist robotic control by enabling end-to-end decision policies directly from multi-modal inputs. As trained VLAs are increasingly shared and adapted, protecting model ownership becomes essential for secure deployment and responsible open-source usage. In this paper, we present GuardVLA, the first backdoor-based ownership verification framework specifically designed for VLAs. GuardVLA embeds a stealthy and harmless backdoor watermark into the protected model during training by injecting secret messages into embodied visual data. For post-release verification, we propose a swap-and-detect mechanism, in which the trigger projector and an external classifier head are used to activate and detect the embedded backdoor based on prediction probabilities. Extensive experiments across multiple datasets, model architectures, and adaptation settings demonstrate that GuardVLA enables reliable ownership verification while preserving benign task performance. Further results show that the embedded watermark remains detectable under post-release model adaptation.

</details>


### [24] [Terminal Matters: Kinodynamic Planning with a Terminal Cost and Learned Uncertainty in Belief State-Cost Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09046)
*Zhuoyun Zhong,Seyedali Golestaneh,Constantinos Chamzas*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: KiTe is a kinodynamic planner that introduces a terminal-cost formulation to optimize goal-reaching quality alongside trajectory cost, preserves asymptotic optimality (proven for AO-RRT), extends to belief-space planning by minimizing Wasserstein distance to improve goal-reaching probability, and supports learning-based uncertainty models, achieving improved success under uncertainty in Flappy Bird, Car Parking, and Planar Pushing tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sampling-based kinodynamic planners optimize accumulated trajectory costs and treat goal reaching as a feasibility check, failing to explicitly optimize terminal-state quality such as goal preference or goal-reaching reliability under uncertainty.

Method: The paper introduces a terminal-cost formulation for kinodynamic planning that optimizes terminal-state quality alongside accumulated trajectory cost; proves AO-RRT preserves asymptotic optimality under this objective; extends the formulation to belief space and proves minimizing Wasserstein distance between terminal belief and goal improves a lower bound on goal-reaching probability; integrates learned dynamics and process uncertainty from data for systems without analytical models.

Result: Experiments on Flappy Bird, Car Parking, and Planar Pushing show consistent improvement in goal-reaching success under uncertainty; real-world Planar Pushing experiments demonstrate effective planning with learned dynamics and uncertainty.

Conclusion: KiTe demonstrates that explicitly optimizing terminal-state quality via a terminal-cost formulation improves goal-reaching reliability under uncertainty, and the approach works with both analytical and learned uncertainty models.

Abstract: In many real-world robotic tasks, robots must generate dynamically feasible motions that reliably reach desired goals even under uncertainty. Yet existing sampling-based kinodynamic planners typically optimize accumulated trajectory costs and treat goal reaching as a feasibility check, rather than explicitly optimizing terminal-state quality, such as goal preference or goal-reaching reliability. In this work, we introduce a terminal-cost formulation for kinodynamic planning that allows terminal-state quality to be optimized alongside accumulated trajectory cost. We prove that AO-RRT, an asymptotically optimal kinodynamic planner, preserves its asymptotic optimality under this augmented objective. We further extend the formulation to belief space and prove that minimizing the Wasserstein distance between the terminal belief and the goal improves a lower bound on the probability of reaching the goal region. The resulting planner, KiTe, uses this terminal-cost objective to encode goal preferences and improve reliability under uncertainty. To support systems without analytical uncertainty models, we learn dynamics and process uncertainty directly from data and integrate the learned belief dynamics into planning. Experiments on Flappy Bird, Car Parking, and Planar Pushing show that KiTe consistently improves goal-reaching success under uncertainty. Real-world Planar Pushing experiments further demonstrate that KiTe can plan effectively with learned dynamics and uncertainty. Source code is available at https://github.com/elpis-lab/KiTe.

</details>


### [25] [Automated Robotic Moisture Monitoring in Agricultural Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09050)
*Senthil Palanisamy,Akila I. S*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A robotic system integrated with moisture sensors and aerial image processing uses Dijkstra's algorithm for path planning to automate soil moisture monitoring in large-scale agricultural fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual monitoring of moisture in large plantations is tedious; the goal is to create an efficient and economical automated moisture monitoring system using a robot and sensor circuits.

Method: A large field is divided into grids with moisture sensors; when a sensor detects dry soil, Dijkstra's shortest path algorithm computes the robot's path using an aerial image; the robot then calculates moisture content via image processing.

Result: A prototype was developed and tested using a small study field with a mounted camera, demonstrating a working automated moisture monitoring system.

Conclusion: The work successfully developed a prototype for an automated, robotic system that monitors agricultural field moisture efficiently by combining sensor networks, path planning, and image processing.

Abstract: Monitoring moisture level of land in a large-scale plantation is tedious. The main objective of this project is to use a robotic kit in collaboration with the on-field moisture sensor circuits, thereby creating an efficient and economical moisture monitoring system. A large agriculture field is divided into smaller grids. Each grid is placed with a moisture sensor. Whenever a sensor reports the soil to be dry, the robot goes to the concerned field for inspection. The path to the concerned field is found by applying Dijkstra's shortest path algorithm on the aerial image of the field. Then the total moisture content of the field is calculated by the robot using suitable image processing algorithms and reported accordingly. For developing and testing this work, a small study field was set up above which a camera was mounted at an appropriate height to capture its aerial view. Thus a prototype for an automated system of monitoring agricultural fields' moisture has been developed through this work.

</details>


### [26] [Octopus Protocol: One-Shot Hardware Discovery and Control for AI Agents via Infrastructure-as-Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09055)
*Quilee Simeon,Justin M. Wei,Yile Fan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Octopus Protocol collapses the cost of bringing up new robotic hardware to a single shell command by using a coding agent that automatically discovers devices, infers capabilities, generates MCP servers with typed tools, and deploys them as live HTTP endpoints, validated on heterogeneous platforms including a 6-DOF robotic arm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic-robotics systems (Code-as-Policies, VLA foundation models) assume that drivers, SDKs, or ROS-style primitives already exist for target hardware. Writing these primitives is the dominant engineering cost, creating a bottleneck for bringing up new hardware for agent control.

Method: A five-stage pipeline (PROBE → IDENTIFY → INTERFACE → SERVE → DEPLOY) executed by a coding agent with raw OS access and an LLM API key. The agent discovers connected devices, infers capabilities via LLM, generates a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server with typed tools, and deploys it as a live HTTP endpoint. A persistent daemon monitors the system, heals broken code, and perceives physical state through self-generated camera tools. Two core principles: protocols are prompts (not code), and the coding agent is the runtime.

Result: Validated on PC/WSL, Apple Silicon macOS, and Raspberry Pi 4, plus a commercial 6-DOF robotic arm with USB camera feedback. A single command onboards hardware in ~10-15 minutes and exposes up to 30 MCP tools. An MCP-compliant client performs closed-loop visual-motor control through tools that no human wrote.

Conclusion: Octopus Protocol eliminates the manual engineering overhead of hardware bring-up by treating protocol generation as an LLM-driven pipeline, enabling zero-human-write agent control across heterogeneous robotic platforms.

Abstract: Recent agentic-robotics systems, from Code-asPolicies to modern vision-language-action (VLA) foundation models, presuppose that drivers, SDKs, or ROS-style primitives for the target hardware already exist. Writing those primitives is the dominant engineering cost of bringing up new hardware for agent control. We present Octopus Protocol, a system that collapses that cost to a single shell command. Given only raw OS access and a language-model API key, a coding agent executes a five-stage pipeline--PROBE, IDENTIFY, INTERFACE, SERVE, DEPLOY--to discover connected devices, infer their capabilities, generate a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server with typed tools, and deploy it as a live HTTP endpoint. A persistent daemon then monitors the system, heals broken code, and perceives physical state through the camera tools it generated for itself. Two architectural principles make this work: protocols are prompts, not code, and the coding agent is the runtime. We validate the system on three heterogeneous platforms (PC/WSL, Apple Silicon macOS, Raspberry Pi 4) and on a commercial 6-DOF robotic arm with USB camera feedback. One command onboards the hardware in ~10-15 minutes and exposes up to 30 MCP tools; an MCP-compliant client then performs closed-loop visual-motor control through tools no human wrote.

</details>


### [27] [Smoothing Out the Edges: Continuous-Time Estimation with Gaussian Process Motion Priors on Factor Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09073)
*Connor Holmes,Sven Lilge,Zi Cong Guo,Frank Dellaert,Timothy D. Barfoot*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper simplifies Gaussian process continuous-time state estimation using factor graphs, providing examples in GTSAM to increase adoption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonparametric GP continuous-time estimation has seen less adoption than parametric methods despite technical advantages, due to complexity barriers.

Method: The authors reinterpret GP continuous-time estimation through factor graph language, which is already the de facto paradigm in robotics, and provide three working examples in the GTSAM framework.

Result: A simplified explanation and practical implementation examples that lower the barrier to adopting GP-based continuous-time estimation.

Conclusion: GP continuous-time estimation can be made more accessible by framing it within the factor graph paradigm, encouraging broader adoption in robotics.

Abstract: Continuous-time state estimation is gaining in popularity due to its abilities to provide smooth solutions, handle asynchronous sensors, and interpolate between data points. While there are two main paradigms, parametric (e.g., temporal basis functions, splines) and nonparametric (Gaussian processes), the latter has seen less adoption despite its technical advantages and relative ease of implementation. In this article, we seek to rectify this situation by providing a new simplified explanation of GP continuous-time estimation rooted in the language of factor graphs, which have become the de facto estimation paradigm in much of robotics. To simplify onboarding, we also provide three working examples implemented in the popular GTSAM estimation framework.

</details>


### [28] [HyDRA Scorpion: A Cost-effective and Modular ROV for Real-Time Underwater Inspection, Intervention, and Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09093)
*Anika Tabassum Orchi,Md Farhan Zaman,Md Darain Khan,Md Alamgir,Mahbubul Islam,Md. Jobayer Rahman,A. M. Zayed Abdullah,Md Mehrab Hossain Khan,Md. Kutub Al Baki,Iftekharul Islam,Shakil Ahmed,Md Sadique Hossain,Md Muzahidul Islam,Shah Mohammad Seaman,Nusrat Jahan Piyal,Shekh Md. Saifur Rahman,Fahim Hafiz,A. K. M. Muzahidul Islam,M. Rezwan Khan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HyDRA Scorpion is a low-cost, locally manufacturable ROV with AI-driven perception, 4-DoF maneuverability, dual manipulators, and validated performance including leak-free operation at 4 bar, stable station-keeping within ±0.15 m, 0.89 mAP underwater object detection, and 360° tangle-free manipulator rotation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High cost of commercial ROVs and the gap between mechanically capable platforms and those with integrated intelligence create a significant access barrier for underwater science.

Method: Integrates an advanced AI perception stack with in-situ measurement capabilities onto a low-cost, locally manufacturable platform featuring 4-DoF maneuverability, dual manipulators, and custom pressure-tested housing.

Result: Leak-free pressure testing at 4 bar (equivalent to ~304.8 m depth); station-keeping within ±0.15 m; underwater object detection mAP of 0.89 with real-time inference; 4-DoF manipulator with 360° tangle-free rotation.
