To incorporate this prior into action generation, we introduce confidence-adaptive prior guidance, which injects the elite prior into an intermediate state of the flow-matching action sampler and adjusts the guidance strength based on retrieval confidence. This design allows the frozen VLA to exploit environment-specific successful experience while preserving observation-conditioned generative refinement. This retrieve-then-steer mechanism enables lightweight, non-parametric test-time adaptation without requiring parameter updates. Simulation and real-world experiments show improved task success and closed-loop stability, especially in long-horizon and multi-stage tasks.

</details>


### [53] [Plan in Sandbox, Navigate in Open Worlds: Learning Physics-Grounded Abstracted Experience for Embodied Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10118)
*Zhixuan Shen,Jiawei Du,Ziyu Guo,Han Luo,Lilan Peng,Joey Tianyi Zhou,Haonan Luo,Tianrui Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SAGE is a framework that enables embodied navigation agents to learn in physics-grounded semantic abstractions (rather than photorealistic simulations) via three phases: Genesis (building semantic environments), Evolution (RL with asymmetric adaptive clipping), and Navigation (transferring to open-world control), achieving 53.21% LLM-Match Success Rate on A-EQA (+9.7% over baseline) with promising real-robot transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs show strong reasoning but struggle in embodied navigation due to scarcity of aligned open-world vision and robot control data; simulators are cost-effective but photorealistic simulations limit policy transferability.

Method: SAGE operates via three phases: (1) Genesis — constructing diverse, physics-constrained semantic environments; (2) Evolution — distilling experiences via RL with a novel asymmetric adaptive clipping mechanism for stable updates; (3) Navigation — bridging the learned abstract policy to open-world control.

Result: 53.21% LLM-Match Success Rate on A-EQA (+9.7% over baseline), with encouraging transfer to physical indoor robot deployment.

Conclusion: Learning in physics-grounded semantic abstractions, mimicking human mental simulation, significantly improves planner-assisted embodied navigation and can transfer to real-world robot deployment.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have demonstrated exceptional general reasoning capabilities. However, their performance in embodied navigation remains hindered by a scarcity of aligned open-world vision and robot control data. Despite simulators providing a cost-effective alternative for data collection, the inherent reliance on photorealistic simulations often limits the transferability of learned policies. To this end, we propose \textit{\textbf{S}andbox-\textbf{A}bstracted \textbf{G}rounded \textbf{E}xperience} (\textbf{\textit{SAGE}}), a framework that enables agents to learn within a physics-grounded semantic abstraction rather than a photorealistic simulation, mimicking the human capacity for mental simulation where plans are rehearsed in simplified physics abstractions before execution. \textit{SAGE} system operates via three synergistic phases: (1) \textit{Genesis}: constructing diverse, physics-constrained semantic environments to bootstrap experience; (2) \textit{Evolution}: distilling experiences through Reinforcement Learning (RL), utilizing a novel asymmetric adaptive clipping mechanism to stabilize updates; (3) \textit{Navigation}: bridging the abstract policy to open-world control. We demonstrate that \textit{SAGE} significantly improves planner-assisted embodied navigation, achieving a 53.21\% LLM-Match Success Rate on A-EQA (+9.7\% over baseline), while showing encouraging transfer to physical indoor robot deployment.

</details>


### [54] [Data-Asymmetric Latent Imagination and Reranking for 3D Robotic Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10166)
*Lianghao Luo,Xizhou Bu,Ruyan Liu,Qingqiu Huang,Chufeng Tang,Xiaoshuai Hao,Hongbo Wang,Wei Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DALI-R is a framework for 3D robotic imitation learning that leverages suboptimal, exploratory, and failed demonstrations by learning a Latent World Model over 3D point clouds for imagined rollouts and a Task Completion Scorer for reranking action chunks, achieving 6.8% average success rate improvement with less than 0.7x additional inference overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic imitation learning typically assumes optimal demonstrations, but real-world data collection often yields suboptimal/exploratory/failed trajectories. Discarding such data wastes valuable information about environment dynamics and failure modes.

Method: DALI-R (Data-Asymmetric Latent Imagination and Reranking) learns a Latent World Model over 3D point clouds for imagined rollouts and a Task Completion Scorer that reranks candidate action chunks, instantiated with both diffusion and efficient flow-matching policies.

Result: 6.8% average improvement in success rate across Adroit and MetaWorld benchmarks with less than 0.7x additional inference overhead compared to two evaluated 3D base policies.

Conclusion: Mixed-quality trajectory data can be effectively leveraged to improve 3D robotic imitation learning without requiring additional high-quality demonstrations, through latent imagination and reranking mechanisms.

Abstract: Robotic imitation learning typically assumes access to optimal demonstrations, yet real-world data collection often yields suboptimal, exploratory, or even failed trajectories. Discarding such data wastes valuable information about environment dynamics and failure modes, which can instead be leveraged to improve decision-making. While 3D policies reduce reliance on high-quality demonstrations through strong spatial generalization, they still require large-scale data to achieve high task success. To address this, we propose DALI-R, a Data-Asymmetric Latent Imagination and Reranking framework for 3D robotic imitation learning from mixed-quality trajectories. It learns a Latent World Model over 3D point clouds for imagined rollouts and a Task Completion Scorer that reranks candidate action chunks, improving decision-making without additional high-quality demonstrations. We instantiate DALI-R with both diffusion and efficient flow-matching policies and evaluate it on Adroit and MetaWorld benchmarks. Across the two evaluated 3D base policies, DALI-R achieves an average $6.8$\% improvement in success rate while incurring less than $0.7\times$ additional inference overhead.

</details>


### [55] [HeteroGenManip: Generalizable Manipulation For Heterogeneous Object Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10201)
*Zhenhao Shen,Zeming Yang,Yue Chen,Yuran Wang,Shengqiang Xu,Mingleyang Li,Hao Dong,Ruihai Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HeteroGenManip is a two-stage framework for generalizable robotic manipulation that decouples grasp localization from interaction execution. It uses Foundation-Correspondence-Guided Grasp for contact point localization and Multi-Foundation-Model Diffusion Policy (MFMDP) for category-specialized trajectory planning, achieving 31% improvement in simulation and 36.7% in real-world tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing foundation-model-based approaches use end-to-end learning that blurs the distinction between contact point localization and interaction trajectory planning, causing error accumulation in long-horizon tasks. They also rely on a single uniform model that fails to capture category-specific features for heterogeneous objects.

Method: The framework has two stages: (1) Foundation-Correspondence-Guided Grasp uses structural priors to align initial contact state and reduce grasp pose uncertainty; (2) Multi-Foundation-Model Diffusion Policy (MFMDP) routes objects to category-specialized foundation models and integrates geometric information with part features via dual-stream cross-attention.

Result: Achieves robust intra-category shape and pose generalization. Average 31% performance improvement in simulation tasks with broad type setting, and 36.7% gain across four real-world tasks with different interaction types.

Conclusion: Decoupling grasp localization from interaction execution and using category-specialized models significantly improves manipulation generalization across heterogeneous objects and interaction types.

Abstract: Generalizable manipulation involving cross-type object interactions is a critical yet challenging capability in robotics. To reliably accomplish such tasks, robots must address two fundamental challenges: ``where to manipulate'' (contact point localization) and ``how to manipulate'' (subsequent interaction trajectory planning). Existing foundation-model-based approaches often adopt end-to-end learning that obscures the distinction between these stages, exacerbating error accumulation in long-horizon tasks. Furthermore, they typically rely on a single uniform model, which fails to capture the diverse, category-specific features required for heterogeneous objects. To overcome these limitations, we propose HeteroGenManip, a task-conditioned, two-stage framework designed to decouple initial grasp from complex interaction execution. First, Foundation-Correspondence-Guided Grasp module leverages structural priors to align the initial contact state, thereby significantly reducing the pose uncertainty of grasping. Subsequently, Multi-Foundation-Model Diffusion Policy (MFMDP) routes objects to category-specialized foundation models, integrating fine-grained geometric information with highly-variable part features via a dual-stream cross-attention mechanism. Experimental evaluations demonstrate that HeteroGenManip achieves robust intra-category shape and pose generalization. The framework achieves an average 31\% performance improvement in simulation tasks with broad type setting, alongside a 36.7\% gain across four real-world tasks with different interaction types.

</details>


### [56] [Nano-U: Efficient Terrain Segmentation for Tiny Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10210)
*Federico Pizzolato,Francesco Pasti,Nicola Bellotto*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents Nano-U, a compact binary terrain segmentation network (~few thousand parameters) for microcontrollers, trained via Quantization-Aware Distillation (QAD), and deployed on ESP32-S3 using a compiler-based inference engine (MicroFlow in Rust), achieving efficient terrain segmentation for small robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: State-of-the-art terrain segmentation models are incompatible with memory and compute constraints of microcontrollers, limiting scalable deployment on small robotic platforms.

Method: Nano-U (highly compact binary segmentation network) trained via Quantization-Aware Distillation (QAD) combining knowledge distillation and quantization-aware training; deployed on ESP32-S3 via MicroFlow, a compiler-based Rust inference engine eliminating interpreter overhead and dynamic memory allocation.

Result: Excellent results on Botanic Garden dataset; very good performance on TinyAgri (custom agricultural field dataset); minimal memory footprint and low latency on ESP32-S3.

Conclusion: Compiler-based execution of quantized Nano-U demonstrates a viable and energy-efficient solution for perception on low-cost robotic platforms.

Abstract: Terrain segmentation is a fundamental capability for autonomous mobile robots operating in unstructured outdoor environments. However, state-of-the-art models are incompatible with the memory and compute constraints typical of microcontrollers, limiting scalable deployment in small robotics platforms. To address this gap, we develop a complete framework for robust binary terrain segmentation on a low-cost microcontroller. At the core of our approach we design Nano-U, a highly compact binary segmentation network with a few thousand parameters. To compensate for the network's minimal capacity, we train Nano-U via Quantization-Aware Distillation (QAD), combining knowledge distillation and quantization-aware training. This allows the final quantized model to achieve excellent results on the Botanic Garden dataset and to perform very well on TinyAgri, a custom agricultural field dataset with more challenging scenes. We deploy the quantized Nano-U on a commodity microcontroller by extending MicroFlow, a compiler-based inference engine for TinyML implemented in Rust. By eliminating interpreter overhead and dynamic memory allocation, the quantized model executes on an ESP32-S3 with a minimal memory footprint and low latency. This compiler-based execution demonstrates a viable and energy-efficient solution for perception on low-cost robotic platforms.

</details>


### [57] [Learning Point Cloud Geometry as a Statistical Manifold: Theory and Practice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10456)
*Jinwoo Lee,Jiwoo Kim,Woojae Shin,Giseop Kim,Hyondong Oh*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: POLI is a self-supervised deep neural estimator that models point cloud geometry as statistical manifolds of Gaussian distributions, predicting per-point ellipsoidal geometry to improve robotic perception tasks without requiring labeled data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LiDAR point clouds are sparse and non-uniform, causing unreliable geometric reasoning and degraded downstream perception performance. Existing methods either rely on hand-crafted statistics or require large labeled datasets, both of which have scalability limitations.

Method: Explicitly models local point cloud geometry as a statistical manifold induced by a family of Gaussian distributions. Introduces Point-to-Ellipsoid (POLI), a deep neural estimator that predicts per-point Gaussian geometry in a self-supervised manner, preserving strong geometric inductive biases without needing labeled data.

Result: POLI achieves accurate and robust geometry estimation and consistently improves performance across diverse robotic perception tasks, integrating seamlessly into existing pipelines without architectural changes.

Conclusion: Modeling point cloud geometry via statistical manifolds with self-supervised learning effectively addresses sparsity and non-uniformity issues, offering a scalable and label-free solution that enhances downstream robotic perception.

Abstract: Point clouds are a fundamental representation for robotic perception tasks such as localization, mapping, and object pose estimation. However, LiDAR-acquired point clouds are inherently sparse and non-uniform, providing incomplete observations of the underlying scene geometry. This makes reliable geometric reasoning challenging and degrades downstream perception performance. Existing approaches attempt to compensate for these limitations by estimating local geometry, but often rely on hand-crafted statistics or end-to-end supervised learning, which can suffer from limited scalability or require large amounts of accurately labeled data. To address these challenges, we explicitly model point cloud geometry under a principled mathematical formulation. We represent local geometry as a statistical manifold induced by a family of Gaussian distributions, where each point is associated with a Gaussian capturing its local geometric structure. Based on this formulation, we introduce Point-to-Ellipsoid (POLI), a deep neural estimator that predicts per-point Gaussian geometry. POLI learns a mapping from point cloud observations to their underlying geometry in a self-supervised manner, removing the need for labeled data while preserving strong geometric inductive biases. The resulting representation integrates seamlessly into existing robotic perception pipelines without architectural modifications. Extensive experiments show that POLI enables accurate and robust geometry estimation and consistently improves performance across diverse robotic perception tasks.

</details>


### [58] [VEGA: Visual Encoder Grounding Alignment for Spatially-Aware Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10485)
*Hao Wang,Xiaobao Wei,Jingyang He,Chengyu Bai,Chun-Kai Fan,Jiajun Cao,Jintao Chen,Ying Li,Shanyu Rong,Ming Lu,Xiaozhu Ju,Jian Tang,Shanghang Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VEGA aligns VLA visual encoder outputs with 3D-aware DINOv2-FiT3D features via a lightweight projector, improving spatial reasoning for robotic manipulation without inference overhead, achieving SOTA among implicit spatial grounding methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual backbones of current VLA models are pretrained on 2D images lacking explicit 3D supervision, leading to poor spatial awareness. Existing implicit grounding methods align features at the LLM level where spatial structure is entangled with linguistic semantics, limiting generalizability and interpretability.

Method: VEGA aligns VLA visual encoder output with spatially-aware features from DINOv2-FiT3D (DINOv2 fine-tuned with multi-view consistent 3D Gaussian Splatting) using a lightweight projector trained with cosine similarity loss alongside standard action prediction. The projector is discarded at inference time, adding no overhead.

Result: VEGA consistently outperforms existing implicit spatial grounding baselines on both simulation benchmarks and real-world manipulation tasks, establishing a new state-of-the-art among implicit spatial grounding methods for VLA models.

Conclusion: Performing spatial alignment at the visual encoder output level before linguistic entanglement yields more interpretable and generalizable spatial awareness for VLA models, with no additional inference cost.

Abstract: Precise spatial reasoning is fundamental to robotic manipulation, yet the visual backbones of current vision-language-action (VLA) models are predominantly pretrained on 2D image data without explicit 3D geometric supervision, resulting in representations that lack accurate spatial awareness. Existing implicit spatial grounding methods partially address this by aligning VLA features with those of 3D-aware foundation models, but they rely on empirical layer search and perform alignment on LLM-level visual tokens where spatial structure has already been entangled with linguistic semantics, limiting both generalizability and geometric interpretability. We propose VEGA (Visual Encoder Grounding Alignment), a simple yet effective framework that directly aligns the output of the VLA's visual encoder with spatially-aware features from DINOv2-FiT3D, a DINOv2 model fine-tuned with multi-view consistent 3D Gaussian Splatting supervision. By performing alignment at the visual encoder output level, VEGA grounds spatial awareness before any linguistic entanglement occurs, offering a more interpretable and principled alignment target. The alignment is implemented via a lightweight projector trained with a cosine similarity loss alongside the standard action prediction objective, and is discarded at inference time, introducing no additional computational overhead. Extensive experiments on simulation benchmark and real-world manipulation tasks demonstrate that VEGA consistently outperforms existing implicit spatial grounding baselines, establishing a new state-of-the-art among implicit spatial grounding methods for VLA models.

</details>


### [59] [Embodied AI in Action: Insights from SAE World Congress 2026 on Safety, Trust, Robotics, and Real-World Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10653)
*Jan-Mou Li,Paul Schmitt,Wei Tong,Majed Mohammed,Akshay Chalana,Arpan Kusari,Edward Griffor*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This white paper summarizes a panel on embodied AI from the SAE World Congress 2026, emphasizing the need for systems-level engineering rigor, lifecycle governance, human-centered design, and evolving standards to ensure safe and trustworthy deployment of embodied AI in autonomous vehicles, robots, and industrial machines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As embodied AI systems move from research into real-world applications (autonomous vehicles, mobile robots, industrial machines), they introduce new challenges in safety, trust, governance, and operational reliability that need to be addressed.

Method: The paper synthesizes insights from a panel session at the SAE World Congress 2026, gathering experts from automotive, robotics, AI, and safety engineering to discuss embodied AI deployment challenges.

Result: The panel concluded that embodied AI must be treated as a systems challenge requiring engineering rigor, lifecycle governance, human-centered design, and evolving standards. Long-term success depends on both AI capability advances and safe/trustworthy deployment.

Conclusion: Responsible adoption of embodied AI requires practical perspectives for executives, policymakers, and technical leaders, with broad agreement that safe and trustworthy deployment is equally important as AI capability improvements.

Abstract: Embodied artificial intelligence is rapidly moving from research into real-world systems such as autonomous vehicles, mobile robots, and industrial machines. As these systems become more capable of perceiving, deciding, and acting in dynamic environments, they also introduce new challenges in safety, trust, governance, and operational reliability. This white paper summarizes key insights from the SAE World Congress 2026 panel session \textit{Embodied AI in Action}, which brought together experts from automotive, robotics, artificial intelligence, and safety engineering. The discussion highlighted the need to treat embodied AI as a systems challenge requiring engineering rigor, lifecycle governance, human-centered design, and evolving standards. The paper provides practical perspectives for executives, policymakers, and technical leaders seeking to adopt embodied AI responsibly. The panel reached broad agreement that long-term success will depend not only on advances in AI capability, but equally on safe and trustworthy deployment.

</details>


### [60] [VRA: Grounding Discrete-Time Joint Acceleration in Voltage-Constrained Actuation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10696)
*Lingwei Zhang,Jiaming Wang,Tianlin Zhang,Zhitao Song,Xuanqi Zeng,Weipeng Xia,Zhongyu Li,Yun-hui Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes Voltage-Realizable Acceleration (VRA), a joint-level acceleration interface that ensures commanded accelerations are physically realizable under voltage-constrained electric actuators, improving execution consistency and reducing oscillations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete-time joint acceleration constraints often produce kinematically admissible but physically unrealizable accelerations under voltage-constrained electric actuators, revealing a missing abstraction between kinematic planning and physical execution.

Method: VRA grounds kinematic acceleration commands in voltage-constrained actuator physics by restricting commanded accelerations to those that are voltage-realizable, creating a joint-level acceleration interface.

Result: Hardware experiments on electric actuators and a wheel-legged quadruped demonstrate that VRA removes unrealizable accelerations, restores consistent near-constraint execution, and reduces constraint-induced oscillations.

Conclusion: VRA effectively bridges the gap between kinematic acceleration constraints and physical actuator limitations, improving real-world performance in voltage-constrained systems.

Abstract: Discrete-time joint acceleration constraints are widely used to enforce position and velocity limits. However, under voltage-constrained electric actuators, kinematically admissible accelerations may be physically unrealizable, exposing a missing execution-level abstraction. We propose Voltage-Realizable Acceleration (VRA), a joint-level acceleration interface that grounds kinematic acceleration in voltage-constrained actuator physics by restricting commanded accelerations to voltage-realizable constraints. Hardware experiments on electric actuators and a wheel-legged quadruped show that VRA removes unrealizable accelerations, restores consistent near-constraint execution, and reduces constraint-induced oscillations.

</details>


### [61] [ObjView-Bench: Rethinking Difficulty and Deployment for Object-Centric View Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10707)
*Sicong Pan,Hao Hu,Xuying Huang,Benno Wingender,Maren Bennewitz*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ObjView-Bench is a new evaluation framework for object-centric view planning that disentangles object complexity, planning difficulty, and deployment constraints (budget, reachability). It shows that method rankings and failure modes change significantly when these factors are controlled, challenging conclusions from idealized evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of object-centric view planning conflate object complexity, planning difficulty, budget assumptions, and physical reachability constraints, making idealized evaluations unreliable for predicting real-world reconstruction performance.

Method: ObjView-Bench disentangles three key quantities: (1) omnidirectional self-occlusion (object-side), (2) observation saturation difficulty, and (3) protocol-dependent planning difficulty via a set-cover formulation. It also introduces deployment-oriented protocols with varying budget regimes and reachable-view constraints.

Result: Difficulty, budget, and reachability constraints substantially change method rankings and failure modes across classical, learned, and hybrid planners. Planning difficulty-aware sampling improves learned view planners.

Conclusion: Controlled evaluation of object complexity, planning difficulty, and deployment constraints is essential for reliable comparison of view planning methods; idealized evaluations may not generalize to realistic settings.

Abstract: Object-centric view planning is a core component of active geometric 3D reconstruction in robotics, yet existing evaluations often conflate object complexity, planning difficulty, budget assumptions, and physical reachability constraints. As a result, conclusions drawn from idealized view-planning evaluations may not reliably predict performance under realistic reconstruction settings. We introduce ObjView-Bench, an evaluation framework for rethinking difficulty and deployment in object-centric view planning. First, we disentangle three quantities underlying view-planning evaluation: omnidirectional self-occlusion as an object-side attribute, observation saturation difficulty, and protocol-dependent planning difficulty defined through a set-cover formulation. This separation supports controlled dataset construction, analysis of slow-saturation objects, and a case study showing that planning difficulty-aware sampling can improve learned view planners. Second, we design deployment-oriented evaluation protocols that reveal how budget regimes and reachable-view constraints alter method behavior. Across classical, learned, and hybrid planners, ObjView-Bench shows that difficulty, budget, and reachability constraints substantially change method rankings and failure modes.

</details>


### [62] [MAGS-SLAM: Monocular Multi-Agent Gaussian Splatting SLAM for Geometrically and Photometrically Consistent Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10760)
*Zhihao Cao,Qi Shao,Shuhao Zhai,Jing Zhang,Anh Nguyen,Baoru Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MAGS-SLAM is the first RGB-only multi-agent 3D Gaussian Splatting SLAM framework for collaborative scene reconstruction, achieving competitive tracking and rendering quality compared to RGB-D methods by using compact submap communication, loop verification, and occupancy-aware Gaussian fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent Gaussian SLAM methods rely on RGB-D sensors for metric depth and cross-agent alignment, limiting deployment on lightweight, low-cost, or power-constrained robotic platforms.

Method: Each agent builds local monocular Gaussian submaps independently, transmits compact submap summaries, and integrates geometry- and appearance-aware loop verification with occupancy-aware Gaussian fusion for global reconstruction.

Result: Competitive tracking accuracy and comparable/superior rendering quality to state-of-the-art RGB-D collaborative Gaussian SLAM methods, validated on synthetic and real-world datasets.

Conclusion: MAGS-SLAM demonstrates that RGB-only multi-agent 3DGS SLAM is feasible and effective, removing the dependency on active depth sensors for collaborative photorealistic 3D reconstruction.

Abstract: Collaborative photorealistic 3D reconstruction from multiple agents enables rapid large-scale scene capture for virtual production and cooperative multi-robot exploration. While recent 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) SLAM algorithms can generate high-fidelity real-time mapping, most of the existing multi-agent Gaussian SLAM methods still rely on RGB-D sensors to obtain metric depth and simplify cross-agent alignment, which limits the deployment on lightweight, low-cost, or power-constrained robotic platforms. To address this challenge, we propose MAGS-SLAM, the first RGB-only multi-agent 3DGS SLAM framework for collaborative scene reconstruction. Each agent independently builds local monocular Gaussian submaps and transmits compact submap summaries rather than raw observations or dense maps. To facilitate robust collaboration in the presence of monocular scale ambiguity, our framework integrates compact submap communication, geometry- and appearance-aware loop verification, and occupancy-aware Gaussian fusion, enabling coherent global reconstruction without active depth sensors. We further introduce ReplicaMultiagent Plus benchmark for evaluating collaborative Gaussian SLAM. Intensive experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets show that MAGS-SLAM achieves competitive tracking accuracy and comparable or superior rendering quality to state-of-the-art RGB-D collaborative Gaussian SLAM methods while relying only RGB images.

</details>


### [63] [ALAM: Algebraically Consistent Latent Transitions for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10819)
*Zuojin Tang,Haoyun Liu,Xinyuan Chang,Changjie Wu,Dongjie Huo,Yandan Yang,Bin Liu,Zhejia Cai,Feng Xiong,Mu Xu,jiachen Luo,De Ma,Zhiheng Ma,Gang Pan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ALAM introduces algebraic consistency constraints (composition and reversal) on latent action spaces learned from action-free video, then uses these structured latent transitions as auxiliary targets in joint flow-matching for VLA policy learning, achieving dramatic improvements on MetaWorld (47.9%→85.0%), LIBERO (94.1%→98.1%), and real-world tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models lack sufficient action-labeled robot data, while action-free videos are abundant but hard to leverage. Existing latent action models trained via reconstruction produce codes that predict observations well but lack the structured geometry needed for coherent policy generation alongside robot actions.

Method: ALAM learns latent transitions from action-free video triplets with reconstruction loss plus algebraic regularization (composition consistency and reversal consistency), enforcing a locally additive transition space. The pretrained encoder is frozen and its latent transition sequences serve as auxiliary generative targets in a joint flow-matching objective alongside robot actions.

Result: ALAM reduces additivity/reversibility errors by 25-85× over baselines, improves long-horizon cumulative reconstruction, and boosts average success rate from 47.9% to 85.0% on MetaWorld MT50 and from 94.1% to 98.1% on LIBERO, with consistent real-world gains.

Conclusion: Algebraically structured latent transitions combined with joint flow matching provide a highly effective way to transfer video-based priors into VLA policies, overcoming the scarcity of action-labeled data without requiring explicit latent-to-action decoding.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models remain constrained by the scarcity of action-labeled robot data, whereas action-free videos provide abundant evidence of how the physical world changes. Latent action models offer a promising way to extract such priors from videos, but reconstruction-trained latent codes are not necessarily suitable for policy generation: they may predict future observations while lacking the structure needed to be reused or generated coherently with robot actions. We introduce ALAM (Algebraic Latent Action Model), an Algebraically Consistent Latent Action Model that turns temporal relations in action-free video into structural supervision. Given frame triplets, ALAM learns latent transitions that are grounded by reconstruction while being regularized by composition and reversal consistency, encouraging a locally additive transition space. For downstream VLA learning, we freeze the pretrained encoder and use its latent transition sequences as auxiliary generative targets, co-generated with robot actions under a joint flow-matching objective. This couples structured latent transitions with flow-based policy generation, allowing the policy to exploit ALAM's locally consistent transition geometry without requiring latent-to-action decoding. Representation probes show that ALAM reduces additivity and reversibility errors by 25-85 times over unstructured latent-action baselines and improves long-horizon cumulative reconstruction. When transferred to VLA policies, ALAM raises the average success rate from 47.9% to 85.0% on MetaWorld MT50 and from 94.1% to 98.1% on LIBERO, with consistent gains on real-world manipulation tasks. Ablations further confirm that the strongest improvements arise from the synergy between algebraically structured latent transitions and joint flow matching.

</details>


### [64] [Unified Noise Steering for Efficient Human-Guided VLA Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10821)
*Junjie Lu,Xinyao Qin,Yuhua Jiang,Kaixin Wang,Chuheng Zhang,Bin Liang,Jun Yang,Min Xu,Li Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: UniSteer combines human corrective guidance with noise-space RL by inverting the frozen flow-matching decoder to convert human action corrections into noise targets, enabling efficient adaptation of VLA models for robotic manipulation with limited real-world interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting diffusion-based VLA models to real-world distributions is challenging because on-robot RL is expensive and time-consuming, and existing noise-space RL suffers from inefficient autonomous exploration.

Method: UniSteer proposes approximate action-to-noise inversion: given a human corrective action, it inverts the frozen flow-matching decoder to recover a noise target, providing supervised guidance for the noise actor that is simultaneously optimized via reinforcement learning.

Result: Real-world experiments show UniSteer improves success rate from 20% to 90% in 66 minutes on average across four tasks, outperforming noise-space RL and action-space human-in-the-loop baselines.

Conclusion: UniSteer effectively combines human corrective feedback with noise-space RL through action-to-noise inversion, enabling efficient and practical adaptation of pretrained VLA models with limited real-world interactions.

Abstract: Diffusion-based vision-language-action (VLA) models have emerged as strong priors for robotic manipulation, yet adapting them to real-world distributions remains challenging. In particular, on-robot reinforcement learning (RL) is expensive and time-consuming, so effective adaptation depends on efficient policy improvement within a limited budget of real-world interactions. Noise-space RL lowers the cost by keeping the pretrained VLA fixed as a denoising generator while updating only a lightweight actor that predicts the noise. However, its performance is still limited due to inefficient autonomous exploration. Human corrective interventions can reduce this exploration burden, but they are naturally provided in action space, whereas noise-space finetuning requires supervision over noise variables. To address these challenges, we propose UniSteer, a Unified Noise Steering framework that combines human corrective guidance with noise-space RL through approximate action-to-noise inversion. Given a human corrective action, UniSteer inverts the frozen flow-matching decoder to recover a noise target, which provides supervised guidance for the same noise actor that is simultaneously optimized via reinforcement learning. Real-world experiments on diverse manipulation tasks show that UniSteer adapts more efficiently than strong noise-space RL and action-space human-in-the-loop baselines, improving the success rate from 20% to 90% in 66 minutes on average across four real-world adaptation tasks.

</details>


### [65] [Safe Aerial 3D Path Planning for Autonomous UAVs using Magnetic Potential Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10880)
*Haechan Mark Bong,Giovanni Beltrame*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 3DMaxConvNet extends a 2D potential-field planner to 3D for UAV navigation, using a convolutional autoencoder to predict obstacle-aware potential fields from LiDAR data. It achieves 100% path planning success in two urban environments without retraining, with runtime 1.7–1.95x faster than A* and ~193–201x faster than RRT*(3k).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safe autonomous UAV navigation in urban environments requires real-time path planning that avoids obstacles. Existing planners suffer from local minima or high computational cost. The authors aim to extend the 2D MaxConvNet magnetic field planner to 3D to address these limitations.

Method: 3DMaxConvNet extends the 2D MaxConvNet magnetic field planner to 3D by using a convolutional autoencoder to predict obstacle-aware potential fields from LiDAR-derived 101^3 voxel grids. Path planning is formulated using potential fields derived from Maxwell's equations to avoid local minima.

Result: 100% path planning success rate on two distinct Cosys-AirSim urban environments (dense night-time cityscape and suburban district) without retraining. Path lengths comparable to A* on unseen maps. Runtime: 0.087–0.089s vs A* 0.155–0.17s (1.7–1.95x faster) and vs RRT*(3k) 17.2–17.5s (~193–201x faster).

Conclusion: 3DMaxConvNet provides efficient, real-time 3D path planning for UAVs with no local minima, high success rate, and significantly reduced runtime compared to A* and RRT* while maintaining comparable path quality on unseen maps without retraining.

Abstract: Safe autonomous Uncrewed Aerial Vehicle (UAV) navigation in urban environments requires real-time path planning that avoids obstacles. MaxConvNet is a potential-field planner that leverages properties of Maxwell's equations to generate a path to the goal without local minima. We extend the 2D MaxConvNet magnetic field planner to 3D, using a convolutional autoencoder to predict obstacle-aware potential fields from LiDAR-derived 101^3 voxel grids. Evaluation across 100 randomized closed-loop trials in two distinct Cosys-AirSim urban environments, a dense night-time cityscape and a suburban district shows a 100% path planning success rate on both maps without retraining. In offline path planning, 3DMaxConvNet produces path lengths comparable to A* on unseen maps while reducing runtime from 0.155--0.17s to 0.087--0.089s, or about 1.7--1.95 times faster than A*. Against RRT*(3k), 3DMaxConvNet achieves similar path quality while reducing planning runtime from 17.2--17.5s to about 0.09s, which is roughly 193--201 times faster than RRT*(3k).

</details>


### [66] [MDrive: Benchmarking Closed-Loop Cooperative Driving for End-to-End Multi-agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10904)
*Marco Coscoy,Zewei Zhou,Seth Z. Zhao,Henry Wei,Angela Magtoto,Johnson Liu,Rui Song,Walter Zimmer,Zhiyu Huang,Chen Tang,Bolei Zhou,Jiaqi Ma*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MDrive is a closed-loop cooperative driving benchmark with 225 scenarios that evaluates multi-agent V2X systems, finding they outperform single-agent systems but face challenges where perception sharing doesn't always improve planning and negotiation can hurt performance in dense traffic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing V2X benchmarks suffer from (i) open-loop evaluations that fail to capture the closed-loop nature of driving, and (ii) current closed-loop evaluations lack behavioral and interactive diversity, making it unclear how beneficial multi-agent systems are for closed-loop driving.

Method: MDrive introduces a closed-loop cooperative driving benchmark comprising 225 scenarios grounded in both NHTSA pre-crash typologies and real-world V2X datasets, with an open-source toolbox for scenario generation, Real2Sim conversion, and human-in-the-loop simulation.

Result: Multi-agent systems generally outperform single-agent counterparts; however, perception sharing enhances perception but doesn't always translate to better planning, and negotiation improves planning performance but harms it in complex/dense traffic scenarios.

Conclusion: MDrive establishes a reproducible foundation for evaluating and improving the generalization and robustness of cooperative driving systems, while highlighting key challenges in perception-to-planning transfer and negotiation in dense traffic.

Abstract: Vehicle-to-Everything (V2X) communication has emerged as a promising paradigm for autonomous driving, enabling connected agents to share complementary perception information and negotiate with each other to benefit the final planning. Existing V2X benchmarks, however, fall short in two ways: (i) open-loop evaluations fail to capture the inherently closed-loop nature of driving, leading to evaluation gaps, and (ii) current closed-loop evaluations lack behavioral and interactive diversity to reflect real-world driving. Thus, it is still unclear the extent of benefits of multi-agent systems for closed-loop driving. In this paper, we introduce MDrive, a closed-loop cooperative driving benchmark comprising 225 scenarios grounded in both NHTSA pre-crash typologies and real-world V2X datasets. Our benchmark results demonstrate that multi-agent systems are generally better than single-agent counterparts. However, current multi-agent systems still face two important challenges: (i) perception sharing enhances perceptions, but doesn't always translate to better planning; (ii) negotiation improves planning performance but harms it in complex and dense traffic scenarios. MDrive further provides an open-source toolbox for scenario generation, Real2Sim conversion, and human-in-the-loop simulation. Together, MDrive establishes a reproducible foundation for evaluating and improving the generalization and robustness of cooperative driving systems.

</details>


### [67] [RoboMemArena: A Comprehensive and Challenging Robotic Memory Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10921)
*Huashuo Lei,Wenxuan Song,Huarui Zhang,Jieyuan Pei,Jiayi Chen,Haodong Yan,Han Zhao,Pengxiang Ding,Zhipeng Zhang,Lida Huang,Donglin Wang,Yan Wang,Haoang Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RoboMemArena is a large-scale robotic memory benchmark with 26 long-horizon tasks (avg. 1000+ steps, 68.9% memory-dependent), featuring VLM-generated multimodal annotations and real-world evaluation. The authors propose PrediMem, a dual-system VLA with predictive coding for memory management, which outperforms baselines on this benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing robotic memory benchmarks lack multimodal annotations for memory formation, have limited task coverage and structural complexity, and remain restricted to simulation without real-world evaluation. RoboMemArena addresses these gaps.

Method: The authors build RoboMemArena via a VLM-based pipeline that designs/composes subtasks, generates full trajectories through atomic functions, and provides memory-related annotations (subtask instructions, native keyframe annotations) with paired real-world tasks. They also propose PrediMem, a dual-system VLA where a high-level VLM planner manages a memory bank (recent + keyframe buffers) and uses a predictive coding head to enhance sensitivity to task dynamics.

Result: PrediMem outperforms all baselines on RoboMemArena, demonstrating improvements in memory management, model architecture, and scaling laws for complex memory systems.

Conclusion: RoboMemArena provides a comprehensive benchmark for robotic memory, and PrediMem shows that combining dual-system VLA architecture with predictive coding is effective for long-horizon memory-dependent tasks in both simulation and real-world settings.

Abstract: Memory is a critical component of robotic intelligence, as robots must rely on past observations and actions to accomplish long-horizon tasks in partially observable environments. However, existing robotic memory benchmarks still lack multimodal annotations for memory formation, provide limited task coverage and structural complexity, and remain restricted to simulation without real-world evaluation. We address this gap with RoboMemArena, a large-scale benchmark of 26 tasks, with average trajectory lengths exceeding 1,000 steps per task and 68.9% of subtasks being memory-dependent. The generation pipeline leverages a vision-language model (VLM) to design and compose subtasks, generates full trajectories through atomic functions, and provides memory-related annotations, including subtask instructions and native keyframe annotations, while paired real-world memory tasks support physical evaluation. We further design PrediMem, a dual-system VLA in which a high-level VLM planner manages a memory bank with recent and keyframe buffers and uses a predictive coding head to improve sensitivity to task dynamics. Extensive experiments on RoboMemArena show that PrediMem outperforms all baselines and provides insights into memory management, model architecture, and scaling laws for complex memory systems.

</details>


### [68] [PriorVLA: Prior-Preserving Adaptation for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10925)
*Xinyu Guo,Bin Xie,Wei Chai,Xianchi Deng,Tiancai Wang,Zhengxing Wu,Xingyu Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PriorVLA preserves pretrained priors via a frozen Prior Expert and trains only an Adaptation Expert (25% of parameters), achieving superior performance over full fine-tuning across simulation and real-world robot tasks, especially under OOD and few-shot settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full fine-tuning of VLA models shifts broad pretrained priors toward narrow training-distribution patterns, limiting generalization. The paper aims to preserve and leverage these priors for more effective downstream adaptation.

Method: PriorVLA keeps a frozen Prior Expert (read-only prior source) and trains an Adaptation Expert. Expert Queries capture scene priors from the pretrained VLM and motor priors from the Prior Expert, integrating both into the Adaptation Expert to guide adaptation with only 25% parameter updates.

Result: PriorVLA outperforms full fine-tuning and SOTA VLA baselines. It improves over pi0.5 by 11 points on RoboTwin 2.0-Hard, achieves 99.1% average success on LIBERO, and on real-world tasks reaches 81% ID / 57% OOD success (standard data) and 48% ID / 32% OOD success (10 demos per task), surpassing pi0.5 by 24 and 22 OOD points respectively.

Conclusion: PriorVLA demonstrates that preserving and leveraging pretrained priors via a frozen expert and query-based integration is a highly effective alternative to full fine-tuning for VLA adaptation, particularly in data-scarce and OOD scenarios.

Abstract: Large-scale pretraining has made Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models promising foundations for generalist robot manipulation, yet adapting them to downstream tasks remains necessary. However, the common practice of full fine-tuning treats pretraining as initialization and can shift broad priors toward narrow training-distribution patterns. We propose PriorVLA, a novel framework that preserves pretrained priors and learns to leverage them for effective adaptation. PriorVLA keeps a frozen Prior Expert as a read-only prior source and trains an Adaptation Expert for downstream specialization. Expert Queries capture scene priors from the pretrained VLM and motor priors from the Prior Expert, integrating both into the Adaptation Expert to guide adaptation. Together, PriorVLA updates only 25% of the parameters updated by full fine-tuning. Across RoboTwin 2.0, LIBERO, and real-world tasks, PriorVLA achieves stronger overall performance than full fine-tuning and state-of-the-art VLA baselines, with the largest gains under out-of-distribution (OOD) and few-shot settings. PriorVLA improves over pi0.5 by 11 points on RoboTwin 2.0-Hard and achieves 99.1% average success on LIBERO. Across eight real-world tasks and two embodiments, PriorVLA reaches 81% in-distribution (ID) and 57% OOD success with standard data. With only 10 demonstrations per task, PriorVLA reaches 48% ID and 32% OOD success, surpassing pi0.5 by 24 and 22 points, respectively.

</details>


### [69] [HarmoWAM: Harmonizing Generalizable and Precise Manipulation via Adaptive World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10942)
*Qiuxuan Feng,Jiale Yu,Jiaming Liu,Yueru Jia,Zhuangzhe Wu,Hao Chen,Zezhong Qian,Shuo Gu,Peng Jia,Siwei Ma,Shanghang Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HarmoWAM is a novel World Action Model that unifies predictive and reactive control through a world model with adaptive gating, achieving 33% and 29% improvements over prior VLA models and WAMs in zero-shot robotic manipulation across unseen environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing WAM paradigms suffer from a fundamental trade-off: "Imagine-then-Execute" models offer generalizable transit but lack interaction precision, while "Joint Modeling" approaches enable fine-grained action generation but are constrained by training distribution exploration space. The authors aim to bridge this gap.

Method: HarmoWAM uses a world model to provide spatio-temporal physical priors that condition two complementary experts: a predictive expert (leverages latent dynamics for iterative action generation) and a reactive expert (directly infers actions from predicted visual evolution). A Process-Adaptive Gating Mechanism automatically determines when and where to switch between them.

Result: Evaluated on six real-world robotic tasks across three training-unseen test environments (varying background, position, and object semantics). HarmoWAM achieves strong zero-shot generalization, outperforming prior VLA models by 33% and WAMs by 29%.

Conclusion: The proposed unified framework successfully resolves the trade-off between generalizable transit and precise manipulation by adaptively combining predictive and reactive control, demonstrating that world models can drive both exploration and precise interaction across different task stages.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for robot control by modeling physical dynamics. Current WAMs generally follow two paradigms: the "Imagine-then-Execute" approach, which uses video prediction to infer actions via inverse dynamics, and the "Joint Modeling" approach, which jointly models actions and video representations. Based on systematic experiments, we observe a fundamental trade-off between these paradigms: the former explicitly leverages world models for generalizable transit but lacks interaction precision, whereas the latter enables fine-grained, temporally coherent action generation but is constrained by the exploration space of the training distribution. Motivated by these findings, we propose HarmoWAM, an end-to-end WAM that fully leverages a world model to unify predictive and reactive control, enabling both generalizable transit and precise manipulation. Specifically, the world model provides spatio-temporal physical priors that condition two complementary action experts: a predictive expert that leverages latent dynamics for iterative action generation, and a reactive expert that directly infers actions from predicted visual evolution. To enable adaptive coordination, a Process-Adaptive Gating Mechanism is proposed to automatically determine the timing and location of switching between them. This allows the world model to drive the reactive expert to expand the exploration space and the predictive expert to perform precise interactions across different stages of a task. For evaluation, we construct three training-unseen test environments across six real-world robotic tasks, covering variations in background, position, and object semantics. Notably, HarmoWAM achieves strong zero-shot generalization across these scenarios, significantly outperforming prior state-of-the-art VLA models and WAMs by margins of 33% and 29%, respectively.

</details>


### [70] [Beyond Self-Play: Hierarchical Reasoning for Continuous Motion in Closed-Loop Traffic Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09153)
*Weifan Zhang,Xiaofeng Zhao,Adel Bazzi,Mingrui Li,Yifan Wei,Dengfeng Sun*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a hierarchical Stackelberg MARL framework for closed-loop traffic simulation that combines high-level interaction-aware intention generation with low-level continuous motion control, enhanced by hybrid co-training to mitigate distribution shift, achieving superior smoothness and safety over self-play and imitation baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-play RL approaches in traffic simulation achieve scalability but produce equilibrium strategies that lack the socially aware behaviors of real human drivers, motivating the need for architectures that capture multi-agent interaction reasoning beyond self-play.

Method: A hierarchical architecture with (1) a Stackelberg-style MARL module for high-level interaction-aware intention commands, (2) a low-level continuous motion module for physically consistent control, and (3) a hybrid co-training scheme combining MARL with auxiliary recovery supervision to address distribution shift in closed-loop deployment.

Result: On a SUMO-based urban network, the framework achieves superior control smoothness and safety compared to self-play and passive imitation baselines, while maintaining competitive traffic efficiency.

Conclusion: Combining hierarchical Stackelberg MARL reasoning with hybrid co-training effectively produces more socially realistic and safe behaviors in closed-loop traffic simulation without sacrificing efficiency.

Abstract: Closed-loop traffic simulation requires agents that are both scalable and behaviorally realistic. Recent self-play reinforcement learning approaches demonstrate strong scalability, but their equilibrium strategies fail to capture the socially aware behaviors of real human drivers. We propose a hierarchical architecture that goes beyond self-play by combining high-level multi-agent interaction reasoning with low-level continuous trajectory realization. Specifically, a Stackelberg-style Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) module generates interaction-aware intention commands. These commands condition a low-level continuous motion module, translating the strategic intent into physically consistent, scene-responsive control sequences. To mitigate distribution shift in closed-loop deployment, we introduce a hybrid co-training scheme combining MARL with auxiliary recovery supervision. Experiments on a SUMO-based urban network demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves superior control smoothness and safety compared to self-play and passive imitation baselines, while maintaining competitive traffic efficiency.

</details>


### [71] [RePO-VLA: Recovery-Driven Policy Optimization for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09410)
*Weijia Liufu,Xiaoyu Guo,Ruiyi Chen,Jingzhi Liu,Kaidong Zhang,Xiwen Liang,Jianqi Lin,Dawei Sun,Yuze Wang,Rongtao Xu,Bingqian Lin,Bowen Yang,Tongtong Cao,Bowen Peng,Dongyu Zhang,Guangrun Wang,Min Wang,Liang Lin,Xiaodan Liang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RePO-VLA improves VLA model robustness in long-horizon contact-rich manipulation by using a recovery-driven policy optimization framework that leverages success, recovery, and failure trajectories, achieving adversarial success rates from 20% to 75% on average and up to 80% in real-world trials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models fail in long-horizon, contact-rich manipulation because success-only imitation learning provides insufficient supervision for execution drift, and failed rollouts are typically discarded, wasting valuable information.

Method: RePO-VLA has three key components: (1) Recovery-Aware Initialization (RAI) slices recovery segments and resets history to make corrective actions depend on the current adverse state; (2) Progress-Aware Semantic Value Function (PAS-VF) aligns trajectory features with instructions and references, using reliability decay to label failure prefixes and distinguish nominal, failed, and corrective actions; (3) Value-Conditioned Refinement (VCR) trains the policy to prefer high-progress actions. A data engine turns adverse states into corrective rollouts via planners or human collection.

Result: Across simulated and real-world bimanual tasks, RePO-VLA improves adversarial success rate from 20% to 75% on average and up to 80% in scaled real-world trials.

Conclusion: RePO-VLA demonstrates that explicitly modeling recovery from failures and assigning distinct roles to success, recovery, and failure trajectories significantly improves robustness in long-horizon contact-rich manipulation without requiring online failure detectors or heuristic retries.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models remain brittle in long-horizon, contact-rich manipulation because success-only imitation provides little supervision for execution drift, while failed rollouts are often discarded. We introduce RePO-VLA, a recovery-driven policy optimization framework that assigns distinct roles to success, recovery, and failure trajectories. RePO-VLA first applies Recovery-Aware Initialization (RAI), slicing recovery segments and resetting history so corrective actions depend on the current adverse state rather than the preceding failure. It then learns a Progress-Aware Semantic Value Function (PAS-VF), aligning spatiotemporal trajectory features with instructions and successful references. The resulting labels salvage useful failure prefixes via reliability decay, while low-value labels mark drift and terminal breakdowns, teaching differences among nominal, failed, and corrective actions. The data engine turns adverse states into planner-generated or human-collected corrective rollouts, teaching recovery to the success manifold. Value-Conditioned Refinement (VCR) trains the policy to prefer high-progress actions. At deployment, a fixed high value ($v=1.0$) biases actions toward the learned success manifold without online failure detectors or heuristic retries. We introduce FRBench, with standardized error injection and recovery-focused evaluation. Across simulated and real-world bimanual tasks, RePO-VLA improves robustness, raising adversarial success from 20% to 75% on average and up to 80% in scaled real-world trials.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [72] [Where Reliability Lives in Vision-Language Models: A Mechanistic Study of Attention, Hidden States, and Causal Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08200)
*Logan Mann,Ajit Saravanan,Ishan Dave,Shikhar Shiromani,Saadullah Ismail,Yi Xia,Emily Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper tests the "Attention-Confidence Assumption" in VLMs (LLaVA-1.5, PaliGemma, Qwen2-VL) and finds that attention map sharpness is a near-zero predictor of correctness (R_pb=0.001). Instead, reliability is better predicted by hidden-state geometry (AUROC>0.95), self-consistency (R_pb=0.43), and sparse late-layer circuits. The study also reveals an architectural split: late-fusion (LLaVA) has a fragile reliability bottleneck, while early-fusion models (PaliGemma, Qwen2-VL) distribute reliability widely and are robust to neuron ablation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To empirically test the pervasive intuition that sharp attention maps in VLMs correlate with confident and calibrated answers (the "Attention-Confidence Assumption").

Method: The authors build a unified mechanistic pipeline (VRP) to compare attention structure, generation dynamics, and hidden-state geometry against correctness labels across three VLM families. They also perform causal neuron-level ablations to identify reliability-critical circuits.

Result: (i) Attention structure is a near-zero predictor of correctness (R_pb≈0.001). (ii) Hidden-state linear probes achieve AUROC>0.95 on POPE for 2/3 families; self-consistency at K=10 is the strongest behavioral predictor (R_pb=0.43). (iii) Late-fusion (LLaVA) has a fragile late bottleneck; early-fusion (PaliGemma, Qwen2-VL) distributes reliability widely and tolerates ~50% hidden dimension ablation with ≤1 pp degradation.

Conclusion: In 3-7B VLMs, reliability is better read from hidden-state geometry, layer-wise margin formation, and sparse late-layer circuits than from attention map sharpness. This has direct implications for designing VLM reliability monitors.

Abstract: A pervasive intuition holds that vision-language models (VLMs) are most trustworthy when their attention maps look sharp: concentrated attention on the queried region should imply a confident, calibrated answer. We test this Attention-Confidence Assumption directly. We instrument three open-weight VLM families (LLaVA-1.5, PaliGemma, Qwen2-VL; 3-7B parameters) with a unified mechanistic pipeline -- the VLM Reliability Probe (VRP) -- that compares attention structure, generation dynamics, and hidden-state geometry against a single correctness label. Three results emerge. (i) Attention structure is a near-zero predictor of correctness (R_pb(C_k,y)=0.001, 95% CI [-0.034,0.036]; R_pb(H_s,y)=-0.012, [-0.047,0.024] on a pooled n=3,090 split), even though attention remains causally necessary for feature extraction (top-30% patch masking drops accuracy by 8.2-11.3 pp, p<0.001). (ii) Reliability becomes legible later in the computation: a single hidden-state linear probe reaches AUROC>0.95 on POPE for two of three families, and self-consistency at K=10 is the strongest behavioral predictor we measure at 10x inference cost (R_pb=0.43). (iii) Causal neuron-level ablations expose a sharp architectural split with direct monitor-design implications: late-fusion LLaVA concentrates reliability in a fragile late bottleneck (-8.3 pp object-identification accuracy after top-5 probe-neuron ablation), whereas early-fusion PaliGemma and Qwen2-VL distribute it widely and absorb destruction of ~50% of their peak-layer hidden dimension with <=1 pp degradation. The takeaway is narrow but consequential: in 3-7B VLMs, reliability is read more reliably off hidden-state geometry, layer-wise margin formation, and sparse late-layer circuits than off attention-map sharpness.

</details>


### [73] [Auto-Rubric as Reward: From Implicit Preferences to Explicit Multimodal Generative Criteria](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08354)
*Juanxi Tian,Fengyuan Liu,Jiaming Han,Yilei Jiang,Yongliang Wu,Yesheng Liu,Haodong Li,Furong Xu,Wanhua Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Auto-Rubric as Reward (ARR) externalizes a VLM's internalized preference knowledge into explicit, prompt-specific rubrics for multi-dimensional evaluation, paired with Rubric Policy Optimization (RPO) that distills this structured evaluation into robust binary rewards for generative training, achieving more reliable and data-efficient multimodal alignment on text-to-image and image editing tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RLHF approaches collapse nuanced preferences into scalar/pairwise labels, losing compositional structure and inviting reward hacking; even Rubrics-as-Reward methods struggle to generate rubrics that are reliable, scalable, and data-efficient simultaneously.

Method: ARR externalizes a VLM's implicit preference knowledge as prompt-specific rubrics (inspectable quality dimensions) before pairwise comparisons, converting implicit structure into explicit constraints. RPO distills ARR's multi-dimensional evaluation into binary rewards conditioned on rubrics for stable policy gradients.

Result: ARR-RPO outperforms pairwise reward models and VLM judges on text-to-image generation and image editing benchmarks, demonstrating superior reliability and data efficiency.

Conclusion: Explicitly externalizing implicit preference knowledge into structured rubrics yields more reliable multimodal alignment, and the core bottleneck is the absence of a factorized interface—not a deficit of knowledge.

Abstract: Aligning multimodal generative models with human preferences demands reward signals that respect the compositional, multi-dimensional structure of human judgment. Prevailing RLHF approaches reduce this structure to scalar or pairwise labels, collapsing nuanced preferences into opaque parametric proxies and exposing vulnerabilities to reward hacking. While recent Rubrics-as-Reward (RaR) methods attempt to recover this structure through explicit criteria, generating rubrics that are simultaneously reliable, scalable, and data-efficient remains an open problem. We introduce Auto-Rubric as Reward (ARR), a framework that reframes reward modeling from implicit weight optimization to explicit, criteria-based decomposition. Before any pairwise comparison, ARR externalizes a VLM's internalized preference knowledge as prompt-specific rubrics, translating holistic intent into independently verifiable quality dimensions. This conversion of implicit preference structure into inspectable, interpretable constraints substantially suppresses evaluation biases including positional bias, enabling both zero-shot deployment and few-shot conditioning on minimal supervision. To extend these gains into generative training, we propose Rubric Policy Optimization (RPO), which distills ARR's structured multi-dimensional evaluation into a robust binary reward, replacing opaque scalar regression with rubric-conditioned preference decisions that stabilize policy gradients. On text-to-image generation and image editing benchmarks, ARR-RPO outperforms pairwise reward models and VLM judges, demonstrating that explicitly externalizing implicit preference knowledge into structured rubrics achieves more reliable, data-efficient multimodal alignment, revealing that the bottleneck is the absence of a factorized interface, not a deficit of knowledge.

</details>


### [74] [Embeddings for Preferences, Not Semantics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08360)
*Carter Blair,Ariel D. Procaccia,Milind Tambe*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies that standard text embeddings measure semantic similarity rather than "preferential similarity" (agreement inversely related to distance), formalizes this as an invariance problem where preference-relevant signal (stance/values) is confounded with semantic nuisance (style/wording), and shows that synthetic training data breaking this correlation improves preference prediction across 11 deliberation datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern AI enables collective decision-making via free-text opinions rather than fixed-set voting, but existing facility location and fair clustering methods require preferential similarity (distance reflects agreement), not semantic similarity. Off-the-shelf embeddings fail when the correlation between semantic and preferential similarity breaks down.

Method: The paper formalizes preferential similarity as an invariance problem: text embeddings conflate preference-relevant signal (stance, values) with semantic nuisance (style, wording). They propose using synthetic training data that decouples these factors to train a scorer that minimizes reliance on nuisance-dominated cosine similarity.

Result: The proposed approach significantly improves preference prediction across 11 online deliberation datasets compared to standard embedding-based cosine similarity.

Conclusion: Achieving genuine preferential similarity requires disentangling preference signal from semantic nuisance, which can be done via carefully constructed synthetic training data that breaks the observational correlation between the two.

Abstract: Modern AI is opening the door to collective decision-making in which participants express their views as free-form text rather than voting on a fixed set of candidates. A natural idea is to embed these opinions in a vector space so that the substantial literature on facility location problems and fair clustering can be brought to bear. But standard text embeddings measure semantic similarity, whereas distances in facility location problems and fair clustering require what we call \textit{preferential similarity}: a participant's agreement with a piece of text should be inversely related to their distance from it. Off-the-shelf embeddings inherit a coarse preference signal through a correlation between semantic and preferential similarity, but fail to capture preferences when the correlation breaks. We formalize this as an invariance problem: text embedding models encode both a preference-relevant signal (stance and values) and semantic nuisance (style and wording), and the two are observationally correlated, so a geometry that relies on nuisance can appear preference-correct even when it is not. We show that synthetic training data designed to break this correlation provably shifts the optimal scorer away from nuisance-dominated cosine and significantly improves preference prediction across 11 online deliberation datasets.

</details>


### [75] [On Distinguishing Capability Elicitation from Capability Creation in Post-Training: A Free-Energy Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08368)
*Yuhao Li,Shengchao Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes distinguishing between capability elicitation (reweighting existing behaviors) and capability creation (expanding the set of reachable behaviors) in LLM post-training, arguing that both SFT and RL can be viewed as reweighting a pretrained reference distribution, and the key question is whether training operates within or expands the model's accessible support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors argue that the common framing of SFT as imitation and RL as discovery is too coarse and fails to capture what truly matters in post-training: whether the procedure merely reweights behaviors the model can already produce or actually expands what the model can practically reach.

Method: The paper introduces the concept of "accessible support" — the set of behaviors a model can practically produce under finite budgets — and presents a free-energy framework where both SFT and RL reweight a pretrained reference distribution, with demonstration signals defining low-energy behavior for SFT and reward signals for RL.

Result: Within this framework, the central distinction is not SFT vs. RL, but whether post-training reweights behaviors already within the accessible support (capability elicitation) or expands the reachable behavioral space through search, interaction, tool use, or new information (capability creation).

Conclusion: Post-training research should shift focus from the SFT/RL dichotomy to whether training procedures elicit existing capabilities or create new ones by expanding the model's accessible support.

Abstract: Debates about large language model post-training often treat supervised fine-tuning (SFT) as imitation and reinforcement learning (RL) as discovery. But this distinction is too coarse. What matters is whether a training procedure increases the probability of behaviors the pretrained model could already produce, or whether it changes what the model can practically reach. We argue that post-training research should distinguish between capability elicitation and capability creation. We make this distinction operational by introducing the notion of accessible support: the set of behaviors that a model can practically produce under finite budgets. Post-training that reweights behaviors within this support is capability elicitation; whereas changing the support itself corresponds to capability creation. We develop this argument through a free-energy view of post-training. SFT and RL can both be seen as reweighting a pretrained reference distribution, only with different external signals. Demonstration signals define low-energy behavior for SFT, and reward signals define low-energy behavior for RL. When the update remains close to the base model, the main effect is local reweighting, not capability creation. Within this framework, the central question is no longer whether post-training is framed as SFT or RL, but whether it reweights behaviors already within reach, or instead expands the model's reachable behavioral space through search, interaction, tool use, or the incorporation of new information.

</details>


### [76] [MemQ: Integrating Q-Learning into Self-Evolving Memory Agents over Provenance DAGs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08374)
*Junwei Liao,Haoting Shi,Ruiwen Zhou,Jiaqian Wang,Shengtao Zhang,Wei Zhang,Weinan Zhang,Ying Wen,Zhiyu Li,Feiyu Xiong,Bo Tang,Muning Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MemQ introduces TD(λ) eligibility traces to assign credit through provenance DAGs in LLM agent memory systems, outperforming independent memory methods across six benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current episodic memory methods for LLM agents treat each memory independently, evaluating retrieval quality in isolation without accounting for dependency chains through which memories enable the creation of future memories.

Method: MemQ applies TD(λ) eligibility traces to memory Q-values, propagating credit backward through a provenance DAG that records which memories were retrieved when each new memory was created. Credit weight decays as (γλ)^d with DAG depth d, replacing temporal distance with structural proximity. The setting is formalized as an Exogenous-Context MDP (EC-MDP) with factored transition decoupling the exogenous task stream from the endogenous memory store.

Result: Across six benchmarks (OS interaction, function calling, code generation, multimodal reasoning, embodied reasoning, expert-level QA), MemQ achieves the highest success rate on all six in generalization evaluation and runtime learning. Gains are largest on multi-step tasks with deep provenance chains (up to +5.7 pp) and smallest on single-step classification (+0.77 pp).

Conclusion: MemQ provides principled guidance for parameter selection (γ and λ) in the EC-MDP structure, demonstrating that structural (DAG-based) credit assignment outperforms temporal-distance-based methods for LLM agent memory.

Abstract: Episodic memory allows LLM agents to accumulate and retrieve experience, but current methods treat each memory independently, i.e., evaluating retrieval quality in isolation without accounting for the dependency chains through which memories enable the creation of future memories. We introduce MemQ, which applies TD($λ$) eligibility traces to memory Q-values, propagating credit backward through a provenance DAG that records which memories were retrieved when each new memory was created. Credit weight decays as $(γλ)^d$ with DAG depth $d$, replacing temporal distance with structural proximity. We formalize the setting as an Exogenous-Context MDP, whose factored transition decouples the exogenous task stream from the endogenous memory store. Across six benchmarks, spanning OS interaction, function calling, code generation, multimodal reasoning, embodied reasoning, and expert-level QA, MemQ achieves the highest success rate on all six in generalization evaluation and runtime learning, with gains largest on multi-step tasks that produce deep and relevant provenance chains (up to +5.7~pp) and smallest on single-step classification (+0.77~pp) where single-step updates already suffice. We further study how $γ$ and $λ$ interact with the EC-MDP structure, providing principled guidance for parameter selection and future research. Code will be available soon.

</details>


### [77] [SkillLens: Adaptive Multi-Granularity Skill Reuse for Cost-Efficient LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08386)
*Yongliang Miao,Ziyang Yu,Liang Zhao,Bowen Zhu,Hasibul Haque*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillLens is a hierarchical skill-evolution framework that organizes skills into a four-layer graph (policies, strategies, procedures, primitives) and retrieves them at mixed granularity, using degree-corrected random walk expansion and a verifier for adaptive reuse, achieving up to 6.31% Acc@1 gain on bug localization and raising success rate from 45.00% to 51.31% on ALFWorld.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill library systems treat skills as flat, single-resolution prompt blocks, creating a tension between relevance and cost: coarse skills introduce irrelevant context, while rewriting entire skills is expensive and often unnecessary.

Method: SkillLens organizes skills into a four-layer hierarchical graph (policies, strategies, procedures, primitives). For a task, it retrieves semantically relevant skill seeds, expands them via degree-corrected random walk over the graph, and uses a verifier to decide whether to accept, decompose, rewrite, or skip each visited unit. It also refines multi-granularity skills and the verifier over time to improve routing decisions.

Result: On MuLocbench, SkillLens achieves up to 6.31 percentage-point Acc@1 gain for bug localization. On ALFWorld, it raises agent success rate from 45.00% to 51.31%.

Conclusion: Mixed-granularity adaptation incurs sublinear cost under sparse mismatch assumptions, and the evolutionary update rule monotonically improves the validation objective until a local optimum. SkillLens consistently outperforms strong skill-based baselines.

Abstract: Skill libraries have become a practical way for LLM agents to reuse procedural experience across tasks. However, existing systems typically treat skills as flat, single-resolution prompt blocks. This creates a tension between relevance and cost: injecting coarse skills can introduce irrelevant or misleading context, while rewriting entire skills is expensive and often unnecessary. We propose SkillLens, a hierarchical skill-evolution framework that organizes skills into a four-layer graph of policies, strategies, procedures, and primitives, and retrieves them at mixed granularity. Given a task, SkillLens first retrieves semantically relevant skill seeds, expands them through degree-corrected random walk over the skill graph, and then uses a verifier to decide whether each visited unit should be accepted, decomposed, rewritten, or skipped. This enables the agent to reuse compatible subskills directly while adapting only locally mismatched components. To improve the system over time, SkillLens further refines multi-granularity skills and verifier in order to improve its routing decisions. We provide theoretical analysis showing that mixed-granularity adaptation incurs sublinear cost under sparse mismatch assumptions and that the evolutionary update rule monotonically improves the validation objective until a local optimum.
