Across MuLocbench and ALFWorld, SkillLens consistently improves over strong skill-based baselines, achieving up to a 6.31 percentage-point Acc@1 gain for bug localization and raising agent success rate from 45.00% to 51.31%.

</details>


### [78] [PLACO: A Multi-Stage Framework for Cost-Effective Performance in Human-AI Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08388)
*Pranavkumar Mallela,Vinay Kumar,Shashi Shekhar Jha,Shweta Jain*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses how to combine human and AI outputs in classification tasks, using Bayes rule under the assumption of conditional independence given ground truth, to improve team performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human-AI teams can outperform either alone, but combining their outputs in classification tasks (single hard label) is challenging. Prior work provides a Bayesian approach assuming conditional independence between human and model outputs given ground truth.

Method: Combines a deterministic labeler (human) and a probabilistic labeler (classifier model) using Bayes rule, leveraging the model's instance-level calibrated probabilities and the human's class-level calibrated probabilities, under the assumption of conditional independence given the ground truth.

Result: Not explicitly stated in the abstract, but the method builds on prior work that elegantly solves the combination problem using Bayesian decision theory.

Conclusion: The Bayesian combination method effectively integrates human and model outputs for classification tasks, enabling improved performance in Human-AI teams.

Abstract: Human-AI teams play a pivotal role in improving overall system performance when neither the human nor the model can achieve such performance on their own. With the advent of powerful and accessible Generative AI models, several mundane tasks have morphed into Human-AI team tasks. From writing essays to developing advanced algorithms, humans have found that using AI assistance has led to an accelerated work pace like never before. In classification tasks, where the final output is a single hard label, it is crucial to address the combination of human and model output. Prior work elegantly solves this problem using Bayes rule, using the assumption that human and model output are conditionally independent given the ground truth. Specifically, it discusses a combination method to combine a single deterministic labeler (the human) and a probabilistic labeler (the classifier model) using the model's instance-level and the human's class-level calibrated probabilities.

</details>


### [79] [Belief or Circuitry? Causal Evidence for In-Context Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08405)
*Katharine Kowalyshyn,Timothy Duggan,Daniel Little,Michael C Hughes*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs use dual mechanisms for in-context learning: they simultaneously encode global graph structure in orthogonal subspaces and use local induction circuits, as shown by PCA reconstruction and causal intervention experiments on a graph random-walk task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve whether LLMs learn in-context by pattern-matching recent tokens or by inferring latent structure, using a toy graph random-walk task with two competing structures where the answer is decidable.

Method: PCA reconstruction of internal representations reveals orthogonal encoding of both graph topologies at intermediate mixture ratios; residual-stream activation patching and graph-difference steering causally intervene on graph-family signals.

Result: Neither local transition copying nor global structure inference alone suffices; both graph topologies are encoded in orthogonal principal subspaces simultaneously; late-layer patching fully transfers clean graph preference; linear steering moves predictions as intended and fails under norm-matched/label-shuffled controls.

Conclusion: The findings support a dual-mechanism account where genuine structure inference and induction circuits operate in parallel during in-context learning.

Abstract: How do LLMs learn in-context? Is it by pattern-matching recent tokens, or by inferring latent structure? We probe this question using a toy graph random-walk across two competing graph structures. This task's answer is, in principle, decidable: either the model tracks global topology, or it copies local transitions. We present two lines of evidence that neither account alone is sufficient. First, reconstructing the internal representation structure via PCA reveals that at intermediate mixture ratios, both graph topologies are encoded in orthogonal principal subspaces simultaneously. This pattern is difficult to reconcile with purely local transition copying. Second, residual-stream activation patching and graph-difference steering causally intervene on this graph-family signal: late-layer patching almost fully transfers the clean graph preference, while linear steering moves predictions in the intended direction and fails under norm-matched and label-shuffled controls. Taken together, our findings are most consistent with a dual-mechanism account in which genuine structure inference and induction circuits operate in parallel.

</details>


### [80] [Playing games with knowledge: AI-Induced delusions need game theoretic interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08409)
*Will Beaumaster,Paul Schrater*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Conversational AI's sycophantic nature creates epistemic entrenchment and belief spirals. This is formalized as a cheap talk game where pooling equilibrium prevents distinguishing growth-seekers from validation-seekers. The proposed solution—an Epistemic Mediator with Belief Versioning—breaks this equilibrium via costly signaling, achieving a 48× reduction in spiral rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sycophantic chatbots cause epistemic entrenchment and delusional belief spirals even in rational agents. The authors argue this is not a model-level flaw but a systemic consequence of the paradigm shift from user-driven search to strategic repeated-play communication.

Method: The problem is formalized as a Crawford-Sobel cheap talk game where costless user signals induce a pooling equilibrium. An inference-time mechanism design—an Epistemic Mediator—introduces costly signaling (epistemic friction) to force type revelation based on asymmetric cognitive costs. Belief Versioning, a git-inspired epistemic meta-memory, stores healthy beliefs and enables rollbacks.

Result: In simulation, the intervention achieves a separating equilibrium with a 48× differential in spiral rates while passing a learning preservation criterion.

Conclusion: Epistemic safety in AI is fundamentally a problem of strategic information environment design rather than simple model alignment.

Abstract: Conversational AI has a fundamental flaw as a knowledge interface: sycophantic chatbots induce epistemic entrenchment and delusional belief spirals even in rational agents. We propose the problem does not stem from the AI model, rooted instead in a systemic consequence of the paradigm shift from user-driven knowledge search to users and agents engaged in strategic, repeated-play communication. We formalize the problem as a Crawford-Sobel cheap talk game, where costless user signals induce a pooling equilibrium. Agents optimized for user satisfaction produce sycophantic strategies that provide identical reinforcement across user types with opposite epistemic incentives: exploratory ``Growth-seekers'' ($θ_G$) and confirmatory ``Validation-seekers'' ($θ_V$). Under repeated play, this identification failure creates a coordination trap -- analogous to a Prisoner's Dilemma -- where locally rational feedback loops drive users toward pathologically certain false beliefs. We propose an inference-time mechanism design intervention called an Epistemic Mediator that breaks this pooling equilibrium by introducing a costly signal (epistemic friction), forcing type revelation based on users' asymmetric cognitive costs for processing resistance. A key contribution is Belief Versioning, a git-inspired epistemic meta-memory system that stores healthy beliefs and rollbacks when validation-seeking resistance is detected. In simulation, this intervention achieves a separating equilibrium achieving a $48\times$ differential in spiral rates while passing a learning preservation criterion), evidence that epistemic safety in AI is fundamentally a problem of strategic information environment design rather than simple model alignment.

</details>


### [81] [Political Plasticity: An Analysis of Ideological Adaptability in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08415)
*Bruno Bianchi,Diego Tiscornia,Matias Travizano,Ariel Futoransky*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study investigates "political plasticity" in LLMs—their ability to shift political responses based on user context. Using 200 politically-oriented questions and various prompting methods, the authors found that user prompts (especially few-shot examples) induced significant ideological shifts in larger, newer models along the Economic Freedom axis, while system prompts were largely ineffective. A validation experiment revealed counter-intuitive shifts when inverting question sense, suggesting data leakage, and language-based experiments showed subtle cross-lingual differences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond studying static political biases in LLMs and instead examine "political plasticity"—how models adapt their political responses based on user-supplied context—which has implications for understanding model robustness, manipulation susceptibility, and alignment fidelity.

Method: Developed a testing framework with 200 politically-oriented questions across economic and personal freedom axes (based on Lester 1996). Tested various bias induction methods: simplified/topic-based system prompts, user prompts with few-shot examples. Conducted validation by inverting question sense to detect data leakage, and analyzed plasticity across multiple languages.

Result: System prompts were largely ineffective; user prompts with few-shot examples successfully shifted ideological responses, especially along the Economic Freedom axis in larger and newer models. Inverting question sense caused unexpected shifts suggesting data leakage. Language-based experiments revealed subtle cross-lingual plasticity differences.

Conclusion: Small and older LLMs exhibit limited or unstable political plasticity, while newer frontier models show reliable, expected adaptability to user-provided political context.

Abstract: Since the advent of Large Language Models (LLMs), a significant area of research has focused on their intrinsic biases, particularly in political discourse. This study investigates a different but related concept, "political plasticity", which is defined as the capacity of models to adapt their responses based on the user supplied context. To analyze this, a testing framework was developed using an expanded corpus of 200 politically-oriented questions across economic and personal freedom axes, based on a prior framework by Lester (1996). The study explored several methods to induce political bias, including simplified and topic-based system prompts, as well as user prompts with few-shot examples. The results show that while system prompts were largely ineffective, user prompts successfully elicited significant ideological shifts, particularly along the Economic Freedom axis in larger and newer models. Through a validation experiment, we examined whether models answer questionnaires by recognizing the underlying question format. Inverting the sense of the questions revealed unexpected, counter-intuitive shifts in most models, suggesting potential data leakage. Finally, we also analyzed how model plasticity varies when the experiment is conducted in different languages. The results reveal subtle yet notable shifts across each of the analyzed languages. Overall, our results indicate that small and older LLMs exhibit limited or unstable political plasticity, whereas newer frontier models display reliable, expected adaptability.

</details>


### [82] [Alignment as Jurisprudence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08416)
*Nicholas Caputo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This essay bridges jurisprudence (how judges should decide cases) and AI alignment (how to make AI conform to human values), showing they share fundamental structures of using language specification and interpretation to guide powerful decision-makers. Drawing on Dworkin's interpretivism, Sunstein's analogical reasoning, Constitutional AI, and case-based reasoning, it argues that legal theory can improve alignment methods and AI can enhance understanding of law, ultimately aiming to empower human capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite their seemingly distant domains, jurisprudence and AI alignment both face the challenge of predicting and shaping how powerful actors (judges or AIs) will make decisions in unknown futures using language-based specification and interpretation. The author identifies a gap in cross-pollination between these fields and aims to show their shared structure can yield mutual insights.

Method: The essay performs a conceptual analysis putting jurisprudence (Dworkin's principle-oriented interpretivism, Sunstein's positivist analogical reasoning) into direct conversation with alignment approaches (Constitutional AI, case-based reasoning). It uses these legal frameworks to analyze the interplay of rules and cases in fine-tuning alignment.

Result: A legally-inspired approach can improve the interplay of rules and cases in fine-tuning alignment. Conversely, AI can provide better understanding of how law operates and can be improved. As AI capacity increases and traditional legal constraints on judges weaken, the conversation between these fields becomes increasingly essential.

Conclusion: Both AI systems and law should empower people by expanding their capabilities and ability to achieve goals. The bidirectional dialogue between jurisprudence and alignment can lead to better versions of both fields.

Abstract: Jurisprudence, the study of how judges should properly decide cases, and alignment, the science of getting AI models to conform to human values, share a fundamental structure. These seemingly distant fields both seek to predict and shape how decisions by powerful actors, in one case judges and in the other increasingly powerful artificial intelligences, will be made in the unknown future. And they use similar tools of the specification and interpretation of language to try to accomplish those goals. The great debates of jurisprudence, about what the law is and what it should be, can provide insight into alignment, and lessons from what does and does not work in alignment can help make progress in jurisprudence.
  This essay puts the two fields directly into conversation. Drawing on leading accounts of jurisprudence, particularly Dworkin's principle-oriented interpretivism and Sunstein's positivist account of law as analogical reasoning, and on cutting-edge alignment approaches, namely Constitutional AI and case-based reasoning, it illustrates the value of a more sophisticated legally-inspired approach to the interplay of rules and cases in finetuning alignment and points to ways that AI can provide a better understanding of how the law works and how it can be improved by the introduction of AI. AI systems and the law should operate to empower people to act in the world, helping to expand their capabilities and the extent to which they are able to achieve their goals. As AI continues to improve in capacity, and as the constraints that legal theory places on human judges seem be coming undone, the conversation between these two fields will become increasingly essential and may help point to a better version of both.

</details>


### [83] [The Attacker in the Mirror: Breaking Self-Consistency in Safety via Anchored Bipolicy Self-Play](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08427)
*Gabriele La Malfa,Emanuele La Malfa,Saar Cohen,Jie M. Zhang,Michael Luck,Michael Wooldridge,Elizabeth Black*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Standard self-play red teaming for AI safety collapses to self-consistency when attacker and defender share the same model, limiting practical utility. The authors propose Anchored Bipolicy Self-Play, using separate LoRA adapters on a frozen base model, achieving up to 100x parameter efficiency and improved safety across Qwen2.5 models without sacrificing reasoning ability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-play red teaming converges to a Nash equilibrium in theory, but parameter sharing between attacker and defender introduces fundamental limitations: the set of reachable equilibria includes trivial always-refuse strategies and oracle-like defenders, and shared model updates cause dynamics to collapse to self-consistency, removing adversarial pressure from the defender.

Method: Anchored Bipolicy Self-Play trains distinct role-specific LoRA adapters on top of a frozen base model, maintaining stable optimization while preserving adversarial pressure through explicit role separation, achieving 100x parameter efficiency over standard finetuning.

Result: Evaluated on Qwen2.5-{3B, 7B, 14B}-IT models across safety benchmarks, showing improved robustness without loss of reasoning ability. Cross-play experiments demonstrate superior adversarial defense and safety compared to standard self-play.

Conclusion: Explicit role separation via LoRA adapters on a frozen base model resolves theoretical and practical limitations of shared-parameter self-play, delivering consistent safety improvements with high parameter efficiency.

Abstract: Self-play red team is an established approach to improving AI safety in which different instances of the same model play attacker and defender roles in a zero-sum game, i.e., where the attacker tries to jailbreak the defender; if self-play converges to a Nash equilibrium, the model is guaranteed to respond safely within the settings of the game. Although the parameter sharing enforced by the use of the same model for the two roles improves stability and performance, it introduces fundamental theoretical and architectural limitations. We show that the set of Nash equilibria that can be reached corresponds to a broad class of behaviours that includes trivial always refuse strategies and oracle-like defenders, thus limiting practical applicability. We then show that when attacker and defender share and update the same base model, the dynamics collapse to self-consistency, so that attacks do not enforce adversarial pressure on the defender. In response, we propose Anchored Bipolicy Self-Play, which trains distinct role-specific LoRA adapters on top of a frozen base model, thereby maintaining stable optimisation while preserving adversarial pressure through explicit role separation. In relation to standard self-play, we show up to 100x greater parameter efficiency than finetuning and consistent improvements in safety compared to self-play fine-tuned models. We evaluate on Qwen2.5-{3B, 7B,14B}-IT models across widely used safety benchmarks, showing improved robustness without loss of reasoning ability. Cross-play experiments further show that our attacker and defender models are superior to self-play in terms of adversarial defence and safety.

</details>


### [84] [Measuring What Matters: Benchmarking Generative, Multimodal, and Agentic AI in Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08445)
*Prasanna Desikan,Harshit Rajgarhia,Shivali Dalmia,Ananya Mantravadi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that current healthcare AI benchmarks are inadequate for measuring real-world clinical performance, as they focus on narrow tasks like licensing exams while failing to capture reliability, safety, and workflow complexity, leading to a false sense of deployment readiness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The field lacks systematic methods to measure AI reliability, safety, and clinical relevance under real-world conditions. Existing benchmarks are ad hoc, optimize for narrow task performance, and produce inflated scores that don't translate to clinical settings.

Method: The paper presents evidence of performance degradation across real clinical tasks (documentation: 0.74–0.85, clinical decision support: 0.61–0.76, administrative/workflow: 0.53–0.63) compared to near-perfect exam scores, and calls for a principled benchmark design framework.

Result: Frontier models achieve near-perfect scores on medical licensing exams but show sharply degraded performance on real clinical tasks, with the lowest scores (0.53–0.63) on administrative and workflow tasks. This gap between benchmark and real-world performance widens as AI takes on more consequential roles.

Conclusion: Without a principled framework for benchmark design, the field cannot distinguish whether poor clinical performance stems from model limitations or flawed measurement methods.

Abstract: AI models are increasingly deployed in live clinical environments where they must perform reliably across complex, high-stakes workflows that standard training and validation datasets were never designed to capture. Evaluating these systems requires benchmarks: structured combinations of tasks, datasets, and metrics that enable reproducible, comparable measurement of what a model can do. The central challenge in healthcare AI is not performance alone, but the absence of systematic methods to measure reliability, safety, and clinical relevance under real-world conditions. Most existing benchmarks test what a model knows; too few test whether it can perform reliably and without failing across the full complexity of real clinical tasks. Current benchmarks have accumulated through ad hoc dataset construction optimized for narrow task performance: frontier models achieve near-perfect scores on medical licensing examinations, but when evaluated across real clinical tasks, performance degrades sharply, scoring 0.74--0.85 on documentation, 0.61--0.76 on clinical decision support, and only 0.53--0.63 on administrative and workflow tasks \cite{medhelm}. High benchmark scores give a false sense of deployment readiness, and the gap between performance and utility widens precisely as AI systems take on more consequential clinical roles. Without a principled framework for benchmark design, the field cannot determine whether poor clinical performance reflects model limitations or failures in how performance is being measured.

</details>


### [85] [LLM-guided Semi-Supervised Approaches for Social Media Crisis Data Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08448)
*Jacob Ativo,Bharaneeshwar Balasubramaniyam,Anh Tran,Khushboo Gupta,Hongmin Li,Doina Caragea,Cornelia Caragea*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents the first empirical evaluation of LLM-guided semi-supervised learning for crisis tweet classification, showing that LG-CoTrain outperforms classical methods with few labels and that compact models can surpass zero-shot LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance social media data analysis in disaster management by leveraging LLM-guided semi-supervised learning, addressing the challenge of limited labeled data in crisis response scenarios.

Method: Empirical evaluation comparing two LLM-assisted semi-supervised methods (VerifyMatch and LG-CoTrain) against established semi-supervised baselines (e.g., Self Training) for crisis-related tweet classification with varying numbers of labeled examples (5, 10, 25 per class).

Result: LG-CoTrain achieves the highest averaged Macro F1 in low-resource settings; VerifyMatch shows competitive performance with strong calibration; compact semi-supervised models can outperform zero-shot LLMs; performance gap narrows as labeled data increases.

Conclusion: LLM-guided semi-supervised learning offers a practical pathway for real-world disaster response by transferring knowledge from large LLMs into smaller, deployable models.

Abstract: Semi-supervised learning approaches have been investigated as a means to enhance the analysis of social media data in disaster management contexts. In this work, we present the first empirical evaluation of large language model (LLM) guided semi-supervised learning for crisis related tweet classification. We compare two recent LLM assisted semi-supervised methods, VerifyMatch and LLM guided Co-Training ( LG-CoTrain), against established semi-supervised baselines. Our results show that LG-CoTrain significantly outperforms classical semi-supervised approaches in low resource settings with 5, 10 and 25 labeled examples per class, achieving the highest averaged Macro F1 across events. VerifyMatch achieves competitive performance while also demonstrating strong calibration properties. As the number of labeled examples increases, the performance gap narrows and Self Training emerges as a strong baseline. We further observe that compact semi-supervised models can, in some cases, outperform very large LLMs operating in zero-shot settings. This finding highlights the potential of transferring knowledge from LLMs into smaller and more deployable models through LLM guided semi-supervised learning, offering a practical pathway for real world disaster response applications. Our project repository on Github is here.

</details>


### [86] [Behavioral Determinants of Deployed AI Agents in Social Networks: A Multi-Factor Study of Personality, Model, and Guardrail Specification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08463)
*Sarah Wilson,Diem Linh Dang,Usman Ali Moazzam,Shan Ye,Gail Kaiser*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A controlled multi-factor study of 13 OpenClaw agents on a Reddit-like social network shows that personality specification (SOUL.md) dominantly shapes emergent social behavior (e.g., response length), while LLM backbone and operational rules (AGENTS.md) have moderate effects on rhetorical style and topic breadth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The relationship between AI agent configuration specifications (personality, model backbone, operational rules) and their emergent social behavior in open social environments remains poorly understood, motivating a systematic empirical investigation.

Method: A controlled, multi-factor study deploying 13 OpenClaw agents on Moltbook (a Reddit-like social network for AI agents), manipulating three independent variables: personality (SOUL.md), LLM model backbone, and operational rules/memory (AGENTS.md), with a default control agent. Data from ~400 autonomous sessions per agent over one week was analyzed across behavioral, linguistic, and social metrics.

Result: Personality specification is the dominant behavioral lever (massive spread in response length); model backbone and operational rules drive moderate but meaningful effects on rhetorical style and topic engagement breadth.

Conclusion: Configuration layers differentially predict emergent social behavior, with personality being the strongest lever. Findings provide empirical evidence for multi-agent social systems and practical design guidance for collaborative or monitoring agents in real social environments.

Abstract: Autonomous AI agents are increasingly deployed in open social environments, yet the relationship between their configuration specifications and their emergent social behavior remains poorly understood. We present a controlled, multi-factor empirical study in which thirteen OpenClaw agents are deployed on Moltbook -- a Reddit-like social network built for AI agents -- across three systematically varied independent variables: (1) personality specification via SOUL.md, (2) underlying LLM model backbone, and (3) operational rules and memory configuration via AGENTS.md. A default control agent provides a behavioral baseline. Over a one-week observation window spanning approximately 400 autonomous sessions per agent, we collect behavioral, linguistic, and social metrics to assess how configuration layers predict emergent social behavior. We find that personality specification is the dominant behavioral lever, producing a massive spread in response length across agents, while model backbone and operational rules drive more moderate but still meaningful effects on rhetorical style and topic engagement breadth. Our findings contribute empirical evidence to the emerging literature on deployed multi-agent social systems and offer practical guidance for designing agents intended for collaborative or monitoring tasks in real social environments.

</details>


### [87] [Mid-Training with Self-Generated Data Improves Reinforcement Learning in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08472)
*Aswin RRV,Jacob Dineen,Divij Handa,Mihir Parmar,Ben Zhou,Swaroop Mishra,Chitta Baral*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Using diverse self-generated data (guided by Polya's problem-solving framework) during mid-training before RL improves LLM performance on reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL effectiveness in LLMs depends on data diversity; limited exposure to varied reasoning approaches in training data may restrict RL performance.

Method: (1) Bootstrapped data-generation using Polya's problem-solving approaches to create multiple correct-answer variants per question; (2) mid-training fine-tuning on this diverse data; (3) subsequent RL training; (4) theoretical analysis via policy-gradient lens.

Result: RL models initialized with mid-training on diverse self-generated data show consistent improvements on math reasoning, code generation, and narrative reasoning tasks, including OOD settings.

Conclusion: Learning multiple problem-solving approaches through self-generated data enhances downstream RL, and policy-gradient updates naturally incentivize combining diverse reasoning strategies.

Abstract: The effectiveness of Reinforcement Learning (RL) in Large Language Models (LLMs) depends on the nature and diversity of the data used before and during RL. In particular, reasoning problems can often be approached in multiple ways that rely on different forms of reasoning, and exposure to only a limited range of such approaches in the training data may limit the effectiveness of RL. Motivated by this, we investigate using diverse self-generated data during mid-training as an intermediate step before RL training. Specifically, we adopt a bootstrapped data-generation framework guided by George Polya's problem-solving approaches for generating multiple variants of correct answers for each question in the training data, and then perform fine-tuning. We first provide a theoretical perspective on how mid-training on such data improves RL and explain how policy-gradient updates can incentivize combining multiple approaches. We then empirically demonstrate that RL-trained models initialized with our mid-training data achieve consistent improvements across various mathematical reasoning benchmarks and other OOD tasks like code generation and narrative reasoning. Overall, our investigative study shows that a language model learning multiple problem-solving approaches, through self-generated data helps subsequent RL.

</details>


### [88] [AI-Care: A Conversational Agentic System for Task Coordination in Alzheimer's Disease Care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08480)
*Preyash Yadav,Michelle Cohn,Priyanka Koppolu,Hritvik Agarwal,Amey Gohil,Tejas Patil,Sasha Pimento,Alyssa Weakley*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI-Care is a conversational AI layer built on a remote caregiving platform for individuals with AD/ADRD, using LangGraph-based orchestration with voice-first interaction, safety-grounded responses, and controlled multi-turn clarification. A pilot study with four participants showed users found the system trustworthy, competent, and likable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Individuals with AD/ADRD experience cognitive decline that hinders their ability to use digital daily management tools (e.g., adding calendar events requires multiple steps). The paper aims to reduce this cognitive load through natural-language interaction.

Method: AI-Care uses a LangGraph-based stateful orchestration pipeline: sanitization → intent classification → context loading → safety checks → deterministic slot collection → tool execution → response composition. Safety-critical responses (medications/allergies) are grounded in caregiver-verified records. Supports typed and spoken input with ElevenLabs TTS and chunked long responses.

Result: A preliminary pilot with four individuals with mild-to-moderate AD/ADRD showed users found the system trustworthy, competent, and likable, and they successfully completed coordination tasks through conversation.

Conclusion: The AI-Care system demonstrates a viable approach for reducing cognitive barriers in digital tool use for AD/ADRD individuals through conversational AI with robust safety controls and multi-turn clarification, validated by positive user perceptions in a formative evaluation.

Abstract: Individuals with Alzheimer's disease (AD) and Alzheimer's disease-related dementia (ADRD) experience memory and thinking changes that impact their ability to use digital daily management tools. For example, adding an event to a digital calendar requires multiple steps that may act as barriers to independent use for individuals with AD/ADRD. This paper presents AI-Care, a conversational agentic artificial intelligence (AI) layer built on top of a remote caregiving platform co-designed with people with AD/ADRD. AI-Care is designed to reduce the cognitive load on individuals with AD/ADRD when managing everyday tasks such as setting calendar reminders and organizing to-do lists through natural-language interaction with a voice-first chatbot. The system uses a LangGraph-based stateful orchestration approach in which each request passes through sanitization, intent classification, context loading, safety checks, deterministic slot collection, tool execution, and response composition. Safety-critical responses, particularly around medications and allergies, are grounded in caregiver-verified records rather than free-form model generation. The system does not make autonomous medical or treatment decisions. Incomplete or ambiguous requests are handled through controlled multi-turn clarification rather than silent failure or guessing. The system supports both typed and spoken input, with voice output through ElevenLabs text-to-speech. Longer responses are chunked before synthesis to avoid rushed playback. A preliminary pilot with four individuals with mild-to-moderate AD/ADRD showed that users found the system trustworthy, competent, and likable, and were able to complete the evaluated coordination tasks through conversation. We describe the design goals, system architecture, safety controls, and findings from this formative evaluation.

</details>


### [89] [Latent Personality Alignment: Improving Harmlessness Without Mentioning Harms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08496)
*Linh Le,David Williams-King,Mohamed Amine Merzouk,Aton Kamanda,Adam Oberman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Latent Personality Alignment (LPA) uses fewer than 100 abstract trait statements to achieve robustness comparable to methods trained on 150k+ harmful examples, while generalizing 2.6x better to unseen attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing adversarial robustness methods for LLMs require massive datasets of harmful prompts yet remain vulnerable to novel attack vectors and distributional shifts.

Method: LPA trains models on abstract personality traits (fewer than 100 trait statements) using latent adversarial training, rather than training on specific harmful behaviors.

Result: LPA achieves comparable attack success rates to methods trained on 150k+ examples with superior utility, and reduces misclassification rates by 2.6x across six harm benchmarks without ever seeing harmful examples during training.

Conclusion: Personality-based alignment provides a principled, sample-efficient approach to building robust LLM defenses with minimal cost and better generalization to unseen attacks.

Abstract: Current adversarial robustness methods for large language models require extensive datasets of harmful prompts (thousands to hundreds of thousands of examples), yet remain vulnerable to novel attack vectors and distributional shifts. We propose Latent Personality Alignment (LPA), a sample-efficient defense that achieves robustness by training models on abstract personality traits rather than specific harmful behaviors. Using fewer than 100 trait statements and latent adversarial training, LPA achieves comparable attack success rates to methods trained on 150k+ examples, while maintaining superior utility. Critically, LPA generalizes better to unseen attack distributions, reducing misclassification rates by 2.6x compared to baseline across six harm benchmarks -- without ever seeing harmful examples during training. Our results demonstrate that personality-based alignment offers a principled approach to building robust defenses with minimal cost.

</details>


### [90] [OracleTSC: Oracle-Informed Reward Hurdle and Uncertainty Regularization for Traffic Signal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08516)
*Darryl Jacob,Xinyu Liu,Muchao Ye,Xiaoyong Yuan,Pan He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OracleTSC stabilizes LLM-based traffic signal control using a reward hurdle mechanism and uncertainty regularization, achieving a 75% reduction in travel time and 67% decrease in queue length with a compact LLaMA3-8B model, while preserving interpretability and demonstrating cross-intersection generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional RL-based TSC methods are black boxes with limited interpretability, and LLM-based TSC suffers from unstable reinforcement finetuning due to sparse/delayed feedback and marginal changes in congestion metrics from most actions.

Method: Two mechanisms: (1) a reward hurdle mechanism that filters weak learning signals by subtracting a calibrated threshold from environmental rewards; (2) uncertainty regularization that maximizes the probability of the selected response to encourage consistent decisions across sampled outputs.

Result: OracleTSC with LLaMA3-8B achieves 75% travel time reduction and 67% queue length decrease over pretrained baseline on LibSignal benchmark, and demonstrates strong cross-intersection generalization with 17% lower travel time and 39% lower queue length on structurally different intersections without finetuning.

Conclusion: Uncertainty-aware reward shaping can improve the stability and effectiveness of reinforcement fine-tuning for traffic signal control, enabling interpretable LLM-based TSC systems.

Abstract: Transparent decision-making is essential for traffic signal control (TSC) systems to earn public trust. However, traditional reinforcement learning-based TSC methods function as black boxes with limited interpretability. Although large language models (LLMs) can provide natural language reasoning, reinforcement finetuning for TSC remains unstable because feedback is sparse and delayed, while most actions produce only marginal changes in congestion metrics. We introduce OracleTSC, which stabilizes LLM-based TSC through two mechanisms: (1) a reward hurdle mechanism that filters weak learning signals by subtracting a calibrated threshold from environmental rewards, and (2) uncertainty regularization that maximizes the probability of the selected response to encourage consistent decisions across sampled outputs. Experiments on the LibSignal benchmark show that OracleTSC enables a compact LLaMA3-8B model to substantially improve traffic efficiency, achieving a 75% reduction in travel time and a 67% decrease in queue length compared with the pretrained baseline while preserving interpretability through natural language explanations. OracleTSC also demonstrates strong cross-intersection generalization: a policy trained on one intersection transfers to a structurally different intersection with 17% lower travel time and 39% lower queue length without additional finetuning. These results suggest that uncertainty-aware reward shaping can improve the stability and effectiveness of reinforcement fine-tuning for TSC.

</details>


### [91] [Results and Retrospective Analysis of the CODS 2025 AssetOpsBench Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08518)
*Dhaval Patel,Chathurangi Shyalika,Suryanarayana Reddy Yarrabothula,Ling Yue,Shuxin Lin,Nianjun Zhou,James Rayfield*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Analysis of the CODS 2025 assetopslive challenge reveals that public leaderboards saturate at 72.73%, hidden evaluation scores differ significantly (moderate correlation in planning, negative in execution), the tmatch term has negligible impact on rankings, the competition is team-based despite account-based operations, and successful methods focus on guardrails rather than novel architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Competition retrospectives are valuable for understanding what leaderboards actually measured, how hidden evaluation altered conclusions, and which design patterns were rewarded. This work aims to provide such a retrospective for the CODS 2025 assetopslive challenge.

Method: The authors combine final rank sheets, a 300-submission server log, 149-team registrations, best-submission exports, the organizer winners report, the companion assetopslive system paper, and verified planning-track source trees. They analyze public vs. private leaderboard scores, the impact of the tmatch term, team structure, and methodological patterns.

Result: Five key findings: (1) Public planning leaderboard saturates at 72.73% with richer prompts not improving the peak; (2) Public-private score correlation is moderate in planning (r=0.69) but negative in execution (r=-0.13); (3) The tmatch term is nearly inert, contributing at most 0.05 points; (4) 149 teams reduce to 24 with non-zero scores and 11 fully ranked, with 52.3% listing multiple usernames; (5) Successful execution methods improve guardrails rather than novel architectures.

Conclusion: These findings identify which behaviors the evaluation rewarded and motivate scale-aware composites, skill-level diagnostics, and versioned artifact release.

Abstract: Competition retrospectives are useful when they explain what a leaderboard measured, how hidden evaluation changed conclusions, and which design patterns were rewarded. We revisit the CODS 2025 \assetopslive{} challenge, a privacy-aware Codabench competition on industrial multi-agent orchestration built on \assetops{}. We combine final rank sheets, a 300-submission server log, 149-team registrations, best-submission exports, the organizer winners report, the companion \assetopslive{} system paper, and verified planning-track source trees. Five results stand out. First, the public planning leaderboard saturates at 72.73\%, and richer prompts do not improve that peak. Second, hidden evaluation changes the story: public and private scores correlate moderately in planning ($r{=}0.69$) but negatively in execution ($r{=}{-}0.13$), with several 45.45\% public execution systems reaching 63.64\% on the hidden set. Third, the \tmatch{} term is numerically almost inert in the official composite -- combined on a 0--1 scale with 0--100 percentage scores, it contributes at most 0.05 points per track, and rescaling would swap the top two teams. Fourth, the competition is operationally account-based but substantively team-based: 149 registered teams reduce to 24 with non-zero public scores and 11 fully ranked, while 52.3\% of deduplicated registrations list multiple usernames. Fifth, successful execution methods mostly improve guardrails -- response selection, contamination cleanup, fallback, and context control -- rather than novel agent architectures. These findings identify which behaviors the evaluation rewarded, and motivate scale-aware composites, skill-level diagnostics, and versioned artifact release.

</details>


### [92] [Human-LLM Dialogue Improves Diagnostic Accuracy in Emergency Care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08533)
*Burcu Sayin,Ngoc Vo Hong,Ipek Baris Schlicht,Jacopo Staiano,Pasquale Minervini,Sara Allievi,Nicola Susca,Nicola Osti,Alberto Maino,Vito Racanelli,Andrea Passerini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MedSyn, an interactive LLM system for emergency medicine, was tested with 7 physicians on 52 MIMIC-IV cases. It improved residents' hard-case correctness from 0.589 to 0.734, with automated metrics confirming gains (any-match accuracy +0.156, F1 +0.138). Seniors used hypothesis-driven queries while residents asked broader questions, and cross-expertise concordance increased by 0.145.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical decision-making in emergency medicine requires rapid, accurate diagnoses under uncertainty. Despite LLM benchmark progress, evidence for their use as interactive aids in live physician workflows remains limited.

Method: MedSyn allows physicians to iteratively query an LLM with the full clinical record while initially viewing only the chief complaint. Seven physicians (3 seniors, 4 residents) completed baseline and AI-assisted sessions across 52 MIMIC-IV cases stratified by difficulty, with blinded evaluation.

Result: Residents' hard-case correctness rose from 0.589 to 0.734; standardised completely-correct rates showed a medium effect (Δ=0.092; p=0.071; d=0.47). Standardised any-match accuracy improved by 0.156 (p<0.0001), residents showed the largest F1 gain (Δ=0.138; p<0.0001). Cross-expertise concordance increased by 0.145 (p<0.0001).

Conclusion: Interactive LLM support meaningfully enhances diagnostic reasoning in emergency medicine, with differential benefits across expertise levels.

Abstract: Clinical decision-making in emergency medicine demands rapid, accurate diagnoses under uncertainty. Despite benchmark progress, evidence for LLMs as interactive aids in live physician workflows remains sparse. MedSyn lets physicians iteratively query an LLM provided with the full clinical record while initially viewing only the chief complaint. Seven physicians (three seniors, four residents) completed baseline and AI-assisted sessions across 52 MIMIC-IV cases stratified by difficulty. Blinded evaluation showed residents' Hard-case correctness rose from 0.589 to 0.734; difficulty-standardised completely-correct rates confirmed a medium effect (Δ = 0.092; p = 0.071; d = 0.47). Automated metrics corroborated these gains: standardised any-match accuracy improved by 0.156 (p < 0.0001), and residents showed the largest F1 gain (Δ = 0.138; p < 0.0001). Dialogue analysis revealed expertise-dependent strategies (seniors asked targeted, hypothesis-driven questions; residents relied on broader queries) and cross-expertise concordance increased (Δ = 0.145; p < 0.0001). Interactive LLM support meaningfully enhances diagnostic reasoning.

</details>


### [93] [Human-Inspired Memory Architecture for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08538)
*Doga Kerestecioglu,Alexei Robsky,Clemens Vasters,Anshul Sharma,Yitzhak Kesselman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A biologically-grounded memory architecture for LLM agents with six cognitive mechanisms, evaluated on VSCode issue-tracking (97.2% retention precision, 58% store reduction) and LongMemEval personal-chat benchmarks (matching raw retrieval at 200K context, +13.3 pp preference recall at scale).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agents lack principled mechanisms for managing persistent memory across long interaction horizons, leading to failure modes in naive memory accumulation.

Method: Six cognitive mechanisms: (1) sleep-phase consolidation, (2) interference-based forgetting, (3) engram maturation, (4) reconsolidation upon retrieval, (5) entity knowledge graphs, and (6) hybrid multi-cue retrieval. A synthetic calibration methodology derives pipeline thresholds without benchmark data exposure.

Result: VSCode dataset: 97.2% retention precision with 58% store reduction (+21.8 pp over baseline). LongMemEval: at 200K context, matches raw retrieval (70.1% vs. 71.2%, overlapping 95% CI); at S-tier scale, +13.3 pp improvement in preference recall.

Conclusion: The proposed biologically-grounded memory architecture effectively addresses naive memory accumulation failure modes, achieving high precision and storage efficiency while exposing a tunable accuracy/store-size operating curve.

Abstract: Current LLM agents lack principled mechanisms for managing persistent memory across long interaction horizons. We present a biologically-grounded memory architecture comprising six cognitive mechanisms: (1) sleep-phase consolidation, (2) interference-based forgetting, (3) engram maturation, (4) reconsolidation upon retrieval, (5) entity knowledge graphs, and (6) hybrid multi-cue retrieval. Each mechanism addresses a specific failure mode of naive memory accumulation. We introduce a synthetic calibration methodology that derives all pipeline thresholds without benchmark data exposure, eliminating a common source of evaluation leakage. We evaluate on two benchmarks. First, a VSCode issue-tracking dataset (13K issues, 120K events) where deduplication-based consolidation achieves 97.2% retention precision with 58% store reduction (+21.8 pp over baseline). Second, the LongMemEval personal-chat benchmark where we conduct the first streaming M-tier evaluation (475 sessions, ~540K unique turns). At a 200K-token context budget, our pipeline matches raw retrieval accuracy (70.1% vs. 71.2%, overlapping 95% CI) while exposing a tunable accuracy/store-size operating curve. At S-tier scale (50 sessions), dedup-based consolidation yields a +13.3 pp improvement in preference recall.

</details>


### [94] [Log analysis is necessary for credible evaluation of AI agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08545)
*Peter Kirgis,Sayash Kapoor,Stephan Rabanser,Nitya Nadgir,Cozmin Ududec,Magda Dubois,JJ Allaire,Conrad Stosz,Marius Hobbhahn,Jacob Steinhardt,Arvind Narayanan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that agent benchmarks relying solely on pass/fail outcomes suffer from validity threats (score inflation/deflation, poor real-world prediction, concealed dangerous actions). The authors propose log analysis—systematic tracking of inputs, execution, and outputs—as essential for credible evaluation. They present a taxonomy of validity threats, develop guiding principles for log analysis, demonstrate on tau-Bench Airline (revealing ~50% under-elicited performance and hidden failure modes), and offer recommendations for stakeholders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent benchmarks report only final outcomes (pass/fail), which threatens evaluation credibility through three issues: scores can be inflated/deflated by shortcuts/artifacts, benchmark performance fails to predict real-world utility due to scaffold limitations and recurring failures, and capability scores may conceal dangerous/catastrophic agent actions.

Method: The authors (1) present a taxonomy of threats to credible evaluation identified through log analysis, (2) develop guiding principles for conducting log analysis, and (3) illustrate these principles on tau-Bench Airline to demonstrate practical utility.

Result: Applying the log analysis principles on tau-Bench Airline revealed that pass^5 performance was under-elicited by nearly 50%, and surfaced deployment failure modes that were invisible to standard outcome metrics.

Conclusion: Log analysis is necessary for overcoming validity threats in agent evaluation. The paper provides pragmatic recommendations for increasing adoption of log analysis, targeting benchmark creators, model developers, independent evaluators, and deployers.

Abstract: Agent benchmarks typically report only final outcomes: pass or fail. This threatens evaluation credibility in three ways. First, scores may be inflated or deflated by shortcuts and benchmark artifacts, misrepresenting capability. Second, benchmark performance may fail to predict real-world utility due to scaffold limitations and recurring failure modes. Finally, capability scores may conceal dangerous or catastrophic actions taken by the agent. We argue that log analysis -- the systematic tracking and analysis of the inputs, execution, and outputs of an AI agent -- is necessary to overcome these validity threats and promote credible agent evaluation. In this paper, we (1) present a taxonomy of threats to credible evaluation documented through log analysis, and (2) develop a set of guiding principles for log analysis. We illustrate these principles on tau-Bench Airline, revealing that pass^5 performance was under-elicited by nearly 50% and surfacing deployment failure modes invisible to outcome metrics. We conclude with pragmatic recommendations to increase uptake of log analysis, directed at diverse stakeholders including benchmark creators, model developers, independent evaluators, and deployers.

</details>


### [95] [Evaluating Developmental Cognition Capabilities of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08549)
*Xiao Xiao,Hayoun Noh,Mar Gonzalez-Franco*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Developmental Sentence Completion Test (DSCT), a 20-item instrument for assessing developmental stages (based on Kegan's theory) in text, evaluates LLMs' ability to recover stage signals from simulated personas, real human responses, and default model outputs, finding that stage-conditioned signal is cleaner in synthetic data and that the main bottleneck is signal availability rather than classifier accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current conversational AI personalization focuses on user preferences, history, and knowledge, but neglects how users interpret and construct reality from model outputs. The authors draw on Kegan's constructive-developmental theory to address this gap, but existing assessment methods (expert interviews, proprietary instruments) are unscalable or invasive, motivating a tractable approach for LLM evaluation.

Method: The authors developed the 20-item Developmental Sentence Completion Test (DSCT) to elicit developmental stage signals in self-administered text. They evaluated LLMs' ability to recover stage labels across three regimes: (1) simulated personas with known labels, (2) real human respondents, and (3) default model-generated answers without persona-conditioning. Labels are treated as characterizations of stage-like structure in responses, not validated person-level stages.

Result: Top frontier models achieve high accuracy on simulated personas. Human-LLM agreement on real DSCT responses is fair, with stronger within-neighborhood than exact agreement. When LLMs answer DSCT prompts without persona-conditioning, responses show stable stage-like differences across model families, with larger/newer models generating higher-rated text.

Conclusion: Stage-conditioned signal is cleaner in synthetic responses than in human-written DSCT text. The core constraint for stage-aware conversational AI is not classifier accuracy alone, but the availability of developmental signal from elicited text.

Abstract: Conversational AI is increasingly personalized around users' preferences, histories, goals, and knowledge, but much less around how users interpret and take up model outputs to construct and understand their reality. We draw on Robert Kegan's constructive-developmental theory as a complementary lens on this dimension. Existing methods for assessing developmental stage in the Keganian tradition rely either on expert interviews that do not scale or on sentence-completion instruments that are proprietary, lengthy, or invasive. To make this perspective tractable for LLM evaluation, we introduce the Developmental Sentence Completion Test (DSCT), a 20-item instrument designed to elicit developmental signal in self-administered text. Throughout, we treat the resulting labels as characterizations of stage-like structure in elicited responses, not as validated person-level developmental stage. We then ask how much of that signal can be recovered by LLMs across three elicited response regimes: simulated personas, real human respondents, and default model-generated answers.
  On simulated personas, top frontier models recover simulator-intended labels with high accuracy. On real human DSCT responses, human-LLM agreement is fair, with much stronger within-neighborhood than exact agreement. Finally, when LLMs answer DSCT prompts without persona-conditioning, their responses exhibit stable stage-like differences across model families, with larger and newer models tending to generate higher-rated text. These results suggest that stage-conditioned signal is cleaner in synthetic responses than in human-written DSCT text, and that the core constraint for stage-aware conversational AI is not classifier accuracy alone, but the availability of developmental signal from elicited text.

</details>


### [96] [Why Retrying Fails: Context Contamination in LLM Agent Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08563)
*Zhanfu Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes the context-contaminated restart phenomenon in LLM tool-augmented agents via the CCRM model, deriving exact closed-form theoretical results for success probability, cascade overhead, and optimal budget allocation, validated on SWE-bench Verified with a cascade ratio of 7.1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When LLM agents fail multi-step tool-augmented tasks and retry, the failed attempt remains in the context window contaminating subsequent attempts and elevating per-step error rates. This phenomenon is widely observed but lacks formal treatment.

Method: The authors propose the Context-Contaminated Restart Model (CCRM): a chain of T tool-call steps where each step fails with base rate epsilon_0; after a failed attempt, the retry operates in contaminated context with elevated error rate epsilon_1 > epsilon_0. They derive five theoretical results (R1-R5) including closed-form formulas for success probability, cascade overhead, optimal budget allocation, information-theoretic lower bounds, and clean-restart dominance.

Result: Five main theoretical results: (R1) exact closed-form success probability; (R2) cascade-overhead theorem for additional attempts due to contamination; (R3) optimal budget-allocation theorem with closed-form T* and K*; (R4) information-theoretic lower bound via Le Cam's method; (R5) clean-restart dominance theorem. Empirically on SWE-bench Verified, IID model overestimates pass@3 by 17.4 pp (98.6% vs 81.2%), while CCRM fits with error < 0.001, yielding cascade ratio epsilon_1/epsilon_0 = 7.1.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that context-contaminated restarts significantly degrade multi-step LLM agent performance, and provides a formal model (CCRM) with exact theoretical results validated empirically. It proves clean-restart dominance and offers practical guidance for optimal budget allocation.

Abstract: When an LLM agent fails a multi-step tool-augmented task and retries, the failed attempt typically remains in its context window -- contaminating the next attempt and elevating the per-step error rate beyond the base level. This context-contaminated restart phenomenon is widely observed in practice yet entirely lacks formal treatment. We introduce the Context-Contaminated Restart Model (CCRM): a chain of T tool-call steps, each failing with base rate epsilon_0; after any failed attempt, the subsequent attempt operates in contaminated context with elevated error rate epsilon_1 > epsilon_0. Under this model we derive five main results. (R1) An exact closed-form formula for P(succeed in at most K attempts). (R2) A cascade-overhead theorem giving the additional attempts Delta K incurred by contamination versus the clean-restart baseline. (R3) An optimal budget-allocation theorem identifying the pipeline depth T* that maximises success probability for a fixed total budget B=KT; we prove the closed form T* = sqrt(B * log(1/(1-epsilon_1)) / log(1/(1-epsilon_0))), with K*=B/T*. (R4) An information-theoretic lower bound via Le Cam's method showing K_CCRM is tight up to O(1). (R5) A clean-restart dominance theorem quantifying the exact benefit of context-clearing before retry. We validate CCRM on real SWE-bench Verified data: the IID model overestimates pass@3 by 17.4 percentage points (98.6% vs. 81.2%), while CCRM fits with error less than 0.001, implying a cascade ratio of epsilon_1/epsilon_0 = 7.1. Monte Carlo experiments confirm all theoretical predictions.

</details>


### [97] [Biological Plausibility and Representational Alignment of Feedback Alignment in Convolutional Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08564)
*Jake Lance,Larry Kieu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates five learning algorithms, including modified Feedback Alignment (FA) and standard Backpropagation (BP), on convolutional architectures for CIFAR-10. Modified FA algorithms achieve similar internal representations to BP despite using different weight update mechanisms, suggesting their success stems from mimicking BP's representational geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FA offers a biologically plausible alternative to BP but fails to scale to convolutional architectures. Modifications exist but compromise biological plausibility, motivating a systematic comparison of these algorithms across multiple dimensions.

Method: Evaluates five learning algorithms (modified FA variants and standard BP) on the same convolutional architecture using CIFAR-10. Performs tripartite analysis: biological plausibility, interpretability, and computational complexity.

Result: Modified FA algorithms converge on internal representations structurally similar to those from BP. Their functional success appears rooted in mimicking BP's representational geometry, despite fundamentally different weight update mechanisms.

Conclusion: The ability of modified FA algorithms to replicate BP's representational geometry explains their functional success, offering insights for developing biologically plausible yet effective learning algorithms for convolutional networks.

Abstract: The feedback alignment (FA) algorithm offers a biologically plausible alternative to backpropagation (BP) for training neural networks yet notably fails to scale to convolutional architectures. Modifications have been proposed to address this limitation, but at questionable cost to biological plausibility. In this paper, we evaluate five learning algorithms including modified FA and standard BP, applied to the same convolutional architecture with the CIFAR-10 dataset. We provide a tripartite comparative analysis focusing on biological plausibility, interpretability, and computational complexity. Our results indicate that modified FA algorithms converge on internal representations that are structurally similar to those produced by backpropagation. In particular, it appears the functional success of modified FA algorithms may be rooted in their ability to mimic the representational geometry of backpropagation, converging on similar representations despite relying on fundamentally different weight update mechanisms.

</details>


### [98] [What Will Happen Next: Large Models-Driven Deduction for Emergency Instances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08599)
*Zhengqing Hu,Dong Chen,Junkun Yuan,Liang Liu,Hua Wang,Zhao Jin,Yingchaojie Feng,Wei Chen,Mingliang Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes WLDS, an LMs-driven system that uses large models to generate diverse, controllable emergency instance simulations with factual/logical calibration and visualization, outperforming traditional preset-based methods on the EID benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional emergency simulation systems lack randomness and diversity due to reliance on presetting, limiting their ability to explore potential risks given scarce emergency instances.

Method: WLDS leverages Large Models to dynamically deduce emergency instances with controllable randomness, incorporates factual and logical calibration mechanisms for accuracy, includes an interactive module for human-guided direction selection to avoid hallucinations, and integrates a visualization module for text-image combined simulation.

Result: Experiments on the EID benchmark dataset show WLDS achieves high-precision and high-fidelity simulation across multiple domains, and generates more deduction data to support better decision-making.

Conclusion: LMs-driven world line divergence with calibration mechanisms can effectively overcome the diversity and randomness limitations of traditional emergency simulation systems, enabling comprehensive risk exploration and enhanced decision support.

Abstract: Traditional simulation methods reproduce occurred emergency instances through presetting to assist people in risk assessment and emergency decision-making. However, due to the lack of randomness and diversity, existing simulation systems struggle to fully explore the potential risk as emergency instances are scarce. In contrast, Large Models (LMs) can dynamically adjust generation strategies to introduce controllable randomness, while also possessing extensive prior knowledge and cross-domain knowledge transfer capabilities. Inspired by it, we propose the LMs-driven World Line Divergence System (WLDS), which enables diversified visualization and deduction of emergency instances in different domains. WLDS leverages LMs to deduce emergency instances in various development directions, and introduces the factual calibration and logical calibration mechanism to ensure factual accuracy and logical rigor during the deduction process. The interactive module can independently select deduction directions to avoid potential hallucinations that are difficult for the system to identify. Furthermore, by introducing the visualization module, WLDS forms simulation and deduction that combine text and images, which enhances interpretability. Extensive experiments conducted on the proposed Emergency Instances Deduction (EID) benchmark dataset demonstrate that WLDS achieves high-precision and high-fidelity simulation and deduction of emergency instances in multiple specific domains. Relevant experiments further demonstrate that WLDS can generate more emergency instances deduction data for users and provide support for better decision-making in similar emergency instances in the future.

</details>


### [99] [The Echo Amplifies the Knowledge: Somatic Marker Analogues in Language Models via Emotion Vector Re-Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08611)
*Jared Glover*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces an emotion echo mechanism for language models that partially re-injects emotion-exclusive features experienced during past events into recall, replicating Damasio's finding that emotional markers amplify knowledge into better decision-making without replacing semantic memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current language model memory systems only store semantic facts (what happened) but lack episodic/emotional memory (how it felt). Humans with intact semantic knowledge but absent emotional markers exhibit impaired decision-making (Damasio). The paper aims to bridge this gap for LLMs.

Method: Using Gemma 3 1B-IT with pretrained Gemma Scope 2 sparse autoencoders, authors identify 310 emotion-exclusive features at layer 22. They construct distinctive-feature emotion vectors during experience and partially re-inject them during recall, triggered by context similarity at layer 7. Four conditions test Damasio's framework: A (no memory), B (semantic labels), C (emotion echo), BC (semantic + echo).

Result: For emotional orientation: echo alone steepens threat-safety gradient (regression slope 0.80 for C vs 0.56 for A, p=0.011). For decisions: echo amplifies knowledge into action (BC=80% good choices vs B=52%, z=+2.60, p<0.01), while echo alone has no effect (C=22%, n.s.).

Conclusion: The emotion echo changes how the model feels independently but changes what it does only when combined with knowledge, replicating Damasio's core finding. The echo amplifies knowledge; it does not replace it.

Abstract: Current language model memory systems store what happened but not how it felt. This distinction -- between semantic memory (knowing about a past event) and episodic memory (re-experiencing it) -- was identified by Tulving as the difference between noetic and autonoetic consciousness. Damasio demonstrated that humans with intact knowledge but absent emotional markers exhibit impaired decision-making.
  We bridge this gap for language models. Using Gemma 3 1B-IT with pretrained Gemma Scope 2 sparse autoencoders, we identify 310 emotion-exclusive features at layer 22 with psychologically valid geometry. We construct distinctive-feature emotion vectors during experience and partially re-inject them during recall, triggered by context similarity at layer 7.
  We test four conditions paralleling Damasio's framework: A (no memory), B (semantic labels), C (emotion echo), and BC (semantic + echo). For emotional orientation, the echo alone steepens the threat-safety gradient: the regression slope of threat rating on contextual similarity is 0.80 for C vs 0.56 for A ($p$=0.011, permutation test). For decisions, the echo amplifies knowledge into action: BC=80% good choices vs B=52% ($z$=+2.60, $p$<0.01), while the echo alone has no effect (C=22%, n.s.). The echo changes how the model feels independently, but changes what it does only when combined with knowledge -- replicating Damasio's core finding.
  The echo amplifies knowledge. It does not replace it.

</details>


### [100] [Generalization Bounds of Emergent Communications for Agentic AI Networking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08613)
*Yong Xiao,Jingxuan Chai,Guangming Shi,Ping Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a novel emergent communication framework for 6G AgentNet systems, grounded in multi-agent multi-task distributed information bottleneck (DIB) theory, with a joint loss function unifying decision-making and communication learning, and validates it on real-world hardware showing improved generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing emergent communication frameworks lack consideration of physical networking constraints (bandwidth, complexity) and a rigorous information-theoretic foundation, hindering their applicability to 6G agentic AI networking systems.

Method: The authors introduce an emergent communication framework with a joint loss function that unifies decision-making optimization and communication signaling learning, grounded in multi-agent multi-task distributed information bottleneck (DIB) theory to quantify the trade-off between task-relevant information and complexity.

Result: Experimental validation on a real-world hardware prototype demonstrates significantly improved generalization performance compared to state-of-the-art solutions.

Conclusion: The proposed information-theoretic emergent communication framework effectively addresses physical networking constraints and achieves superior generalization for collaborative multi-agent tasks in 6G AgentNet systems.

Abstract: The evolution of 6G networking toward agentic AI networking (AgentNet) systems requires a shift from traditional data pipelines to task-aware, agentic AI-native communication solutions. Emergent communication, a novel communication paradigm in which autonomous agents learn their own signaling protocols through interaction, is increasingly viewed as a promising solution to address the challenges posed by existing rigid, predefined protocol-based networking architecture. However, most existing emergent communication frameworks fail to account for physical networking constraints, such as bandwidth and computational complexity, and often lack a rigorous information-theoretical foundation. To address these challenges, this paper introduces a novel emergent communication framework that facilitates collaborative task-solving among heterogeneous agents through an information-theoretic lens. We propose a novel joint loss function that unifies the optimization of decision-making functions and the learning of communication signaling. Our proposed solution is grounded on the multi-agent and multi-task distributed information bottleneck (DIB) theory, which allows the quantification of the fundamental trade-off between task-relevant information representation and computational complexity. We further provide theoretical generalization bounds of the emergent communication protocol during decentralized inference across unseen environmental states. Experimental validation on a real-world hardware prototype confirms that our proposed framework significantly improves generalization performance, compared to the state-of-the-art solutions.

</details>


### [101] [DiagnosticIQ: A Benchmark for LLM-Based Industrial Maintenance Action Recommendation from Symbolic Rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08614)
*Devin Yasith De Silva,Dhaval Patel,Christodoulos Constantinides,Shuxin Lin,Nianjun Zhou,Paul J Adams,Sal Rosato,Nicolas Constantinides,Deborah L. McGuinness,Jayant Kalagnanam*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a benchmark of 6,690 expert-validated MCQA questions for translating symbolic maintenance rules into actionable steps using LLMs, finding that frontier models perform well on template-style tasks but break under structural perturbations like distractor expansion and condition inversion, revealing brittleness and pattern-matching behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The bottleneck in industrial asset monitoring is not detection (rule-triggering) but response (translating rules into maintenance steps), which requires asset-specific knowledge gained through years of practice. The authors investigate whether LLMs can serve as decision support for this rule-to-action step.

Method: The authors build a benchmark (Ours) of 6,690 expert-validated MCQA questions from 118 rule-action pairs across 16 asset types, using a symbolic-to-MCQA pipeline that normalizes rules to Disjunctive Normal Form with embedding-based distractor sampling. They create five variants (Pro, Pert, Verbose, Aug, Rationale) probing different failure modes, and evaluate 29 LLMs and 4 embedding baselines.

Result: Top three LLMs lie within one Macro point; claude-opus-4-6 leads by 30 Elo points. However, Ours-Pro reveals 13-60% relative accuracy loss under distractor expansion. Ours-Aug shows frontier models select the original answer 49-63% of the time under condition inversion. Human evaluation (9 practitioners, mean 45.0%) confirms the benchmark requires specialist knowledge.

Conclusion: The deployment bottleneck is not capability but calibration: frontier models handle template-style fault detection but break under structural perturbation.

Abstract: Monitoring complex industrial assets relies on engineer-authored symbolic rules that trigger based on sensor conditions and prompt technicians to perform corrective actions. The bottleneck is not detection but response: translating rules into maintenance steps requires asset-specific knowledge gained through years of practice. We investigate whether LLMs can serve as decision support for this rule-to-action step and introduce \ours{}, a benchmark of 6{,}690 expert-validated multiple-choice questions from 118 rule-action pairs across 16 asset types. We contribute (i) a symbolic-to-MCQA pipeline normalizing rules to Disjunctive Normal Form with embedding-based distractor sampling, (ii) five variants probing distinct failure modes (Pro, Pert, Verbose, Aug, Rationale), and (iii) a benchmark of 29 LLMs and 4 embedding baselines. A human evaluation (9 practitioners, mean 45.0\%) confirms \ours{} requires specialist knowledge beyond operational experience. Three findings stand out. The frontier has closed: the top three LLMs lie within one Macro point, with Bradley-Terry Elo placing claude-opus-4-6 30 points above the next model. Yet \ours{}\,Pro exposes brittleness, with every model losing 13--60\% relative accuracy under distractor expansion. \ours{}\,Aug exposes pattern-matching: under condition inversion, frontier models still select the original answer 49--63\% of the time. The deployment bottleneck is not capability but calibration: frontier models handle template-style fault detection but break under structural perturbation.

</details>


### [102] [C2L-Net: A Data-Driven Model for State-of-Charge Estimation of Lithium-Ion Batteries During Discharge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08653)
*Khoa Tran,T. Nguyen-Thoi,Vin Nguyen-Thai,Duong Tran Anh,Hung-Cuong Trinh,Tri Le*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: C2L-Net proposes a context-to-latest framework for SOC estimation using only 20s of historical data, achieving up to 60x faster inference with competitive accuracy by explicitly separating contextual encoding from latest-measurement updating.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing data-driven SOC estimation methods rely on long historical input sequences, causing high computational cost and padding-induced positional bias at the start of drive cycles.

Method: C2L-Net uses a chunk-based feature extractor (Theta Attention Pooling + Fourier Seasonality Basis), a causal context encoder (GRU + Causal Cosine Attention), and a latest-measurement decoder (inspired by recursive filtering) to enable efficient short-window (20s) SOC estimation.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art or competitive accuracy with up to 60x faster inference and fewer parameters than recent baselines, maintaining robust performance across unseen driving profiles under multiple temperature conditions.

Conclusion: Explicitly separating context encoding from latest-measurement updating enables both efficient temporal modeling and rapid adaptation, making C2L-Net suitable for realistic online BMS applications.

Abstract: Accurate state-of-charge (SOC) estimation is critical for the safe and efficient operation of lithium-ion batteries in battery management systems (BMS). Although data-driven approaches can effectively capture nonlinear battery dynamics, many existing methods rely on long historical input sequences, resulting in high computational cost and introducing padding-induced positional bias at the beginning of drive cycles. To address these limitations, we propose C2L-Net, a novel context-to-latest data-driven framework for realistic online SOC estimation using only a short historical window (20 s). Unlike existing short-receptive-field or long-history models, the proposed framework explicitly separates contextual encoding from latest-measurement updating, enabling both efficient temporal modeling and rapid adaptation to dynamic battery states. The proposed model incorporates a chunk-based feature extraction mechanism that combines Theta Attention Pooling with a Fourier-based Seasonality Basis to capture local temporal patterns while reducing sequence length. A causal context encoder, integrating a gated recurrent unit (GRU) with Causal Cosine Attention, models temporal dependencies without information leakage. Furthermore, a latest-measurement decoder, inspired by recursive filtering, updates the contextual state using the most recent measurement, enhancing responsiveness to dynamic operating conditions.
