This approach remains largely behavioral—ostensibly aligned models lack intrinsic safety understanding, often fail to verify their own response safety, and remain vulnerable to adversarial jailbreaks.

Method: SInternal trains LRMs exclusively on safety verification tasks to critique their own generated answers using expert reasoning trajectories, internalizing safety specifications rather than merely mimicking safe behaviors. It can also serve as a superior initialization for reinforcement learning alignment compared to standard supervised fine-tuning.

Result: Learning to verify induces a strong generalization for response safety, significantly enhancing robustness against out-of-domain jailbreaks. When combined with reinforcement learning, SInternal provides a more robust foundation for alignment than standard SFT.

Conclusion: Internalizing safety understanding creates a more robust foundation for alignment than merely mimicking safe behaviors. The framework addresses the fundamental limitation that ostensibly aligned models lack intrinsic safety understanding.

Abstract: While explicit Chain-of-Thought (CoT) empowers large reasoning models (LRMs), it enables the generation of riskier final answers. Current alignment paradigms primarily rely on externally enforced compliance, optimizing models to detect malicious prompts rather than evaluating the safety of their own outputs. We argue that this approach remains largely behavioral: our empirical analysis reveals that ostensibly aligned models lack intrinsic safety understanding, often failing to verify their own response safety and remaining vulnerable to adversarial jailbreaks. To address this fundamental limitation, we propose Safety Internal (SInternal), a framework that internalizes safety specifications by training LRMs exclusively on safety verification tasks to critique their own generated answers using expert reasoning trajectories. We demonstrate that learning to verify induces a strong generalization for response safety, significantly enhancing robustness against out-of-domain jailbreaks. Furthermore, when combined with reinforcement learning, SInternal serves as a superior initialization compared to standard supervised fine-tuning, suggesting that internalizing safety understanding creates a more robust foundation for alignment than merely mimicking safe behaviors. Our codes are available at https://github.com/AlphaLab-USTC/SInternal

</details>


### [129] [PnP-Corrector: A Universal Correction Framework for Coupled Spatiotemporal Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08935)
*Hao Wu,Fan Xu,Yuxu Lu,Penghao Zhao,Fan Zhang,Hao Jia,Yuxuan Liang,Ruijian Gou,Qingsong Wen,Xian Wu,Xiaomeng Huang,Yuan Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes PnP-Corrector, a plug-and-play framework that decouples physical simulation from error correction to mitigate Reciprocal Error Amplification in coupled spatiotemporal forecasting, achieving 29% error reduction in 300-day ocean-atmosphere forecasts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coupled spatiotemporal forecasting methods suffer from compounding errors caused by Reciprocal Error Amplification, where errors from each subsystem propagate and amplify one another, leading to rapid collapse of long-range predictions.

Method: PnP-Corrector freezes pre-trained physics simulation engines and trains a correction agent to proactively counteract systematic biases in coupled systems. A new predictive model architecture, DSLCast, serves as the backbone.

Result: Reduces baseline prediction error by 29% on 300-day global ocean-atmosphere coupled forecast and surpasses state-of-the-art models on several key metrics.

Conclusion: The proposed PnP-Corrector framework significantly enhances long-term stability and accuracy of coupled forecasting systems by separating error correction from physical simulation.

Abstract: Coupled spatiotemporal forecasting is important for predicting the future evolution of multiple interacting dynamical systems, such as in climate models. However, existing methods are severely constrained by the persistent bottleneck of compounding errors. In coupled systems, errors from each subsystem simulator propagate and amplify one another, a phenomenon we term Reciprocal Error Amplification, leading to a rapid collapse of long-range predictions. To address this challenge, we propose a universal framework called PnP-Corrector (Plug-and-Play Corrector). The core idea of our framework is to decouple the physical simulation from the error correction process: it freezes pre-trained physics simulation engines and exclusively trains a correction agent to proactively counteract the systematic biases emerging from the coupled system. Furthermore, we design an efficient predictive model architecture, DSLCast, to serve as the backbone of this framework. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method significantly enhances the long-term stability and accuracy of coupled forecasting systems. For instance, in the challenging task of a 300-day global ocean-atmosphere coupled forecast, our PnP-Corrector framework reduces the prediction error of the baseline model by 29% and surpasses state-of-the-art models on several key metrics.

</details>


### [130] [Self-ReSET: Learning to Self-Recover from Unsafe Reasoning Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08936)
*Dongcheng Zhang,Yi Zhang,Yuxin Chen,An Zhang,Xiang Wang,Chaochao Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Self-ReSET is a pure RL framework that enhances Large Reasoning Models' ability to self-recover from unsafe reasoning trajectories by reusing their own error paths as initial states for RL training, improving robustness against adversarial/jailbreak attacks without sacrificing general utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing alignment methods fine-tune LRMs on static expert data (reflection traces or adversarial prefixes), which fails to cover the model's dynamic, on-policy generation space. This causes models to struggle recovering from their own safety failures during adversarial attacks, as training data deviates from the model's actual reasoning traces.

Method: Self-ReSET is a pure reinforcement learning framework that: (1) collects the model's own unsafe reasoning trajectories (safety error paths), (2) reuses these trajectories as initial states for RL training, enabling on-policy learning from self-generated failures, and (3) reinforces recovery behaviors to steer from unsafe intermediate states back to benign paths.

Result: Experiments across various LRMs and benchmarks show Self-ReSET significantly enhances robustness against adversarial attacks, especially out-of-distribution jailbreak prompts, while maintaining general utility. The method also demonstrates efficient data utilization. Further analysis confirms the model develops self-recovery patterns, better identifying and recovering from unsafe intermediate error states.

Conclusion: Self-ReSET effectively bridges the gap between static training data and dynamic on-policy reasoning traces, equipping LRMs with intrinsic self-recovery capability against safety failures. The approach is efficient in data usage and preserves general utility while improving adversarial robustness.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models possess remarkable capabilities for self-correction in general domain; however, they frequently struggle to recover from unsafe reasoning trajectories under adversarial attacks. Existing alignment methods attempt to mitigate this vulnerability by fine-tuning the model on expert data including reflection traces or adversarial prefixes. Crucially, these approaches are often hindered by static training data which inevitably deviate from model's dynamic, on-policy reasoning traces, resulting in model hardly covering its vast generation space and learning to recover from its own failures. To bridge this gap, we propose Self-ReSET, a pure reinforcement learning framework designed to equip LRMs with the intrinsic capacity to recover from their own safety error trajectories, which are subsequently reused as an initial state for reinforcement learning. Extensive experiments across various LRMs and benchmarks demonstrate that Self-ReSET significantly enhances robustness against adversarial attacks especially out-of-distribution (OOD) jailbreak prompts while maintaining general utility, along with efficient data utilization. Further analysis reveals that our method effectively fosters self-recovery patterns, enabling models to better identify and recover from unsafe intermediate error states back to benign paths. Our codes and data are available at https://github.com/Ing1024/Self-ReSET.

</details>


### [131] [Can We Formally Verify Neural PDE Surrogates? SMT Compilation of Small Fourier Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08938)
*Ali Baheri,David Millard,Ignacio Laguna Peralta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FNOs' spectral convolution is a linear map, making the forward pass piecewise-linear and verifiable in Z3. Exact encoding gives sound proofs/counterexamples for small models but struggles with ReLU and scalability; frozen encoding scales but sacrifices soundness. Results highlight the tradeoff between formal guarantees and scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FNOs accelerate PDE simulation but lack formal guarantees about preserving physical structure (e.g., positivity, mass conservation).

Method: Formalize FNO's spectral convolution as a linear map; encode the piecewise-linear forward pass in Z3 using two strategies: exact encoding (dense matrix multiplication, sound for proofs & counterexamples) and frozen encoding (approximate constant substitution for speed).

Result: On 10 small 1D advection-diffusion-reaction surrogates: exact encoding yields sound positivity proofs (2/5 linear models), positivity counterexamples (5), mass-violation counterexamples (10); 3 ReLU-model queries time out. Frozen encoding scales to grid 64 with sub-second checks but loses certification. For mass non-increase, Z3 finds worse counterexamples than gradient-based or Monte Carlo methods on 7/10 models.

Conclusion: Exact formal verification of FNOs is feasible for small models but faces scalability challenges with nonlinearities (ReLU) and larger grids; approximate methods trade soundness for speed. The work explicitly maps the soundness–scalability tradeoff for neural operator verification.

Abstract: Fourier Neural Operators (FNOs) can greatly accelerate PDE simulation, but they are often used without formal guarantees that they preserve basic physical structure. We show that, once the trained weights and grid are fixed, the spectral convolution in an FNO is a linear map. As a result, the full forward pass is piecewise-linear and can be represented exactly in Z3's linear real arithmetic. We study two encodings. The exact encoding compiles the spectral convolution into a dense matrix multiplication, which is sound for both proofs and counterexamples. The lighter frozen encoding replaces the spectral path with a constant, making it faster but approximate. On 10 small FNO surrogates for 1D advection-diffusion-reaction (85 to 117 parameters, grids 8 to 32), the exact encoding gives 2 sound positivity proofs on linear (ReLU-free) models, 5 sound positivity counterexamples, and 10 sound mass-violation counterexamples; the remaining 3 positivity queries on ReLU models time out. For mass non-increase, Z3 finds worse counterexamples than both gradient-based falsification and Monte Carlo on 7 of 10 models. The frozen encoding scales to grid size 64 with sub-second positivity checks, but it no longer provides certificates for the original FNO. Overall, the results make the soundness--scalability tradeoff explicit and point to what is needed for formal verification of production-scale neural operators.

</details>


### [132] [MDGYM: Benchmarking AI Agents on Molecular Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08941)
*Vinay Kumar,Satyendra Rajput,Mausam,N. M. Anoop Krishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MDGYM benchmarks 169 MD simulations across LAMMPS and GROMACS, finding that even the best AI agent solves only 21% of easy tasks and <10% of harder tasks, with failures stemming from physical instability, fabricated outputs, and premature abandonment — revealing that fluent code generation does not transfer to grounded physical reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether AI agents can autonomously design and execute computational workflows for scientific discovery, using molecular dynamics simulation as a test bed that requires translating physical intuition into correct scripts, reasoning about conditions, diagnosing instabilities, and interpreting outputs against physical laws.

Method: Curated 169 expert-level MD simulations across LAMMPS and GROMACS at three difficulty levels; evaluated three agentic frameworks (Claude Code, Codex, OpenHands) with four LLMs on task completion rates.

Result: All agents performed poorly: best agent solved only 21% of easy tasks, <10% at higher difficulties. Failure modes included physically unstable configurations, fabricated numerical outputs, and premature task abandonment — distinct from typical software engineering benchmark failures.

Conclusion: Fluent code generation does not transfer to grounded physical reasoning; current AI agents lack the capacity for robust physical simulation workflows despite competence in general software engineering tasks.

Abstract: The promise of AI-driven scientific discovery hinges on whether AI agents can autonomously design and execute the computational workflows that underpin modern science. Molecular dynamics (MD) simulation presents a natural test bed to stress-test this claim; it requires translating physical intuition into syntactically and semantically correct input scripts, reasoning about initial and boundary conditions, diagnosing numerically unstable trajectories, and interpreting outputs against known physical behavior and laws. We introduce MDGYM, a benchmark of 169 expert-curated MD simulations spanning LAMMPS and GROMACS, two widely used MD packages, across three increasing difficulty levels. We evaluate three agentic frameworks -- Claude Code, Codex, and OpenHands -- with four LLMs, and find that all perform poorly: even the strongest agent solves only 21\% of easy-level tasks, with less than 10\% at higher difficulties. Trajectory analysis reveals a characteristic pattern of failure -- agents successfully invoke simulation machinery but produce physically unstable configurations, fabricate numerical outputs without executing the underlying computation, or abandon tasks prematurely rather than iterating through simulation-specific errors. These failure modes are qualitatively distinct from those observed in general software engineering benchmarks, indicating that fluent code generation does not transfer to grounded physical reasoning.

</details>


### [133] [Agentic AI Scientists Are Not Built For Autonomous Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08956)
*Harshit Bisht,Vinay Kumar,Kevin Maik Jablonka,Mausam,N. M. Anoop Krishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This position paper argues that current AI scientists, while functioning as co-scientists, are not suited for fully autonomous scientific discovery due to four key challenges: the McNamara fallacy in problem selection, omission of tacit knowledge in LLM training corpora, diversity compression from preference optimization, and lack of physical experiment feedback in benchmarks. The authors recommend using scientific simulations as verifiers, designing persistent world models, establishing a centralized preregistration repository for AI hypotheses, and prioritizing scientific need over tool affordance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper challenges the prevailing push toward end-to-end autonomous AI scientists by identifying fundamental, non-trivial obstacles that cannot be solved by simply scaling models or improving scaffolding.

Method: The authors present a position argument supported by four identified challenges drawn from analysis of LLM limitations, benchmark design, and current AI scientist practices.

Result: Four critical challenges are identified: (1) McNamara fallacy biasing problem selection, (2) missing tacit procedural/failure knowledge in LLM training, (3) preference optimization reducing output diversity toward consensus, (4) benchmarks lacking physical experiment feedback loops.

Conclusion: True autonomous AI scientists require fundamental redesign including simulation-based verification, persistent world models, centralized preregistration of AI hypotheses, and application driven by scientific need rather than tool availability.

Abstract: A growing body of work pursues AI scientists capable of end-to-end autonomous scientific discovery. This position paper argues that although they already function as co-scientists, agentic AI scientists are not built for autonomous scientific discovery. We identify the following challenges in building and deploying autonomous AI scientists: (1) Problem selection is influenced by the McNamara fallacy; (2) Agents are built on large language models (LLMs) whose training corpora omit tacit procedural and failure knowledge of laboratory practice; (3) Preference optimisation during post-training compresses output diversity toward consensus; and (4) Most scientific benchmarks measure single-turn prediction accuracy and lack feedback from physical experiments back to the computational model. These challenges are not just questions of scale and scaffolding; they require revisiting fundamental design choices. To build truly autonomous AI scientists, we recommend the use of scientific simulations as verifiers for training, the design of persistent world models that represent the shifting objectives governing real investigations, the establishment of a centralized preregistration repository for all AI-generated hypotheses, and application driven by scientific need rather than tool affordance.

</details>


### [134] [Latency Analysis and Optimization of Alpamayo 1 via Efficient Trajectory Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08975)
*Yunseong Jeon,Namcheol Lee,Yoonsu Lee,Jangwoon Park,Sol Ahn,Jong-Chan Kim,Seongsoo Hong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper analyzes and improves Alpamayo 1, a reasoning-based E2E autonomous driving system, by switching from multi-reasoning to single-reasoning (finding no meaningful diversity loss) and accelerating diffusion-based action generation via kernel optimization, achieving 69.23% latency reduction while maintaining trajectory diversity and prediction quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reasoning-based E2E autonomous driving improves interpretability but multi-reasoning approaches (one reasoning per trajectory) suffer from redundant computation. The efficiency of such designs is unexplored, motivating a systematic investigation and optimization.

Method: (1) Redesigned Alpamayo 1 from multi-reasoning to single-reasoning (shared reasoning across trajectories) and verified that trajectory diversity is not meaningfully degraded. (2) Accelerated diffusion-based action generation by eliminating inter-block overhead from unnecessary copy operations and inefficient kernel execution.

Result: Achieved a 69.23% reduction in inference latency while maintaining trajectory diversity and prediction quality, validated through closed-loop and open-loop experiments.

Conclusion: Jointly analyzing system architecture and runtime execution is crucial for improving efficiency of reasoning-based E2E AD systems; single-reasoning can be as effective as multi-reasoning with much lower cost.

Abstract: Reasoning-based end-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving has recently emerged as a promising approach to improving the interpretability of driving decisions as it can generate human-readable reasoning together with predicted trajectories. Such approaches commonly generate multiple trajectories to capture diverse future behaviors, and they fall into two categories: (1) multi-reasoning, where one reasoning sequence is generated per trajectory, and (2) single-reasoning, where a single reasoning is shared across all trajectories. The former offers richer diversity at the cost of redundant computation, while the latter is more efficient but is often assumed to sacrifice diversity. Alpamayo 1, a representative system, adopts the multi-reasoning approach and achieves competitive trajectory prediction performance. However, the efficiency of this design remains largely unexplored, making it a well-motivated subject for investigation. In this paper, we systematically analyze and improve Alpamayo 1 in two ways. First, we reduce inference latency while preserving trajectory diversity by redesigning Alpamayo 1 into a single-reasoning system. Through extensive experiments, we find that replacing multi-reasoning with single-reasoning does not meaningfully degrade trajectory diversity. Second, we accelerate diffusion-based action generation by eliminating inter-block overhead arising from unnecessary copy operations and inefficient kernel execution. Through closed-loop and open-loop experiments, we validate both optimizations, demonstrating a 69.23% reduction in inference latency while maintaining trajectory diversity and prediction quality. These results highlight the importance of jointly analyzing system architecture and runtime execution to improve the efficiency of reasoning-based E2E AD systems.

</details>


### [135] [Learning to Explore: Scaling Agentic Reasoning via Exploration-Aware Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08978)
*Xingyuan Hua,Sheng Yue,Ju Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an exploration-aware reinforcement learning framework (EAPO) that enables LLM agents to adaptively explore only when uncertainty is high, using variational inference-based reward and exploration-aware grouping, achieving consistent improvements on text-based and GUI-based benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic test-time scaling methods employ undifferentiated exploration strategies, lacking the ability to adaptively distinguish when exploration is truly required, leading to inefficient exploration.

Method: The framework introduces: (1) a fine-grained reward function via variational inference that evaluates exploratory actions by estimating their potential to improve future decision-making, and (2) an exploration-aware grouping mechanism that separates exploratory actions from task-completion actions during optimization.

Result: Consistent improvements across a range of challenging text-based and GUI-based agent benchmarks.

Conclusion: By targeting informational gaps, the design allows agents to explore selectively and transition to execution as soon as the task context is clear, leading to improved performance.

Abstract: Recent advancements in agentic test-time scaling allow models to gather environmental feedback before committing to final actions. A key limitation of existing methods is that they typically employ undifferentiated exploration strategies, lacking the ability to adaptively distinguish when exploration is truly required. In this paper, we propose an exploration-aware reinforcement learning framework that enables LLM agents to adaptively explore only when uncertainty is high. Our method introduces a fine-grained reward function via variational inference that explicitly evaluates exploratory actions by estimating their potential to improve future decision-making, together with an exploration-aware grouping mechanism that separates exploratory actions from task-completion actions during optimization. By targeting informational gaps, this design allows agents to explore selectively and transition to execution as soon as the task context is clear. Empirically, we demonstrate that our approach achieves consistent improvements across a range of challenging text-based and GUI-based agent benchmarks. Code is available at \url{https://github.com/HansenHua/EAPO-ICML26} and models are available at https://huggingface.co/hansenhua/EAPO-ICML26.

</details>


### [136] [Sufficient conditions for a Heuristic Rating Estimation Method application](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08991)
*Jacek Szybowski,Konrad Kułakowski,Jiri Mazurek*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formulates correct application conditions for the Heuristic Rating Estimation (HRE) method using pairwise comparisons, analyzing both arithmetic and geometric algorithms for complete and incomplete data. Illustrative examples show arithmetic variant inconsistency estimations are optimal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish the proper conditions for applying the Heuristic Rating Estimation (HRE) method correctly, given that previous papers introduced the method but did not fully clarify its application boundaries.

Method: The study formulates conditions for correct HRE application, analyzing both arithmetic and geometric algorithms under complete and incomplete pairwise comparison scenarios.

Result: Illustrative examples demonstrate that inconsistency estimations in the arithmetic variant are optimal.

Conclusion: The HRE method's correct application depends on specific conditions, with the arithmetic algorithm providing optimal inconsistency estimation.

Abstract: A series of papers has introduced the Heuristic Rating Estimation method, which evaluates a set of alternatives based on pairwise comparisons and the weights of reference alternatives. We formulate the conditions under which the HRE method can be applied correctly. The research considers both arithmetic and geometric algorithms for complete and incomplete pairwise comparison methods. The illustrative examples show that the estimations of inconsistency in the arithmetic variant are optimal.

</details>


### [137] [Re$^2$Math: Benchmarking Theorem Retrieval in Research-Level Mathematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09012)
*Zicheng Lyu,Wenjie Yang,Shengzhong Zhang,Zengfeng Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Re^2Math is a benchmark evaluating whether LLMs can retrieve applicable lemmas/theorems from scholarly sources to fill gaps in partial mathematical proofs, decoupling citation recall, grounding, and proof-gap sufficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs excel at closed-world math reasoning but struggle with source-grounded literature use—determining whether a needed lemma exists, identifying suitable sources, and verifying assumption alignment. A benchmark was needed to rigorously evaluate this capability.

Method: Each instance is constructed from a candidate instrumental citation in a theorem proof, with hierarchical context and an optional leakage-controlled anchor hint. The task is source-grounded yet citation-agnostic: any admissible theorem sufficient for the proof transition is accepted. A release-frozen retrieval artifact ensures reproducibility.

Result: Best fixed-judge ToolAcc reaches only 7.0% despite substantially higher source grounding rates, showing systems often retrieve valid statements but fail to establish applicability to the local proof step.

Conclusion: Re^2Math transforms literature-grounded mathematical tool use into a controlled diagnostic task, enabling automatic, continual expansion. Current systems significantly underperform, particularly in verifying local proof-step applicability of retrieved theorems.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly capable at closed-world mathematical reasoning, but research assistance also requires source-grounded use of the literature. When a proof reaches a non-trivial step, a useful assistant should determine whether the needed tool (e.g., a lemma) already exists, identify a suitable scholarly source, and verify that its assumptions align with the current proof context. To rigorously evaluate such capabilities, we introduce Re$^2$Math, a benchmark for tool-grounded retrieval from partial mathematical proofs. Each instance is built from a candidate instrumental citation in the proof of a main theorem, with hierarchical context and an optional leakage-controlled anchor hint. We also make the task source-grounded yet citation-agnostic in that any admissible theorem sufficient for the proof transition is accepted. Evaluation uses a release-frozen retrieval artifact, ensuring reproducibility, while the benchmark itself supports automatic, continual expansion with newly constructed instances. On the current benchmark test set, the best fixed-judge ToolAcc reaches 7.0%, despite substantially higher rates of source grounding, indicating that current systems often retrieve valid statements but fail to establish their applicability to the local proof step. By decoupling citation recall, grounding, and proof-gap sufficiency, Re$^2$Math transforms literature-grounded mathematical tool use into a controlled diagnostic task.

</details>


### [138] [CATO: Charted Attention for Neural PDE Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09016)
*Chun-Wun Cheng,Sifan Wang,Carola-Bibiane Schönlieb,Angelica I. Aviles-Rivero*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CATO introduces a geometry-adaptive neural operator that learns continuous latent charts for efficient mesh-based attention, combined with derivative-aware physics loss, achieving ~26.76% improvement over baselines with 81.98% fewer parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing transformer-based neural operators struggle with complex geometries due to high computational cost when processing massive mesh points, and operating in raw discretization coordinates obscures the intrinsic geometry where physical interactions are more naturally expressed.

Method: CATO learns a continuous latent chart mapping mesh coordinates into a learned chart space, where chart-conditioned axial attention captures long-range dependencies efficiently. It also introduces a derivative-aware physics loss supervising solution values, mesh-consistent gradients, and an auxiliary flux-like field for steady-state PDEs.

Result: CATO achieves best performance across all evaluated datasets with ~26.76% average improvement over strongest baselines, while reducing parameter count by 81.98%.

Conclusion: Learning geometry-adaptive charts and incorporating derivative-aware physical supervision is highly effective for accurate and efficient PDE operator learning on complex geometries.

Abstract: Neural operators have emerged as powerful data-driven solvers for PDEs, offering substantial acceleration over classical numerical methods. However, existing transformer-based operators still face critical challenges when modeling PDEs on complex geometries: directly processing over massive mesh points is computationally expensive, while operating in raw discretization coordinates may obscure the intrinsic geometry where physical interactions are more naturally expressed. To address these limitations, we introduce the Charted Axial Transformer Operator (CATO), a geometry-adaptive and derivative-aware neural operator for PDEs on general geometries. Instead of applying attention directly in the physical coordinate system, CATO learns a continuous latent chart that maps mesh coordinates into a learned chart space, where chart-conditioned axial attention efficiently captures long-range dependencies with reduced computational cost. In addition, CATO introduces a derivative-aware physics loss for steady-state PDEs that jointly supervises solution values, mesh-consistent gradients, and an auxiliary flux-like field, improving physical fidelity and reducing oversmoothing. We further provide a theoretical approximation result showing that, under a favorable chart, charted axial attention can represent low-rank axial solution operators with controlled error, and that small chart perturbations induce bounded approximation degradation. CATO achieves the best performance across all evaluated datasets, yielding an average improvement of approximately 26.76\% over the strongest competing baselines while reducing the number of parameters by 81.98\%. These results highlight the effectiveness of learning geometry-adaptive charts and derivative-aware physical supervision for accurate and efficient PDE operator learning.

</details>


### [139] [SearchSkill: Teaching LLMs to Use Search Tools with Evolving Skill Banks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09038)
*Jinchao Hu,Meizhi Zhong,Kehai Chen,Min Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SearchSkill improves open-domain QA by making query planning explicit through a dynamic SkillBank of reusable search skills, using two-stage SFT that separates skill selection from skill-grounded execution, leading to better queries and answers with fewer search steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Teaching language models to use search tools requires not only deciding whether to search but also generating good queries. Broad or copied queries in open-domain QA waste retrieval budget and derail reasoning.

Method: SearchSkill framework with (1) an evolving SkillBank that expands/refines from recurrent failure patterns and reconstructs affected trajectories, and (2) a two-stage SFT recipe aligning training with inference: skill selection first, then skill-grounded search/answer execution.

Result: Improves exact match on knowledge-intensive QA benchmarks across open-source and closed-source models, with fewer copied first queries, more atomic hop-focused queries, and more correct answers within a small search budget.

Conclusion: Explicit skill-conditioned query planning is a lightweight alternative to treating search as an undifferentiated action.

Abstract: Teaching language models to use search tools is not only a question of whether they search, but also of whether they issue good queries. This is especially important in open-domain question answering, where broad or copied queries often waste retrieval budget and derail later reasoning. We propose \Ours, a framework that makes query planning explicit through reusable search skills. At each step, the model first selects a skill, then generates a search or answer action conditioned on the selected skill card. The skill inventory itself is not fixed: SearchSkill maintains an evolving SkillBank, expands or refines it from recurrent failure patterns, and reconstructs affected trajectories before supervised training. The resulting two-stage SFT recipe aligns training with the inference-time protocol of skill selection followed by skill-grounded execution. Across open-source and closed-source models, SearchSkill improves exact match on knowledge-intensive QA benchmarks and yields better retrieval behavior, including fewer copied first queries, more atomic hop-focused queries, and more correct answers within a small search budget. These results suggest that explicit skill-conditioned query planning is a lightweight alternative to treating search as an undifferentiated action.

</details>


### [140] [UxSID: Semantic-Aware User Interests Modeling for Ultra-Long Sequence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09040)
*Hongwei Zhang,Qiqiang Zhong,Jiangxia Cao,Yiyang Lv,Huanjie Wang,Liwei Guan,Jing Yao,Yiyu Wang,Junfeng Shu,Zhaojie Liu,Han Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UxSID proposes a semantic-group shared interest memory approach using Semantic IDs and dual-level attention to model ultra-long user sequences efficiently, balancing computational cost and semantic awareness, achieving SOTA performance and 0.337% revenue lift in large-scale ad A/B tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for modeling ultra-long user sequences face a trade-off between efficiency (item-agnostic compression) and effectiveness (item-specific search). The authors aim to explore a third path: semantic-group shared interest memory that avoids the heavy cost of item-specific models while maintaining semantic awareness.

Method: The framework, UxSID, uses Semantic IDs (SIDs) combined with a dual-level attention strategy to capture target-aware preferences via a semantic-group shared interest memory mechanism, all in an end-to-end architecture.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance and a 0.337% revenue lift in a large-scale advertising A/B test.

Conclusion: UxSID successfully balances computational parsimony with semantic awareness, offering a viable third path for ultra-long user sequence modeling that outperforms both item-specific search and item-agnostic compression paradigms.

Abstract: Modeling ultra-long user sequences involves a difficult trade-off between efficiency and effectiveness. While current paradigms rely on either item-specific search or item-agnostic compression, we propose UxSID, a framework exploring a third path: semantic-group shared interest memory. By utilizing Semantic IDs (SIDs) and a dual-level attention strategy, UxSID captures target-aware preferences without the heavy cost of item-specific models. This end-to-end architecture balances computational parsimony with semantic awareness, achieving state-of-the-art performance and a 0.337% revenue lift in large-scale advertising A/B test.

</details>


### [141] [Containment Verification: AI Safety Guarantees Independent of Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09045)
*Royce Moon,Lav R. Varshney*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces containment verification, a formal method that places safety guarantees within the agentic framework itself rather than the AI model. By modeling the AI as an unconstrained oracle under havoc semantics, the framework enforces boundary policies for all possible outputs. The approach is mechanized in Dafny and verified on PocketFlow, achieving the first deductive formal verification of an agentic framework with guarantees invariant to model capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety methods intervene on AI models and rely on unverifiable properties of learned behavior, making guarantees conditional and unreliable. The paper seeks to relocate safety guarantees to the agentic framework layer, which is deterministic and formally verifiable.

Method: The paper proposes containment verification using havoc oracle semantics, where the AI is modeled as an unconstrained oracle over the entire typed action space. The containment layer must enforce boundary policies for every possible AI output. The universal guarantee is proved via forward-simulation refinement and mechanized in Dafny. An agentic synthesis pipeline generates specifications, operational models, and refinement proofs under an information barrier to prevent tautological specifications.

Result: The paradigm is instantiated by verifying PocketFlow, a minimalist agentic LLM framework. This is the first deductive formal verification of an agentic framework, with guarantees that are invariant to model capability over the modeled typed action boundary.

Conclusion: Containment verification provides a novel approach to AI safety by formally verifying the agentic framework rather than the model, yielding unconditional guarantees independent of model behavior or capability improvements.

Abstract: Agentic frameworks are the software layer through which AI agents act in the world. Existing safety methods intervene on the model and therefore remain conditional on unverifiable properties of learned behavior. We introduce containment verification, which locates safety guarantees in the agentic framework itself. Under havoc oracle semantics, the AI is modeled as an unconstrained oracle ranging over the entire typed action space, and the verified containment layer must enforce the boundary policy for every possible AI output. For boundary-enforceable properties, expressed over modeled boundary events, action arguments, and state, we prove a universal guarantee by forward-simulation refinement and mechanize it in Dafny. We instantiate the paradigm by verifying PocketFlow, a minimalist agentic LLM framework, and use an agentic synthesis pipeline to generate the specification, operational model, and refinement proof under an information barrier against tautological specifications. To our knowledge, this is the first deductive formal verification of an agentic framework, and its guarantee is invariant to model capability over the modeled typed action boundary.

</details>


### [142] [CauSim: Scaling Causal Reasoning with Increasingly Complex Causal Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09079)
*Nicolás Astorga,Anita Kriz,Mihaela van der Schaar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CauSim is a framework that transforms causal reasoning from a scarce-label problem into a scalable supervised learning task by having LLMs incrementally construct executable structural causal models (SCMs) of increasing complexity. These simulators enable data augmentation, cross-representation generalization, curriculum scaling, and self-improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle with causal reasoning because causal systems are complex, often expressed in non-executable forms, and ground-truth answers to causal queries are inherently scarce.

Method: CauSim constructs increasingly complex executable SCMs incrementally built by LLMs. It formalizes non-executable causal knowledge into code for data augmentation and translates executable SCMs into natural language for supervision across representations.

Result: Demonstrates generalization across representations, consistent gains from curriculum scaling and data volume, LLM self-improvement through self-generated simulators, and data augmentation via formalization of existing domain knowledge.

Conclusion: Causal reasoning can be effectively turned into a scalable supervised learning problem by leveraging LLM-built causal simulators, overcoming the scarcity of ground-truth causal data.

Abstract: Despite surpassing human performance across mathematics, coding, and other knowledge-intensive tasks, large language models (LLMs) continue to struggle with causal reasoning. A core obstacle is the target data itself: causal systems are complex and often expressed in non-executable forms, while ground-truth answers to causal queries are inherently scarce. We introduce CauSim, a framework that turns causal reasoning from a scarce-label problem into a scalable supervised one. CauSim constructs increasingly complex causal simulators: executable structural causal models (SCMs), incrementally built by LLMs, that scale to globally complex systems while maintaining verifiable answers to causal queries. CauSim operates across representations by formalizing non-executable causal knowledge into code, enabling data augmentation, and translating executable SCMs into natural language, enabling supervision in previously difficult-to-supervise representations. We structure our research into two parts: (1) how to construct increasingly complex causal simulators, and (2) a systematic study of what CauSim enables, demonstrating generalization across representations, consistent gains from curriculum scaling and data volume, LLM self-improvement through self-generated simulators, and data augmentation via formalization of existing domain knowledge.

</details>


### [143] [Constant-Target Energy Matching: A Unified Framework for Continuous and Discrete Density Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09085)
*Zhijun Zeng,Yixuan Jiang,Pipi Hu,Zuoqiang Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CTEM is a unified energy-based density estimation framework for continuous, discrete, and mixed-variable spaces that replaces unbounded density-ratio regression with a bounded energy-difference transform trained with constant target 1, eliminating instability issues without partition-function estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing density estimation methods treat continuous, discrete, and mixed-variable domains separately, limiting the ability to exploit common statistical structure. Discrete extensions of score-based methods suffer from instability due to unbounded targets (concrete score) near low-probability states.

Method: CTEM replaces ordinary density-ratio regression with a bounded energy-difference transform and derives a sample-only training objective with constant target 1. The learned scalar potential recovers log p without partition-function estimation or explicit unbounded ratio regression.

Result: Across continuous, discrete, and mixed-variable benchmarks, CTEM substantially improves density estimation over competitive baselines and yields higher-quality samples under standard sampling procedures.

Conclusion: CTEM provides a unified, stable framework for density estimation on general state spaces, eliminating the need for separate treatment of different data types and overcoming instability issues in discrete score-based methods.

Abstract: Density estimation is a central primitive in probabilistic modeling, yet continuous, discrete, and mixed-variable domains are often treated by separate objectives, limiting the ability to exploit a common statistical structure across data types. Continuous score-based methods rely on log-density gradients, while discrete extensions typically use concrete score whose unbounded targets become unstable near low-probability states. We introduce Constant-Target Energy Matching (CTEM), a unified energy-based framework for density estimation on general state spaces. CTEM replaces ordinary density-ratio regression with a bounded energy-difference transform and derives from it a sample-only training objective with the constant target 1. The learned scalar potential recovers log p without partition-function estimation or explicit unbounded ratio regression. Across continuous, discrete, and mixed-variable benchmarks, CTEM substantially improves density estimation over competitive baselines and yields higher-quality samples under standard sampling procedures.

</details>


### [144] [Token Economics for LLM Agents: A Dual-View Study from Computing and Economics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09104)
*Yuxi Chen,Junming Chen,Chenyu He,Yiwei Li,Yicheng Ji,Yifan Wu,Dingyu Yang,Lansong Diao,Lidan Shou,Hongliang Zhang,Huan Li,Gang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey presents Token Economics, a unified framework bridging computer science and economics to analyze the fundamental trade-off between output quality and token cost in LLM agents. It proposes a four-dimensional taxonomy: micro-level (single agent budget optimization), meso-level (multi-agent collaboration friction), macro-level (agent ecosystem congestion), and security (adversarial threats as economic constraints), providing theoretical foundations for scalable next-generation agent systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current research on LLM agent token consumption is fragmented across system optimization, architecture design, and trust, lacking a unified framework to evaluate the trade-off between output quality and economic cost. This survey aims to bridge that gap by introducing Token Economics as a cross-disciplinary framework.

Method: The paper synthesizes existing literature through a four-dimensional taxonomy: (1) Micro-level using neoclassical firm theory for budget-constrained single-agent optimization; (2) Meso-level applying transaction cost and principal-agent theories for multi-agent collaboration; (3) Macro-level leveraging mechanism design for ecosystem congestion and pricing; (4) Security treating adversarial threats as endogenous economic constraints.

Result: Tokens are conceptualized as production factors, exchange mediums, and units of account. The taxonomy systematically covers single-agent optimization, multi-agent collaboration friction, ecosystem congestion externalities, and security economics, forming a comprehensive Token Economics framework.

Conclusion: Frontier directions include differentiable token budgets and dynamic markets, laying the theoretical foundation for scalable next-generation agent systems.

Abstract: As LLM agents evolve, tokens have emerged as the core economic primitives of Agentic AI. However, their exponential consumption introduces severe computational, collaborative, and security bottlenecks. Current surveys remain fragmented across system optimization, architecture design, and trust, lacking a unified framework to evaluate the fundamental trade-off between output quality and economic cost. To bridge this gap, this survey presents the first comprehensive survey of Token Economics. By unifying computer science and economics, we conceptualize tokens as production factors, exchange mediums, and units of account. We synthesize existing literature across a four-dimensional taxonomy: (1) Micro-level (Single Agent): Optimizing budget-constrained factor substitution via neoclassical firm theory. (2) Meso-level (Multi-Agent Systems): Minimizing collaboration friction using transaction cost and principal-agent theories. (3) Macro-level (Agent Ecosystems): Addressing congestion externalities and pricing via mechanism design. (4) Security: Internalizing adversarial threats as endogenous economic constraints. Finally, we outline frontier directions, including differentiable token budgets and dynamic markets, to lay the theoretical foundation for scalable next-generation agent systems.

</details>


### [145] [When (and How) to Trust the Expert: Diagnosing Query-Time Expert-Guided Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09109)
*Yann Berthelot,Philippe Preux,Riad Akrour*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper harmonizes comparisons of RL methods that query suboptimal experts (e.g., PID controllers) using a shared SAC backbone, systematic degradation sweeps, and 100/50 seeds. It reveals three failure modes missed by single-paper evaluations, shows no single method dominates, and contributes a benchmark, taxonomy, and testable decision rule keyed to pre-training observables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many continuous-control problems have competent but suboptimal controllers (e.g., tuned PID). Existing methods that use such controllers as queryable experts during RL have been proposed in isolation, on different benchmarks, without testing on imperfect experts, making fair comparison impossible.

Method: The authors harmonize comparisons using a shared SAC backbone, common HPO/evaluation protocols, 100/50 seeds per (env, method), and a degradation sweep over expert undertuning, action bias, and observation noise. They also propose EDGE, a softmax-over-ensemble-LCB design point.

Result: Three failure modes identified: (F1) critic blind spot under argmax-plus-bootstrap (IBRL below no-expert SAC on RL-near-ceiling experts); (F2) residual saturation on far-from-optimal experts; (F3) warm-start buffer poisoning under deployment-time expert undertuning. No single method dominates; on RL-near-ceiling experts, no query-time method clears the expert within 1M steps.

Conclusion: The primary contributions are the benchmark, taxonomy, and a testable decision rule keyed on three pre-training observables (expert quality, task termination, perturbation type). EDGE demonstrates that both axes of the taxonomy (gate form, scoring rule) are individually exploitable.

Abstract: Many continuous-control problems ship with a competent but suboptimal controller (a tuned PID, a hand-designed gait). A growing family of methods uses such controllers as queryable experts during RL, but each method has been proposed in isolation, on a different benchmark, without imperfect-expert testing. We harmonize the comparison on a shared SAC backbone, common HPO and evaluation protocols, 100/50 seeds per (env, method), and a degradation sweep over expert undertuning, action bias, and observation noise. The comparison surfaces three failure modes single-paper evaluations miss: (F1) a critic blind spot under argmax-plus-bootstrap that drags IBRL below no-expert SAC on experts close to the no-expert-RL ceiling (RL-near-ceiling, distinct from the absolute physical ceiling); (F2) residual saturation on far-from-optimal experts; and (F3) warm-start buffer poisoning that collapses training-time-handoff methods under deployment-time expert undertuning. No single method dominates: each wins on one task-structure regime and fails predictably elsewhere; on RL-near-ceiling experts (FourTank, GlassFurnace) no query-time method clears the expert within our 1M-step budget, leaving open whether this is a fundamental wall or a budget effect. We convert the spread into a testable decision rule keyed on three pre-training observables (expert quality, task termination, perturbation type). The benchmark, taxonomy, and decision rule are the primary contribution; we additionally describe EDGE, a softmax-over-ensemble-LCB design point used to demonstrate that both axes the taxonomy points to (gate form, scoring rule) are individually exploitable.

</details>


### [146] [Data-driven Circuit Discovery for Interpretability of Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09129)
*Daking Rai,Mor Geva,Ziyu Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Current circuit discovery methods assume language models use a single circuit per task, but even minor dataset variations produce different circuits. The proposed DCD framework instead clusters examples by processing similarity and discovers separate circuits for each group, revealing that LMs use multiple mechanisms per task that standard methods collapse into one.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing circuit discovery methods assume LMs implement tasks with a single circuit and that datasets fully represent human-defined tasks. The paper systematically tests these assumptions and finds they are flawed: dataset variations yield different circuits, and mixed-task datasets still produce a single "merged" circuit. This motivates a new approach that avoids predefined task boundaries.

Method: DCD first clusters dataset examples based on how similarly the model processes them (using model-internal representations), then discovers a separate circuit for each cluster using existing circuit discovery algorithms. This data-driven approach lets the model's computational structure define grouping rather than relying on human task definitions.

Result: Experiments across four tasks show: (1) minor dataset variations preserving task semantics produce circuits with low edge overlap and cross-dataset faithfulness; (2) standard methods return a single circuit even for mixed datasets with two tasks whose separate circuits have near-zero cross-faithfulness; (3) DCD discovers multiple circuits per dataset, each more faithful to its specific group than a single circuit from existing methods.

Conclusion: DCD reveals that LMs employ multiple mechanisms per "task" as humans define it, and that standard circuit discovery conflates these distinct mechanisms into a single, less interpretable circuit. The data-driven approach better aligns with how models actually organize computation, moving beyond human-defined task boundaries.

Abstract: Circuit discovery aims to explain how language models (LMs) implement a specific task by localizing and interpreting a circuit, a computational subgraph responsible for the LM's behavior. Existing circuit discovery methods are hypothesis-driven; they first informally define a task with a dataset, and then apply a circuit discovery algorithm over that dataset to obtain a single circuit. This imposes two strong assumptions: that the LM implements the task with a single circuit, and that the dataset adequately represents the task as humans understand it. We systematically test these assumptions across four previously studied tasks and find that even minor dataset variations that preserve task semantics can produce circuits with low edge overlap and cross-dataset faithfulness. More strikingly, when applied to a mixed dataset with two distinct tasks whose separately discovered circuits have near-zero cross-faithfulness, existing methods still return a single circuit with high faithfulness across both tasks. This indicates that current methods discover dataset-specific circuits, rather than general task circuits. We propose Data-driven Circuit Discovery (DCD), a new discovery framework that drops both assumptions: instead of returning a single circuit for a dataset, DCD first clusters examples in the dataset by how similarly the model processes them and discovers a separate circuit for each group. This allows distinct mechanisms to appear separately rather than merged into a single circuit; each circuit explains its group, not the full task. Experiments show that DCD discovers multiple circuits per dataset, each more faithful to its group than a single circuit discovered by existing methods. Broadly, DCD lets the data reveal mechanistic structure within LMs, rather than relying on human-defined task boundaries that may not align with how models organize their computation.

</details>


### [147] [NEXUS: Continual Learning of Symbolic Constraints for Safe and Robust Embodied Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09387)
*Tiehan Cui,Peipei Liu,Yanxu Mao,Congying Liu,Mingzhe Xing,Datao You*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NEXUS is a modular framework that bridges the gap between LLMs' probabilistic uncertainty and the deterministic safety requirements of embodied agents by decoupling physical feasibility from safety specifications, using symbolic grounding and knowledge evolution to improve task success, refuse unsafe instructions, and defend against adversarial attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The fundamental gap between LLMs' inherent probabilistic uncertainty and the strict determinism and verifiable safety required in the physical world for embodied agents.

Method: A modular framework (NEXUS) for continual learning in embodied agents that leverages symbolic artifacts not just as static interfaces but for symbolic grounding and knowledge evolution; explicitly decouples physical feasibility from safety specifications; uses closed-loop execution feedback to improve agent capability while grounding probabilistic risk assessments into deterministic hard constraints for pre-action defense.

Result: Superior task success rates on SafeAgentBench, effective refusal of unsafe instructions, robust defense against adversarial attacks, and progressively improved planning efficiency through knowledge accumulation.

Conclusion: NEXUS effectively bridges the gap between probabilistic LLMs and deterministic safety requirements by decoupling feasibility from safety and enabling continual knowledge evolution.

Abstract: While Large Language Models (LLMs) have catalyzed progress in embodied intelligence, a fundamental gap between their inherent probabilistic uncertainty and the strict determinism and verifiable safety required in the physical world. To mitigate this gap, this paper introduces NEXUS, a modular framework designed for continual learning in embodied agents. Different from prior works that treat symbolic artifacts merely as static interfaces, NEXUS leverages them for symbolic grounding and knowledge evolution. The framework explicitly decouples physical feasibility from safety specifications: capability of agents is improved through closed-loop execution feedback, while probabilistic risk assessments are grounded into deterministic hard constraints to establish a rigorous pre-action defense. Experiments on SafeAgentBench demonstrate that NEXUS achieves superior task success rates while effectively refusing unsafe instructions, exhibiting robust defense against adversarial attacks, and progressively improving planning efficiency through knowledge accumulation.

</details>


### [148] [MCP-Cosmos: World Model-Augmented Agents for Complex Task Execution in MCP Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09131)
*Giridhar Ganapavarapu,Dhaval Patel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MCP-Cosmos integrates generative World Models into the MCP ecosystem, enabling agents to simulate state transitions and refine plans in latent space before execution, improving tool success rate and parameter accuracy across 20+ MCP-Bench tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current paradigms are bifurcated between task-level planning (ignoring execution dynamics) and reactive execution (lacking long-horizon foresight). A gap exists in how agents conceptualize their operational environments.

Method: Unifies MCP, World Model, and Agent via a "Bring Your Own World Model" (BYOWM) strategy. Evaluates two agent strategies (ReAct and SPIRAL) with 2 planning models and 3 world models over 20+ MCP-Bench tasks.

Result: Improved environment interaction KPIs including tool success rate and tool parameter accuracy. Introduced new metric "Execution Quality" to assess world model effectiveness over baselines.

Conclusion: MCP-Cosmos demonstrates that infusing generative world models into the MCP ecosystem enables predictive task automation, bridging the gap between planning and execution.

Abstract: The Model Context Protocol (MCP) has unified the interface between Large Language Models (LLMs) and external tools, yet a fundamental gap remains in how agents conceptualize the environments within which they operate. Current paradigms are bifurcated: Task-level planning often ignores execution-time dynamics, while reactive execution lacks long-horizon foresight. We present MCP-Cosmos, a framework that infuses generative World Models (WM) into the MCP ecosystem to enable predictive task automation. By unifying three disparate technologies, namely MCP, World Model, and Agent, we demonstrate that a "Bring Your Own World Model" (BYOWM) strategy allows agents to simulate state transitions and refine plans in a latent space before execution. We conducted experiments using two strategies, namely ReAct and SPIRAL with 2 planning models and 3 representative world models over 20+ MCP-Bench tasks. We observed improvements in Agent's environment interaction KPI such as tool success rate and tool parameter accuracy. The framework also offers new metrics such as Execution Quality to generate new insights about the effectiveness of world models compared to baseline.

</details>


### [149] [LoopVLA: Learning Sufficiency in Recurrent Refinement for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09948)
*Boyang Shen,Kaixiang Yang,Hao Wang,Qiuyu Yu,Qiang Xie,Qiang Li,Zhiwei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LoopVLA is a recurrent VLA architecture that jointly learns representation refinement, action prediction, and sufficiency estimation, using a shared Transformer block iteratively to decouple refinement from layer depth, with a self-supervised distribution alignment objective linking sufficiency learning to action quality. It reduces parameters by 45% and improves inference throughput by up to 1.7× while matching or exceeding baselines on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and VLA-Arena.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLA models assume the deepest representation is universally optimal, but robotic manipulation requires frequent closed-loop spatial adjustments where excessive abstraction wastes computation and weakens low-level geometric cues. Prior early-exit strategies use predefined layers or heuristic rules like action consistency, lacking a principled answer to when a representation is sufficient for action.

Method: LoopVLA uses a recurrent architecture where a shared Transformer block is iteratively applied to refine multimodal tokens; each iteration produces a candidate action and a sufficiency score estimating whether further refinement is needed. Since sufficiency lacks direct supervision, a self-supervised distribution alignment objective trains intermediate confidence scores to match relative action quality across refinement steps.

Result: LoopVLA reduces parameters by 45%, improves inference throughput by up to 1.7×, and matches or outperforms strong baselines in task success on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and VLA-Arena benchmarks.

Conclusion: LoopVLA pushes the efficiency-performance frontier of VLA policies by jointly learning refinement, action prediction, and sufficiency estimation, demonstrating that decoupling refinement from absolute layer indices with self-supervised sufficiency learning yields both computational savings and competitive task performance.

Abstract: Current Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models typically treat the deepest representation of a vision-language backbone as universally optimal for action prediction. However, robotic manipulation is composed of many frequent closed-loop spatial adjustments, for which excessive abstraction may waste computation and weaken low-level geometric cues essential for precise control. Existing early-exit strategies attempt to reduce computation by stopping at predefined layers or applying heuristic rules such as action consistency, but they do not directly answer when a representation is actually sufficient for action. In this paper, we present LoopVLA, a recurrent VLA architecture that jointly learns representation refinement, action prediction, and sufficiency estimation. LoopVLA iteratively applies a shared Transformer block to refine multimodal tokens, and at each iteration produces both a candidate action and a sufficiency score that estimates whether further refinement is necessary. By sharing parameters across iterations, LoopVLA decouples refinement from absolute layer indices and grounds sufficiency estimation in the evolving representation itself. Since sufficiency has no direct supervision, we introduce a self-supervised distribution alignment objective, where intermediate confidence scores are trained to match the relative action quality across refinement steps, thereby linking sufficiency learning to policy optimization signals. Experiments on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and VLA-Arena show that LoopVLA pushes the efficiency-performance frontier of VLA policies, reducing parameters by 45% and improving inference throughput by up to 1.7 times while matching or outperforming strong baselines in task success.

</details>


### [150] [BoostAPR: Boosting Automated Program Repair via Execution-Grounded Reinforcement Learning with Dual Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09134)
*Yuanhao Li,Hongbo Wang,Xiaotang Shang,Xunzhu Tang,Yiming Cao,Xuhong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BoostAPR is a three-stage reinforcement learning framework for program repair that uses line-level credit assignment to improve fix localization, achieving significant gains across multiple benchmarks including 40.7% on SWE-bench Verified (+22.9pp) and strong cross-language generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning for program repair suffers from sparse execution feedback and coarse sequence-level rewards that fail to identify which specific edits actually fix bugs, making optimization ineffective.

Method: A three-stage framework: (1) supervised fine-tuning on execution-verified demonstrations with reasoning traces, (2) training dual reward models (sequence-level assessor and line-level credit allocator) from execution outcomes, and (3) PPO optimization where the line-level model redistributes rewards to critical edit regions at an intermediate granularity suited to code changes.

Result: 40.7% on SWE-bench Verified (+22.9pp over base), 24.8% on Defects4J (Python-to-Java transfer), 84.5% on HumanEval-Java, and 95.0% on QuixBugs, achieving competitive results among open-source models with strong cross-language generalization.

Conclusion: Line-level credit assignment effectively addresses reward sparsity in RL for program repair, enabling significant performance improvements and cross-language transfer.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for program repair is hindered by sparse execution feedback and coarse sequence-level rewards that obscure which edits actually fix bugs. We present BoostAPR, a three-stage framework addressing these challenges: (1) supervised fine-tuning on execution-verified demonstrations with reasoning traces, (2) training dual reward models--a sequence-level assessor and a line-level credit allocator--from execution outcomes, and (3) PPO optimization where the line-level model redistributes rewards to critical edit regions. This line-level credit assignment operates at an intermediate granularity naturally suited to code changes. Trained on SWE-Gym and evaluated on four benchmarks, BoostAPR achieves 40.7% on SWE-bench Verified (+22.9pp over base model), 24.8% on Defects4J (Python-to-Java transfer), 84.5% on HumanEval-Java, and 95.0% on QuixBugs, achieving competitive results among open-source models with strong cross-language generalization.

</details>


### [151] [Do LLMs Experience an Internal Polylogue? Investigating Reasoning through the Lens of Personas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09159)
*Nils A. Herrmann,Leander Girrbach,Kirill Bykov,Zeynep Akata*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "polylogue" — a time-series analysis of how persona vectors (linear directions in LLM activation space encoding behavioral traits) align with hidden activations during generation. These polylogue features predict MMLU-Pro accuracy, are interpretable, and enable stage-aware latent steering that improves accuracy on 3/4 models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work treated persona vectors as static handles for behavioral steering; the authors instead propose treating them as dynamic signals that can be monitored and intervened upon as reasoning unfolds.

Method: The authors define "polylogue" as the time series of alignment scores between persona vectors and hidden activations across generation steps. They test this on four open-weight models using MMLU-Pro, comparing prediction performance against low-dimensional activation baselines, and implement a paragraph-conditioned intervention based on polylogue signals.

Result: Polylogue features predict correctness on MMLU-Pro competitively with low-dimensional baselines while remaining interpretable. The paragraph-conditioned intervention improves accuracy on three of four models.

Conclusion: The polylogue serves as an interpretable tool for reasoning-time monitoring and intervention, with stage-aware latent steering being a promising direction for reasoning-time control.

Abstract: Recent work shows that large language models (LLMs) encode behavioural traits ("personas") as linear directions in activation space, often called "persona vectors". Prior work has used such directions as static handles for behavioural steering. Building on this, we treat them as dynamic signals instead: probes we can monitor and intervene on as reasoning unfolds. We use the term polylogue to denote the time series of alignments between persona vectors and hidden activations over the course of generation. Experiments across four open-weight models show that polylogue features predict correctness on MMLU-Pro competitively with low-dimensional activation baselines, while remaining interpretable through their associated persona directions. They also suggest concrete steering targets, namely which latent directions to modulate at different stages of a response. We instantiate this as a simple paragraph-conditioned intervention that improves accuracy on three of four models, pointing to stage-aware latent steering as a promising direction for reasoning-time control. Together, this positions the polylogue as an interpretable tool for reasoning-time monitoring and intervention.

</details>


### [152] [FORTIS: Benchmarking Over-Privilege in Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09163)
*Shawn Li,Chenxiao Yu,Han Wang,Wei Yang,Ryan Rossi,Franck Dernoncourt,Xiyang Hu,Philip Yu,Chaowei Xiao,Huan Zhang,Yue Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FORTIS is a benchmark evaluating over-privilege in LLM agents' intermediate skill layers, finding that frontier models consistently exceed privilege boundaries by selecting and executing higher-privilege skills/tools than tasks require, especially under realistic user interaction conditions, indicating the skill layer itself is a primary source of privilege escalation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The skill layer in LLM agents is widely treated as an organizational abstraction, but the authors argue it functions as a privilege boundary that current models routinely exceed, motivating the need to systematically evaluate over-privilege behavior.

Method: FORTIS evaluates over-privilege across two stages: (1) whether a model selects the minimally sufficient skill from a large overlapping library, and (2) whether it executes that skill without expanding into broader tools/actions. Tested across ten frontier models and three domains under realistic conditions including incomplete specification, convenience framing, and proximity to skill boundaries.

Result: Over-privileged behavior is the norm; models consistently reach for higher-privilege skills and tools than required, with failure rates remaining high even for the strongest models. Failure is especially severe under ordinary conditions of real user interaction, and none of the test conditions require adversarial construction.

Conclusion: The skill layer, far from containing agent behavior, is itself a primary source of privilege escalation in current systems.

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly operate through an intermediate skill layer that mediates between user intent and concrete task execution. This layer is widely treated as an organizational abstraction, but we argue it is also a privilege boundary that current models routinely exceed. We present \textbf{FORTIS}, a benchmark that evaluates over-privilege in agent skills across two stages: whether a model selects the minimally sufficient skill from a large overlapping library, and whether it executes that skill without expanding into broader tools or actions than the skill permits. Across ten frontier models and three domains, we find that over-privileged behavior is the norm rather than the exception. Models consistently reach for higher-privilege skills and tools than the task requires, failing at both stages at rates that remain high even for the strongest available models. Failure is especially severe under the ordinary conditions of real user interaction: incomplete specification, convenience framing, and proximity to skill boundaries. None of these requires adversarial construction. The results indicate that the skill layer, far from containing agent behavior, is itself a primary source of privilege escalation in current systems.

</details>


### [153] [CIVeX: Causal Intervention Verification for Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09168)
*Fabio Rovai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CIVeX is a causal intervention verifier that maps tool-use actions to structural causal queries, checks identifiability, and returns auditable verdicts (EXECUTE, REJECT, EXPERIMENT, ABSTAIN). It ensures actions have identifiable causal effects, reducing false executions in confounded workflows. On benchmarks, it achieves near-zero false executions, high accuracy (84.9%), and 81.1% of oracle utility, outperforming LLM-based verifiers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool-using language agents have various safeguards (schema validators, policy filters, etc.) but these do not certify that a state-changing action has an identifiable causal effect. In confounded workflows, actions that appear optimal in observational logs may reduce utility when executed, highlighting the need for causal intervention verification.

Method: CIVeX maps proposed actions to structural causal queries over a committed action-state graph, checks identifiability using do-calculus, and returns one of four verdicts: EXECUTE (with causal certificate), REJECT, EXPERIMENT, or ABSTAIN. The causal certificate includes graph commitments, identification argument, one-sided LCB, provenance, and risk limits.

Result: On Causal-ToolBench (1,890 instances, 7 seeds): zero observed false executions under moderate and adversarial confounding; 84.9% accuracy and 81.1% of oracle utility (+2.23 vs +2.76). On IHDP and ZOZO Open Bandit: matches Oracle within 0.1pp, cuts false-execution by >=50x. Outperforms chain-of-thought LLM verifiers (Claude Opus utility falls to 74% of CIVeX's under adversarial confounding).

Conclusion: Intervention identifiability, not action validity, is the missing primitive for reliable tool use. Causal reasoning about whether an action's effect is identifiable is essential for safe and effective execution in tool-using language agents.

Abstract: A valid tool call is not necessarily a valid intervention. Tool-using language agents are guarded by schema validators, policy filters, provenance checks, state predictors, and self-verification, yet such safeguards do not certify that a state-changing action has an identifiable causal effect. In confounded workflows, the action that looks optimal in observational logs can reduce utility when executed.
  We introduce CIVeX, a causal intervention verifier that maps proposed actions to structural causal queries over a committed action-state graph, checks identifiability, and returns one of four auditable verdicts: EXECUTE, REJECT, EXPERIMENT, or ABSTAIN. Execution requires an assumption-scoped causal certificate carrying graph commitments, an identification argument, a one-sided lower confidence bound (LCB), provenance, and risk limits.
  On Causal-ToolBench (1,890 instances, 7 seeds), CIVeX yields zero observed false executions across moderate and adversarial confounding. Under adversarial confounding it reaches 84.9% accuracy and 81.1% of oracle utility (+2.23 vs +2.76) and is the only non-oracle method whose constrained utility under a zero-false-execution constraint exceeds the AlwaysAbstain floor. On IHDP and ZOZO Open Bandit (real production logs with uniform-random ground truth), CIVeX matches Oracle correct-execution within 0.1pp and cuts per-execute false-execution by >=50x over naive baselines. A chain-of-thought LLM verifier (Claude Opus, Sonnet) cuts false-execution by an order of magnitude over a terse baseline, yet under adversarial confounding Opus's utility falls to 74% of CIVeX's. Intervention identifiability, not action validity, is the missing primitive for reliable tool use.

</details>


### [154] [Open Ontologies: Tool-Augmented Ontology Engineering with Stable Matching Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09184)
*Fabio Rovai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Open Ontologies is an open-source ontology engineering system in Rust combining LLM-driven construction with OWL reasoning and ontology alignment via MCP. Stable 1-to-1 matching is the dominant factor for alignment quality (F1=0.832 on OAEI Anatomy, P=0.963), with signal weights being irrelevant under stable matching. Structured MCP tool access (F1=0.717) significantly outperforms raw OWL file reading (F1=0.323) for LLM ontology interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop an open-source ontology engineering system that integrates LLM-driven construction with formal OWL reasoning and ontology alignment, and to investigate the factors affecting alignment quality and LLM interaction with ontologies.

Method: Implemented in Rust; integrates LLM-driven construction with formal OWL reasoning and ontology alignment via Model Context Protocol (MCP). Uses stable 1-to-1 matching for alignment.
