Evaluated on OAEI Anatomy and Conference tracks. Ablation across five weight configurations. Compared LLM interaction modes: raw OWL file reading vs. no file vs. structured MCP tool access.

Result: OAEI Anatomy track: F1=0.832 (P=0.963, R=0.733). Ablation shows stable matching dominates; removing it drops F1 to 0.728 while weight variations cause <0.004 F1 change. Conference track: F1=0.438. LLM with raw OWL file: F1=0.323; LLM with no file: F1=0.431; structured MCP tool access: F1=0.717.

Conclusion: Stable 1-to-1 matching is the dominant factor for ontology alignment quality, rendering signal weights irrelevant. Structured MCP tool access provides a qualitatively different mode of ontology interaction for LLMs that raw syntax reading cannot replicate. The system is released as a single MIT-licensed binary.

Abstract: We present Open Ontologies, an open-source ontology engineering system implemented in Rust that integrates LLM-driven construction with formal OWL reasoning and ontology alignment via the Model Context Protocol. Our primary finding is that stable 1-to-1 matching is the dominant factor in ontology alignment quality: on the OAEI Anatomy track, it achieves F1 = 0.832 (P = 0.963, R = 0.733), competitive with state-of-the-art systems and exceeding all in precision. Ablation across five weight configurations shows that signal weights are irrelevant when stable matching is applied (F1 varies by less than 0.004), while removing stable matching drops F1 to 0.728. On the Conference track, the same method achieves F1 = 0.438. On tool-augmented ontology interaction, we find a surprising result: an LLM reading a raw OWL file (F1 = 0.323) performs worse than the same LLM with no file at all (F1 = 0.431), while structured MCP tool access achieves F1 = 0.717. This demonstrates that tool structure provides a qualitatively different mode of access that the LLM cannot replicate by reading raw syntax. The system ships as a single binary under the MIT licence.

</details>


### [155] [Agentic MIP Research: Accelerated Constraint Handler Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09186)
*Liding Xu,Yugeng Zhou,Sebastian Pokutta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an LLM-agent-based framework that automates the MIP research loop by generating, verifying, and evaluating propagation-based constraint handlers for the SCIP solver, successfully recovering global constraints, discovering novel propagation strategies, and solving five additional MIPLIB 2017 instances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MIP research requires significant engineering effort (implementation, debugging, tuning, benchmarking) to test algorithmic hypotheses within branch-and-cut solvers. The paper aims to shorten this feedback loop by automating the process of generating and evaluating solver plugins.

Method: The framework embeds LLM agents into a solver-aware harness for the open-source solver SCIP. It focuses on semantic lifting of MIP formulations into global constraints and automatic construction of propagation-only SCIP constraint handlers. The framework supports in-context learning within a sandboxed environment for tuning, debugging, and discovering novel propagation strategies.

Result: On MIPLIB 2017, the framework successfully recovered global constraint structures from constraint programming, generated executable constraint detectors and propagation-only handlers. The novel propagation methods solved five additional instances within the benchmark.

Conclusion: LLM agents can autonomously navigate the complex MIP research loop, enabling systematic distinction between meaningful algorithmic improvements and low-value candidates, and paving the way for more automated solver development.

Abstract: Mixed-integer programming (MIP) research is both mathematically sophisticated and engineering-intensive: testing an algorithmic hypothesis within a branch-and-cut solver requires substantial implementation, debugging, tuning, and large-scale benchmarking. We propose an agentic MIP research framework that shortens this feedback loop by embedding LLM agents into a solver-aware harness for generating, verifying, and evaluating plugins for the open-source solver SCIP. Propagation methods play a central role in accelerating MIP solving by exploiting global constraints. We instantiate our framework on the semantic lifting of MIP formulations into global constraints and the automatic construction of propagation-only SCIP constraint handlers. On the MIPLIB 2017 benchmark set, the framework successfully recovers global constraint structures from constraint programming and generates executable constraint detectors and propagation-only constraint handlers. Furthermore, the framework naturally extends to in-context learning within a sandboxed environment, enabling agents not only to tune and debug generated constraint handlers on real instances, but also to explore global constraint patterns in MIP problems and discover novel propagation strategies not yet implemented in SCIP. This framework allows us to systematically distinguish meaningful algorithmic improvements from low-value or overly costly candidates: the novel propagation methods successfully solved five additional instances within the explored benchmark. Overall, this framework demonstrates that LLM agents can autonomously navigate the complex MIP research loop, paving the way for a more automated solver development process.

</details>


### [156] [Emergent Semantic Role Understanding in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09187)
*Carla Griffiths,Mirco Musolesi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Study investigates whether semantic role understanding emerges during language model pre-training or requires task-specific fine-tuning, finding that frozen representations contain substantial role information but don't fully match fine-tuned models, indicating partial emergence from pre-training alone with distributed representations growing with scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether semantic role understanding ("who did what to whom")—a core component of meaning representation—emerges from pre-training alone or depends on task-specific fine-tuning, and to interpret how linguistic structure emerges in language models.

Method: Freeze decoder-only transformers, train linear probes to extract semantic roles, and compare performance across model scales to infer whether role information is encoded in pre-training or learned during adaptation.

Result: Frozen representations contain substantial semantic role information; performance improves but does not fully match fine-tuned models. Semantic role structure emerges from language modeling objectives, but its internal implementation shifts toward more distributed representations as model scale increases.

Conclusion: Semantic role understanding partially but incompletely emerges from pre-training alone, with distributed representation shifting at larger scales.

Abstract: Understanding how linguistic structure emerges in language models is central to interpreting what these systems learn from data and how much supervision they truly require. In particular, semantic role understanding ("who did what to whom") is a core component of meaning representation, yet it remains unclear whether it arises from pre-training alone or depends on task-specific fine-tuning. We study whether semantic role understanding emerges during language model pre-training or requires task-specific fine-tuning. We freeze decoder-only transformers and train linear probes to extract semantic roles, using performance to infer whether role information is already encoded in pre-training or learned during adaptation. Across model scales, we find that frozen representations contain substantial semantic role information, with performance improving but not fully matching fine-tuned models. This indicates partial but incomplete emergence from pre-training alone. We show that semantic role structure emerges from language modeling objectives, but its internal implementation shifts toward more distributed representations as model scale increases.

</details>


### [157] [Evidence Over Plans: Online Trajectory Verification for Skill Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09192)
*Yang Zhou,Zihan Dong,Zhenting Wang,Can Jin,Shiyu Zhao,Bangwei Guo,Difei Gu,Linjun Zhang,Mu Zhou,Dimitris N. Metaxas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SPARK introduces the Posterior Distillation Index (PDI) to guide skill generation from environment-verified trajectories, producing efficient and transferable skills that outperform human-written ones at much lower inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill generation methods rely on preference logs rather than direct environment interaction, often yielding negligible or degraded gains. The key bottleneck is that robust skills should be posterior-based, distilled from empirical interaction rather than prior plans.

Method: SPARK (Structured Pipelines for Autonomous Runnable tasKs and sKill generation) generates environment-verified trajectories, computes PDI (Posterior Distillation Index) as a trajectory-level metric to quantify skill grounding, and uses PDI as an online diagnostic and intervention signal for posterior skill formation.

Result: Across 86 runnable tasks, SPARK-generated skills consistently surpass no-skill baselines and outperform human-written skills on student models, achieving up to 1,000x cheaper inference cost than teacher models.

Conclusion: PDI-guided distillation produces efficient and transferable skills grounded in task-environment interaction, validating that posterior-based skills from empirical evidence are superior to prior-based plans.

Abstract: Agent skills can remarkably improve task success rates by using human-written procedural documents, but their quality is difficult to assess without environment-grounded verification. Existing skill generation methods heavily rely on preference logs rather than direct environment interaction, often yielding negligible or even degraded gains. We identify that it is a fundamental timing bottleneck: robust skills should be posterior-based, distilled from empirical environment interaction rather than prior plans. In this study, we introduce the Posterior Distillation Index (PDI), a trajectory-level metric that quantifies how well a distilled skill is grounded in the task-environment evidence. To operationalize PDI, we present SPARK (Structured Pipelines for Autonomous Runnable tasKs and sKill generation) for preserving task execution evidence towards full trajectory-level analysis. SPARK generates environment-verified trajectories used to compute PDI, and it applies PDI as an online diagnostic and intervention signal to ensure posterior skill formation. Across 86 runnable tasks, SPARK-generated skills consistently surpass no-skill baselines and outperform human-written skills on student models (inference cost up to 1,000x cheaper than teacher models). These findings show that PDI-guided distillation produces efficient and transferable skills grounded in the task-environment interaction. We release our code at https://github.com/EtaYang10th/spark-skills .

</details>


### [158] [The Geometry of Forgetting: Temporal Knowledge Drift as an Independent Axis in LLM Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09195)
*Rania Elbadry,Ahmed Heakl,Fan Zhang,Dani Bouch,Yuxia Wang,Preslav Nakov,Zhuohan Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that temporal drift (outdated facts in LLMs) is encoded as a direction in the residual stream that is geometrically orthogonal to both correctness and uncertainty, making existing detection methods fundamentally incapable of identifying stale answers. Linear probes on drift labels achieve high AUROC (0.83-0.95), while entropy-based and other methods remain near chance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models confidently produce outdated answers, and no existing method can detect them. The authors want to show this is a structural failure, not an engineering one.

Method: The authors verify across six instruction-tuned models that temporal drift is encoded orthogonally to correctness and uncertainty in the residual stream. They use linear probes on drift labels, compare with token entropy, semantic entropy, CCS, SAPLMA, and perform five geometric orthogonality tests (weight cosines, score correlations, null-space projections, iterative null-space projection, difference-of-means dissociation). They also analyze MLP retrieval circuit dynamics and perform cross-cutoff experiments.

Result: Linear probes achieve AUROC 0.83-0.95; existing methods (token entropy, semantic entropy, CCS, SAPLMA) remain near chance (0.49-0.57). Orthogonality confirmed: |cos|≤0.14, |r|≤0.20, |Δ|≤0.008. MLP retrieval circuit dynamics for stale recall and confabulation are nearly identical (r>0.81). Cross-cutoff experiments show probe fires only on models trained before fact transition (P(A>B)=0.975-0.998).

Conclusion: Temporal drift is structurally orthogonal to correctness and uncertainty, explaining why existing methods cannot detect it. A linear probe on drift labels works effectively, and the cross-cutoff experiment confirms the probe reads model-internal knowledge state rather than input properties.

Abstract: Large language models confidently produce outdated answers, and no existing method can detect them. We show this is not an engineering failure but a structural one: temporal drift, whether a stored fact has changed since training, is encoded as a direction in the residual stream geometrically orthogonal to both correctness and uncertainty. Any method operating on correctness or uncertainty signals is therefore blind to drift by construction. We verify this across six instruction-tuned models. A linear probe trained directly on drift labels achieves AUROC $0.83$--$0.95$; methods based on token entropy, semantic entropy, CCS, and SAPLMA all remain near chance ($0.49$--$0.57$). Five tests confirm the geometric orthogonality: weight cosines ($|\cos| \leq 0.14$), score correlations ($|r| \leq 0.20$), bidirectional null-space projection ($|Δ| \leq 0.008$), iterative null-space projection with $k{=}10$, and difference-of-means dissociation. Mechanistically, the MLP retrieval circuit produces identical dynamics for stale recall and confabulation ($r > 0.81$, six models), explaining why output confidence cannot separate them. A cross-cutoff experiment holds inputs constant and varies only the model: the probe fires on the model whose training predates the fact's transition and stays silent otherwise ($P(A{>}B) = 0.975$--$0.998$, twelve model pairs), confirming it reads model-internal knowledge state rather than input properties. Our code and datasets will be publicly released.

</details>


### [159] [Learning the Preferences of a Learning Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09217)
*Karim Abdel Sadek,Mark Bedaywi,Rhys Gould,Stuart Russell*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes the problem of learning preferences from a learning (not necessarily optimal) agent, establishing theoretical guarantees for preference learning algorithms when the learner uses no-regret or Boltzmann policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard IRL assumes humans are approximately optimal, which fails when the human is also learning to act optimally. The authors aim to infer preferences from a learning agent that may behave suboptimally initially.

Method: The learner is modeled as either no-regret or converging to an optimal Boltzmann policy over time. Theoretical guarantees for various preference learning algorithms are established under these settings, with impossibility results shown where guarantees cannot exist.

Result: Theoretical guarantees (or impossibility results) for preference learning algorithms when the observed agent is learning rather than optimal.

Conclusion: Preference learning from learning agents is formally tractable under certain assumptions (no-regret, Boltzmann convergence), with clear boundaries on which algorithmic guarantees are achievable.

Abstract: For AI systems to be useful to humans, they must understand and act in accordance with our values and preferences. Since specifying preferences is a hard task, inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) aims to develop methods that allow for inferring preferences from observed behavior. However, IRL assumes the human to be approximately optimal. This is a big limitation in cases where the human themselves may be learning to act optimally in an environment. In this paper, we formalize the problem of learning the preferences of a learning agent: a predictor observes a learner acting online and tries to infer the underlying reward function being (initially suboptimally) optimized by the learner. We model the learner as either being no-regret, or as converging to an optimal Boltzmann policy over time. In each of these settings, we establish theoretical guarantees for various preference learning algorithms, or otherwise show that such guarantees are impossible.

</details>


### [160] [How Much is Brain Data Worth for Machine Learning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09243)
*Lane Lewis,Zhixin Wang,David Schwab,Xaq Pitkow*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper develops a theoretical framework using linear Gaussian models to derive scaling laws for how neural recordings improve model training, quantifying when and how much brain data is valuable relative to task labels for machine learning performance and robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand theoretically when and why supplementing task training with neural recordings improves model performance and robustness, and to quantify the conditions under which brain data is worth collecting.

Method: Mathematical formulation using a linear Gaussian model of task targets and neural recordings; derivation of scaling laws for multimodal estimators trained on both brain data and task labels; analysis of sample exchange rates, task-brain alignment, noise, latent dimension, and test distribution shift.

Result: Derived scaling laws relating performance to numbers of brain and task samples; quantified exchange rates between brain and task samples as functions of task-brain alignment, noise, latent dimension, and sample size; identified regimes where brain data provides substantial robustness gains under distribution shift and where collecting brain data is worth the budget.

Conclusion: Provides a theoretical foundation for understanding the value of neural data in improving machine learning, establishing conditions under which brain-regularized learning yields meaningful benefits.

Abstract: If a person can solve a task, can measuring their brain make it easier to train a model to solve that task too? Recent NeuroAI work suggests that supplementing task training with neural recordings can modestly improve model performance and robustness. However, it is unclear when there should be a benefit from using neural data and how much benefit to expect. We formulate this question mathematically, and begin to address it theoretically using a simple, analytically tractable linear gaussian model of task targets and neural recordings. For a multimodal estimator trained on both brain data and task labels, we derive scaling laws for how performance scales with the numbers of brain and task samples. From these laws we derive relative value and exchange rates between brain samples and task samples, quantifying how much extra task samples neural data is worth as a function of task-brain alignment, neural and task noise, latent dimension, and brain data sample size. We also analyze test distribution shift, to identify conditions where brain-regularized learning can produce substantial robustness gains through learned invariances. Finally, under a fixed collection budget, we characterize the regimes in which brain data is worth collecting. Our results provide a foundation for understanding how valuable brain data could be for improving machine learning.

</details>


### [161] [SeePhys Pro: Diagnosing Modality Transfer and Blind-Training Effects in Multimodal RLVR for Physics Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09266)
*Kun Xiang,Terry Jingchen Zhang,Zirong Liu,Bokai Zhou,Yueling Tang,Junjie Yu,Jiacong Lu,Shangrui Huang,Heng Li,Likui Zhang,Kunkun Liu,Changzheng Zhang,Yangle Fang,Boqiang Guo,Hui-Ling Zhen,Dandan Tu,Yinya Huang,Xiaodan Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SeePhys Pro is a benchmark for testing modality-invariant reasoning by progressively transferring information from text to image. Current models struggle with visual grounding, and RL training can show spurious gains from non-visual cues rather than genuine visual reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether models preserve reasoning capability when information is progressively transferred from text to image, and to expose the fragility of current multimodal models under modality transfer.

Method: SeePhys Pro benchmark with four semantically aligned variants per problem (text→image). Multimodal RLVR training with blind training (masked images) as diagnostic control. Text-deletion, image-mask-rate, and format-saturation controls for analysis.

Result: Frontier models show performance degradation as information moves from language to diagrams; visual variable grounding is the key bottleneck. RL with masked images still improves unmasked validation performance, but gains stem from residual textual and distributional cues, not valid visual evidence.

Conclusion: Multimodal reasoning evaluation should go beyond final-answer accuracy to include robustness under modality transfer and diagnostics verifying that improvements rely on task-critical visual evidence.

Abstract: We introduce SeePhys Pro, a fine-grained modality transfer benchmark that studies whether models preserve the same reasoning capability when critical information is progressively transferred from text to image. Unlike standard vision-essential benchmarks that evaluate a single input form, SeePhys Pro features four semantically aligned variants for each problem with progressively increasing visual elements. Our evaluation shows that current frontier models are far from representation-invariant reasoners: performance degrades on average as information moves from language to diagrams, with visual variable grounding as the most critical bottleneck. Motivated by this inference-time fragility, we further develop large training corpora for multimodal RLVR and use blind training as a diagnostic control, finding that RL with all training images masked can still improve performance on unmasked validation sets. To analyze this effect, text-deletion, image-mask-rate, and format-saturation controls suggest that such gains can arise from residual textual and distributional cues rather than valid visual evidence. Our results highlight the need to evaluate multimodal reasoning not only by final-answer accuracy, but also by robustness under modality transfer and by diagnostics that test whether improvements rely on task-critical visual evidence.

</details>


### [162] [Shaping Schema via Language Representation as the Next Frontier for LLM Intelligence Expanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09271)
*Zhiqin Yang,Yuhan Liu,Jingwen Fu,Pei Fu anf Bo Han,Masashi Sugiyama,Nanning Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that language representation design—how tasks are linguistically and symbolically structured—significantly impacts LLM performance, and that advancing beyond scaling requires deliberate schema shaping through sophisticated language representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural language's limited expressive capacity creates a bottleneck for complex problem-solving in LLMs; merely scaling up internalized knowledge does not guarantee its effective application.

Method: The authors formalize the claim that schema (knowledge activation and organization) depends on language representation sophistication, then provide empirical evidence through (1) review of existing practices showing performance gains from representation design without parameter changes, and (2) controlled experiments demonstrating LLM performance and internal feature activations vary under different language representations of the same task.

Result: Deliberate language representation design yields substantial performance gains even without modifying model parameters or scale; LLM performance and internal feature activations differ across various language representations of identical underlying tasks.

Conclusion: Language representation design is a promising frontier for expanding LLM intelligence beyond scaling, and shaping schemas through advanced language representation should be a focus of future research.

Abstract: Although natural language is the default medium for Large Language Models (LLMs), its limited expressive capacity creates a profound bottleneck for complex problem-solving. While recent advancements in AI have relied heavily on scaling, merely internalizing knowledge does not guarantee its effective application. Defining language representation as the linguistic and symbolic constructs used to map and model the real world, this paper argues that shaping schemas through advanced language representation is the next frontier for expanding LLM intelligence. We posit that an LLM's knowledge activation and organization -- its schema -- depends heavily on the structural and symbolic sophistication of the language used to represent a given task. This paper contributes both a formalization of this claim and the empirical evidence to support it. With a new formalization, we present multiple lines of evidence to support our position: Firstly, we review recent empirical practices and emerging methodologies that demonstrate the substantial performance gains achievable through deliberate language representation design, even without modifying model parameters or scale. Secondly, we conduct controlled experiments showing that LLM performance and its internal feature activations vary under different language representations of the same underlying task. Together, these findings highlight language representation design as a promising direction for future research.

</details>


### [163] [Towards Conversational Medical AI with Eyes, Ears and a Voice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09272)
*Meet Shah,Jason Gusdorf,Anil Palepu,Chunjong Park,Jack W. O'Sullivan,Vishnu Ravi,Tim Strother,Pavel Dubov,Aliya Rysbek,Toshiyuki Fukuzawa,Yana Lunts,Jan Freyberg,Michael B. Chang,Aniruddh Raghu,David Stutz,Devora Berlowitz,Eliseo Papa,Taylan Cemgil,JD Velasquez,Jack Chen,Arthur Chen,Doug Fritz,Charlie Taylor,Katya Tregubova,Jing Rong Lim,Richard Green,Sara Mahdavi,Mahvish Nagda,Jihyeon Lee,Craig Schiff,Liviu Panait,Sukhdeep Singh,Valentin Liévin,David G. T. Barrett,Hannah Gladman,Anna Cupani,Francesca Pietra,Uchechi Okereke,Katherine Tong,Clemens Meyer,Erwan Rolland,Mili Sanwalka,Michael D. Howell,Shixiang Shane Gu,Bibo Xu,Euan A. Ashley,S. M. Ali Eslami,Gregory Wayne,Pushmeet Kohli,Vivek Natarajan,Adam Rodman,Alan Karthikesalingam,Ryutaro Tanno*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AI co-clinician, a real-time conversational AI system that uses continuous audio-visual data from live patient consultations to support clinical decisions. It employs a dual-agent architecture balancing clinical reasoning and low latency. In a simulated telemedicine study (120 encounters), the system approached primary care physicians in key metrics like management plans and differential diagnosis, significantly outperformed GPT-Realtime, but fell short in physical examination and disease-specific reasoning. The authors advocate for collaborative triadic AI models rather than autonomous diagnostic systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical practice depends on rich auditory and visual cues during doctor-patient dialogue, which text-only AI systems fail to capture. The authors aim to bridge this gap by building a real-time multimodal AI system that processes continuous audio-visual streams from live conversations to inform clinical decision-making.

Method: The authors developed a dual-agent conversational AI system built on Gemini's low-latency voice/video processing. They conducted a randomized, interface-blinded, crossover simulation study (n=120 encounters) with 10 internal medicine residents as patient actors, comparing AI co-clinician against primary care physicians, GPT-Realtime, and a baseline agent across 20 standardized outpatient scenarios using TelePACES evaluation criteria.

Result: AI co-clinician approached PCP performance in management plans and differential diagnosis, significantly outperformed GPT-Realtime across all general criteria, and showed parity with PCPs in case-specific triage measures. However, physicians maintained superior overall performance in case-specific assessments, and gaps remained in physical examination and disease-specific reasoning.

Conclusion: Text-only AI approaches are insufficient for medical consultation. Real-time multimodal diagnostic AI is most safely advanced in collaborative triadic models where AI serves as a supportive co-clinician alongside doctors and patients, rather than as an autonomous decision-maker.

Abstract: The practice of medicine relies not only upon skillful dialogue but also on the nuanced exchange and interpretation of rich auditory and visual cues between doctors and patients. Building on the low-latency voice and video processing capabilities of Gemini, we introduce AI co-clinician, a first-of-its-kind conversational AI system utilizing continuous streams of audio-visual data from live patient conversations to inform real-time clinical decisions. Its dual-agent architecture balances deep clinical reasoning with the low latency required for natural dialogue. To assess this system, we implemented a video-based interface emulating telemedicine consultations. We crafted 20 standardized outpatient scenarios requiring proactive real-time auditory and visual reasoning and designed "TelePACES" evaluation criteria alongside case-specific rubrics. In a randomized, interface-blinded, crossover simulation study (n = 120 encounters) with 10 internal medicine residents as patient actors, we compared AI co-clinician with primary care physicians (PCPs), GPT-Realtime, and a baseline agent. AI co-clinician approached PCPs in key TelePACES dimensions, including management plans and differential diagnosis, while significantly outperforming GPT-Realtime across all general criteria. While our agent demonstrated parity with PCPs in case-specific triage measures, physicians maintained superior overall performance in case-specific assessments. Although AI co-clinician marks a significant advance in real-time telemedical AI, gaps remain in physical examination and disease-specific reasoning. Our work shows that text-only approaches fail to capture the true challenges of medical consultation and suggests that high-stakes real-time diagnostic AI is most safely advanced in collaborative, triadic models where AI can be a supportive co-clinician for doctors and patients.

</details>


### [164] [EquiMem: Calibrating Shared Memory in Multi-Agent Debate via Game-Theoretic Equilibrium](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09278)
*Yuqiao Meng,Sakshi Sunil Narvekar,Luoxi Tang,Rupali Rajendra Vaje,Yingxue Zhang,Muchao Ye,Zhaohan Xi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EquiMem is a zero-trust inference-time calibration mechanism for multi-agent debate systems that detects corrupted shared memory entries by formulating memory updates as a game-theoretic equilibrium, using existing agent retrieval queries as evidence without relying on fallible LLM judgments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent debate systems using shared memory are vulnerable to single corrupted entries contaminating reasoning, and existing safeguards (heuristics or LLM-based validation) fail because they share the same failure modes and ignore cross-agent dynamics.

Method: Formulates memory updating as a zero-trust memory game where no agent is assumed honest; uses the game's equilibrium as an indicator of optimal memory trust. EquiMem quantifies each update algorithmically against shared memory state using agents' existing retrieval queries and traversal paths as evidence, without soliciting LLM judgments.

Result: Outperforms existing safeguards across diverse benchmarks, MAD frameworks, and memory architectures; remains robust under adversarial agents; incurs negligible inference overhead.

Conclusion: EquiMem provides a principled, lightweight defense against memory contamination in multi-agent debate systems by leveraging game-theoretic equilibrium and existing agent behavior rather than unreliable AI-based validation.

Abstract: Multi-agent debate (MAD) systems increasingly rely on shared memory to support long-horizon reasoning, but this convenience opens a critical vulnerability: a single corrupted entry can contaminate the downstream memory-augmented reasoning, and debate alone fails to filter such errors. Existing safeguards filter entries via heuristics or LLM-based validation, yet they rely on AI judgments that share the same failure modes and overlook the cross-agent dynamics of MAD. We address this gap by formulating memory updating in MAD as a zero-trust memory game, in which no agent is assumed honest and the game's equilibrium serves as an indicator of optimal memory trust. Guided by this equilibrium, we propose EquiMem, an inference-time calibration mechanism that quantifies each update algorithmically against the shared memory state, using agents' existing retrieval queries and traversal paths as evidence rather than soliciting any LLM judgment. EquiMem instantiates calibration for both embedding- and graph-based memory, and across diverse benchmarks, MAD frameworks, and memory architectures, it consistently outperforms existing safeguards, remains robust under adversarial agents, and incurs negligible inference overhead.

</details>


### [165] [A Prompt-Aware Structuring Framework for Reliable Reuse of AI-Generated Content in the Agentic Web](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09283)
*Shusaku Egami,Masahiro Hamasaki*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a framework that attaches structured metadata (prompts, contexts, model info, confidence, etc.) with verifiable credentials to AI-Generated Content at generation time, enabling reliable assessment, reuse, and safe application of AIGC in an Agentic Web era.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current lack of mechanisms for AI agents to verify reliability, reproducibility, or license compliance of AI-Generated Content (AIGC) risks chained hallucinations and compliance violations when AIGC is reused.

Method: Automatically attaches structured metadata (modularized prompts, contexts, thoughts, model information, hyperparameters, confidence) to AIGC at generation time, enveloped with verifiable credentials.

Result: Enables efficient curation of structured AIGC and facilitates safe reuse for applications such as fine-tuning and knowledge distillation.

Conclusion: A metadata management framework for AIGC provenance is essential for the transition to an Agentic Web, and the proposed approach addresses this need.

Abstract: The evolution of Large Language Models (LLMs) and the software agents built on them (AI agents) marks a turning point in the transition from a human-centric Web to an ``Agentic Web'' driven by AI agents. However, for AI-Generated Content (AIGC), which is expected to dominate the Web, there is currently no mechanism for agents to verify its reliability, reproducibility, or license compliance during generation. This lack of transparency risks causing chained hallucinations and compliance violations through the reuse of AIGC. Consequently, a framework to manage the provenance and generation conditions of AIGC is essential. In this paper, we present a framework that automatically attaches structured metadata to AIGC at generation time, including modularized prompts, contexts, thoughts, model information, hyperparameters, and confidence. The metadata is enveloped together with verifiable credentials to support the reliable assessment and reuse of AIGC. This framework enables efficient curation of structured AIGC and facilitates its safe use for applications such as fine-tuning and knowledge distillation.

</details>


### [166] [PiCA: Pivot-Based Credit Assignment for Search Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09287)
*Dongyi Liu,Yifan Niu,Qinwen Wang,Han Xiao,Jia Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PiCA is a novel step reward mechanism for LLM-based search agents that addresses reward sparsity, isolated credit assignment, and distributional shift by using Potential-Based Reward Shaping to define process rewards as success probabilities dependent on historical context, identifying pivot steps (golden sub-queries and sub-answers) as information peaks to provide dense, trajectory-aware guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL-trained LLM search agents suffer from three critical challenges in long-horizon credit assignment: Reward Sparsity (only outcome-level feedback), Isolated Credit (step rewards assigned independently without capturing sequential dependencies), and Distributional Shift (rewards estimated on templates deviating from the model's natural generative distribution).

Method: PiCA reformulates search trajectories as sequential cumulative progress, using Potential-Based Reward Shaping (PBRS) to define process rewards as success probabilities conditioned on historical context. It identifies "pivot steps" (golden sub-queries and sub-answers from historical trajectories) as information peaks that boost final answer likelihood, providing dense, pivot-aware, trajectory-dependent step rewards while maintaining distributional consistency.

Result: PiCA outperforms existing strong baselines across seven knowledge-intensive QA benchmarks, achieving 15.2% and 2.2% improvements for 3B and 7B models respectively, with consistent performance gains across various models demonstrating robust generalization.

Conclusion: PiCA effectively addresses the credit assignment challenges in LLM-based search agents through pivot-based, trajectory-aware step rewards, achieving superior performance and generalization across multiple QA benchmarks.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM)-based search agents trained with reinforcement learning (RL) have significantly improved the performance of knowledge-intensive tasks. However, existing methods encounter critical challenges in long-horizon credit assignment: (i) Reward Sparsity, where models receive only outcome feedback without step-level guidance to differentiate action quality; (ii) Isolated Credit, where credit is assigned to steps independently, failing to capture sequential dependencies; and (iii) Distributional Shift, where rewards are estimated on templates that deviate from the model's natural generative distribution. To address these issues, we propose Pivot-Based Credit Assignment (PiCA), a novel step reward mechanism that reformulates the search trajectory as a sequential process of cumulative search progress. Unlike prior isolated step rewards, PiCA defines process rewards as success probabilities dependent on the historical context based on Potential-Based Reward Shaping (PBRS). This approach identifies pivot steps, which comprise target golden sub-queries and sub-answers derived from historical trajectories, as information peaks that significantly boost the likelihood of a correct final answer. By anchoring these step rewards to the final task objective, PiCA provides dense, pivot-aware and trajectory-dependent guidance while maintaining distributional consistency. Extensive experiments show that PiCA outperforms existing strong baselines across seven knowledge-intensive QA benchmarks, achieving 15.2% and 2.2% improvements for 3B and 7B models. The consistent performance gains across various models show PiCA's robust generalization. The code is available at https://github.com/novdream/PiCA.

</details>


### [167] [Beyond Accuracy: Evaluating Strategy Diversity in LLM Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09292)
*Xia Yang,Xuanyi Zhang,Hao Hu,Feng Ji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs achieve high accuracy (95-100%) on math benchmarks but show limited reasoning flexibility: they recover fewer valid strategies than humans (e.g., best model gets 184 vs. 217 human strategies), with largest gaps in Geometry and Number Theory, and only 71% coverage after three runs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accuracy alone on mathematical reasoning benchmarks does not capture reasoning flexibility, so a strategy-level evaluation framework is needed to assess how models reason beyond just final answers.

Method: Introduced a strategy-level evaluation framework on 80 AMC 10/12 and AIME problems with 217 AoPS-derived reference strategy families; used dual-AI coding with human adjudication to annotate model outputs for strategy identity, validity, and correctness; evaluated four frontier models under single-solution and multiple-strategy prompts, plus a repeated-run robustness check on 20 problems.

Result: All models achieved 95-100% accuracy under single-solution prompt but recovered substantially fewer strategies than humans under multiple-strategy prompt: Gemini (184), DeepSeek (152), GPT (151), Claude (110). Largest gaps in Geometry and Number Theory. Models produced 50 benchmark-novel valid strategies. Best model recovered only 39/55 (71%) human strategies after three runs.

Conclusion: Strategy diversity is a complementary dimension for evaluating mathematical reasoning beyond answer correctness, as there is a pronounced decoupling between answer accuracy and reasoning flexibility in current LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models now achieve high final-answer accuracy on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, but accuracy alone does not capture reasoning flexibility. We introduce a strategy-level evaluation framework instantiated on 80 AMC 10/12 and AIME problems with 217 AoPS-derived reference strategy families. Model outputs are annotated for strategy identity, validity, and correctness using dual-AI coding with human adjudication. Across four frontier models, we find a pronounced decoupling between answer accuracy and strategy diversity. Under a single-solution prompt, all models achieve high accuracy (95%-100%), but under a multiple-strategy prompt they recover substantially fewer strategies than the human reference set. Gemini, DeepSeek, GPT, and Claude generate 184, 152, 151, and 110 distinct valid strategies, respectively, with the largest gaps in Geometry and Number Theory. The models collectively produce 50 benchmark-novel valid strategies, indicating both incomplete coverage of human strategies and some capacity for alternative reasoning. A repeated-run robustness check on 20 problems shows diminishing gains in discovered strategies, with the strongest model recovering only 39 of 55 AoPS-reference strategies (71%) after three runs. These findings position strategy diversity as a complementary dimension for evaluating mathematical reasoning beyond answer correctness.

</details>


### [168] [Beyond ESG Scores: Learning Dynamic Constraints for Sequential Portfolio Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09310)
*Xin Li,Yan Ke,Longbing Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes MACF/MACF-X, a method for ESG-constrained portfolio optimization that learns mechanism-specific ESG costs from multimodal evidence and converts them into optimizer-native constraints, outperforming static ESG-score approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing learning-based methods treat ESG as static scores appended to observations/rewards, which misaligns with the sequential, noisy, low-frequency nature of ESG data; financial evidence suggests ESG is better modeled as a preference/risk dimension than a robust alpha factor.

Method: Introduces Multimodal Action-Conditioned Constraint Field (MACF) that learns ESG costs from point-in-time multimodal evidence and portfolio transitions, plus MACF-X adapters that convert costs/uncertainties into native constrained-optimization interfaces via a slack- and uncertainty-aware pressure layer.

Result: MACF-X reduces tail ESG budget pressure while maintaining competitive financial performance; ablations show improvement depends on dynamic evidence inputs and three-head decomposition, while static ESG-score proxies are nearly indistinguishable from noise baselines.

Conclusion: Treating ESG via dynamic, mechanism-specific constraints learned from multimodal evidence is superior to appending static ESG scores; the approach enables ESG-aware portfolio optimization without modifying financial policy observation or reward.

Abstract: ESG-aware portfolio optimization is increasingly important for sustainable capital allocation, yet most learning-based methods still operationalize ESG by appending static scores to the policy observation or reward. This creates a mismatch for sequential control: ESG scores are noisy, provider-dependent, low-frequency, and temporally misaligned with sequential portfolio decisions, while financial evidence suggests that ESG is better treated as a portfolio preference, risk-exposure, or hedge dimension than as a robust alpha factor. We propose to impose ESG constraints without modifying the financial policy's observation or reward, using a Multimodal Action-Conditioned Constraint Field (MACF) that learns mechanism-specific ESG costs from point-in-time multimodal evidence and contemplated portfolio transitions. We then introduce MACF-X, a family of optimizer-specific adapters that converts MACF costs and uncertainties into native constrained-optimization interfaces through a shared slack- and uncertainty-aware pressure layer. Across multiple constraint-integration interfaces, MACF-X reduces tail ESG budget pressure while maintaining competitive financial performance. Ablations show that this improvement depends on dynamic evidence inputs and three-head decomposition, while static ESG-score proxies are nearly indistinguishable from score-shuffled noise baselines.

</details>


### [169] [How LLMs Are Persuaded: A Few Attention Heads, Rerouted](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09314)
*Xiangkun Sun,Lingkai Kong,Aoqi Zhang,Liang Zeng,Tonghan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Persuasion-induced factual errors in LLMs are caused by a compact circuit: a small set of mid-layer attention heads that copy answer options via a rank-one evidence-routing feature. Persuasion redirects attention to cause a discrete latent jump from the correct to the target answer, and this mechanism is monitorable and removable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the internal mechanism of why language models abandon factual knowledge under persuasion, which is critical for AI safety but remains poorly understood.

Method: Causal intervention analysis identifying a small set of mid-layer attention heads that determine model answers; discovery of a low-dimensional polyhedron structure with answer options at distinct vertices; isolation of a rank-one evidence-routing feature that controls attention routing; tracing this feature back to shallower attention heads that build it from persuasive keywords.

Result: Persuasion causes a discrete latent jump (not gradual blurring) from the correct-answer vertex to the persuasion-target vertex. Decision heads copy option tokens selected by attention rather than reasoning over evidence. A rank-one evidence-routing feature controls the route; modifying it steers choices and removing it blocks persuasion. The mechanism generalizes across open-source LLMs and realistic poisoning scenarios like Generative Engine Optimization.

Conclusion: Persuasion in LLMs is a narrow, monitorable circuit rather than a diffuse failure, offering a concrete target for safety interventions.

Abstract: Language models can be persuaded to abandon factual knowledge. This vulnerability is central to AI safety, but its internal mechanism remains poorly understood. We uncover a compact causal mechanism for persuasion-induced factual errors. A small set of mid-layer attention heads almost entirely determines the model's answer. These heads write answer options into a low-dimensional polyhedron, with options occupying distinct vertices. Persuasion does not blur belief or merely reduce confidence; it causes a discrete latent jump from the correct-answer vertex to the persuasion-target vertex. We show that decision heads are not reasoning over evidence. Instead, they copy whichever option token their attention selects. Persuasion works by redirecting attention. We isolate a rank-one evidence-routing feature that controls the route. Directly modifying this feature steers the model's choice, and removing it blocks persuasion. We then trace the feature back to a band of shallower attention heads that build it from persuasive keywords in the input. Every step is validated by intervention. This mechanism appears across open-source LLMs and realistic poisoning scenarios such as Generative Engine Optimization, revealing persuasion as a narrow, monitorable circuit.

</details>


### [170] [Do Self-Evolving Agents Forget? Capability Degradation and Preservation in Lifelong LLM Agent Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09315)
*Ye Yu,Xiaopeng Yuan,Haibo Jin,Heming Liu,Yaoning Yu,Haohan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents undergoing self-evolution suffer from capability erosion—non-monotonic loss of previously acquired skills—across workflow, skill, model, and memory evolution channels. The proposed Capability-Preserving Evolution (CPE) stabilization principle mitigates this, improving retained simple-task performance from 41.8% to 52.8% under GPT-5.1 optimization while maintaining complex-task adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To show that self-evolving LLM agents experience non-monotonic capability degradation across all major evolution channels (workflow, skill, model, memory) when adapting to new task distributions, and to propose a solution for preserving previously learned capabilities.

Method: The paper identifies capability erosion under self-evolution across four dimensions (workflow, skill, model, memory) and proposes Capability-Preserving Evolution (CPE), a general stabilization principle that constrains destructive capability drift during continual adaptation.

Result: CPE consistently improves retained capability stability while preserving adaptation performance across all four evolution dimensions. In workflow evolution, retained simple-task performance improved from 41.8% to 52.8% under GPT-5.1 optimization, with stronger complex-task adaptation.

Conclusion: Stable long-horizon self-evolving agents require not only acquiring new capabilities but also explicitly preserving previously learned ones during continual adaptation.

Abstract: Recent advances in LLM agents enable systems that autonomously refine workflows, accumulate reusable skills, self-train their underlying models, and maintain persistent memory. However, we show that such self-evolution is often non-monotonic: adapting to new task distributions can progressively degrade previously acquired capabilities across all major evolution channels.
  We identify this phenomenon as \emph{capability erosion under self-evolution} and show that it consistently emerges across workflow, skill, model, and memory evolution. To mitigate this issue, we propose \emph{Capability-Preserving Evolution} (CPE), a general stabilization principle that constrains destructive capability drift during continual adaptation. Across all four evolution dimensions, CPE consistently improves retained capability stability while preserving adaptation performance. For example, in workflow evolution, CPE improves retained simple-task performance from 41.8\% to 52.8\% under GPT-5.1 optimization while simultaneously achieving stronger complex-task adaptation.
  Our findings suggest that stable long-horizon self-evolving agents require not only acquiring new capabilities, but also explicitly preserving previously learned ones during continual adaptation.

</details>


### [171] [SKG-VLA: Scene Knowledge Graph Priors for Structured Scene Semantics and Multimodal Reasoning for Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09343)
*Zeyu Li,Lei Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SKG-VLA models complaint cases as structured Scene Knowledge Graphs (SKG) to integrate multimodal evidence, then uses a three-stage training strategy to improve policy-grounded reasoning and decision accuracy in large-scale complaint handling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing complaint understanding systems rely on shallow classification or template matching over isolated modalities, failing to leverage explicit scene structure, rule knowledge, and cross-evidence dependencies for decision making.

Method: (1) Represent each case as a Scene Knowledge Graph (SKG) unifying complaint entities, evidence, policy clauses, temporal events, and action-relevant relations. (2) Build a data synthesis pipeline for scene descriptions, graph generalizations, QA supervision, and decision recommendations. (3) Construct a large-scale multimodal complaint scene dataset. (4) Adopt three-stage training: domain-adaptive pre-training, task-oriented instruction fine-tuning, and end-to-end multimodal alignment.

Result: SKG-VLA consistently improves policy-grounded reasoning, complaint decision accuracy, long-tail generalization, and robustness under incomplete evidence.

Conclusion: Structured scene knowledge graphs combined with staged multimodal training effectively address the limitations of existing shallow classification approaches in complaint decision making.

Abstract: Decision making in large-scale complaint handling systems increasingly relies on heterogeneous evidence, including complaint narratives, screenshots, order metadata, historical interactions, and platform policies. Existing complaint understanding systems mainly perform shallow classification or template matching over isolated modalities, while underutilizing explicit scene structure, rule knowledge, and cross-evidence dependencies. To address this limitation, we present SKG-VLA for multimodal complaint decision making. The core idea is to model each case as a structured complaint scene and represent its decision-relevant semantics with a \emph{Scene Knowledge Graph} (SKG), which organizes complaint entities, evidence items, policy clauses, temporal events, transactional states, and action-relevant relations into a unified graph. Based on SKG, we build a data synthesis pipeline that generates complaint scene descriptions, rule-consistent graph generalizations, question-answer supervision, and decision recommendations. We further construct a large-scale complaint scene dataset with both text-only and multimodal in-domain benchmarks. Finally, we adopt a three-stage training strategy -- domain-adaptive pre-training, task-oriented instruction fine-tuning, and end-to-end multimodal alignment -- to inject structured scene priors into a multimodal decision model. Experiments show that SKG-VLA consistently improves policy-grounded reasoning, complaint decision accuracy, long-tail generalization, and robustness under incomplete evidence.

</details>


### [172] [Dsat: A Native SAT Solver for Discrete Logic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09347)
*Yaofang Zhang,Ken Zhou,Adnan Darwiche*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper develops a native SAT solver for discrete logic (variables with arbitrary values) that extends Boolean SAT techniques (unit resolution, clause learning) to handle discrete variables directly, avoiding computational and semantical issues of binarization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete variables are common in probabilistic reasoning, planning, and explainable AI, but the standard binarization approach for using Boolean SAT solvers introduces computational and semantical challenges.

Method: A native SAT solver for discrete logic that extends Boolean SAT algorithms (unit resolution, clause learning) to operate directly on multi-valued discrete variables without binarization.

Result: Empirical comparison shows the proposed solver outperforms CSP solvers on discrete CNFs, Boolean SAT solvers on binarized CNFs, and hybrid solvers.

Conclusion: A native discrete SAT solver can effectively handle discrete variables, avoiding the drawbacks of binarization while maintaining similar algorithmic efficiency to Boolean SAT solvers.

Abstract: Discrete variables are common in many applications, such as probabilistic reasoning, planning and explainable AI. When symbolic reasoning techniques are brought in to bear on these applications, a standard technique for handling discrete variables is to binarize them into Boolean variables to allow the use of Boolean computational machinery such as SAT solvers. This technique can face both computational and semantical challenges though. In this work, we develop a native SAT solver for discrete logic, which is a direct extension of Boolean logic in which variables can take arbitrary values. Our proposed solver has a similar design to Boolean SAT solvers, with ingredients such as unit resolution and clause learning but ones that operate natively on discrete variables. We illustrate the merits of the developed SAT solver by comparing it empirically to CSP solvers applied to discrete CNFs, to Boolean SAT solver applied to binarized CNFs, and to some hybrid solvers.

</details>


### [173] [CHAINTRIX: A multi-pipeline LLM-augmented framework for automated smart-contract security auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09350)
*Gabriela Dobrita,Simona-Vasilica Oprea,Adela Bara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Chaintrix is an end-to-end smart-contract auditing framework that combines LLMs with deterministic structural analysis via a Cross-Contract Interaction Model (CCIM). It achieves 71.7% recall on high-severity vulnerabilities (86/120) on EVMbench, outperforming frontier-model baselines by 26 percentage points, by forcing every LLM-generated claim to be verified against parsed Solidity code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Smart-contract exploits have caused billions in losses, but audits are expensive and slow. Existing automated tools fail: static analyzers produce findings that fail manual triage at high rates, while LLMs hallucinate findings that contradict source code. A gap exists for a reliable, scalable auditing approach.

Method: Chaintrix introduces a Cross-Contract Interaction Model (CCIM) that parses Solidity into function-level reads, writes, modifiers, and cross-contract calls. This serves as the substrate for 12 deterministic signal engines and parallel LLM audit pipelines. A staged false-positive-reduction pipeline with a Structural Verdict Engine (SVE) applies deterministic structural checks; selected high-confidence findings are further validated via symbolic execution and fuzz testing.

Result: On EVMbench (OpenAI, Paradigm, OtterSec), Chaintrix detects 86 of 120 high-severity vulnerabilities (71.7% recall), with 25 audits achieving 100% recall. It outperforms the strongest frontier-model baseline by 26 percentage points.

Conclusion: Chaintrix demonstrates that grounding LLM-generated claims against a deterministic structural contract representation (CCIM) significantly improves recall and reliability in smart-contract auditing, outperforming both static analyzers and pure LLM approaches.

Abstract: Smart-contract exploits have caused billions of USD in cumulative losses, yet audits remain expensive and slow. Automated tools have emerged to close this gap, but each class has a characteristic failure mode. Static analyzers report findings that frequently fail manual triage at high rates, while large language models (LLMs) hallucinate findings that contradict the source code. Thus, we propose Chaintrix, an end-to-end auditing framework whose central architectural commitment is that every LLM-generated claim must be discharged against a deterministic structural contract representation. We introduce a Cross-Contract Interaction Model (CCIM) that parses Solidity into a structured map of function-level reads, writes, modifiers and resolved cross-contract calls. CCIM serves as the substrate against which all 12 of Chaintrix's deterministic signal engines and the parallel LLM audit pipelines operate. A staged false-positive-reduction pipeline, terminating in a Structural Verdict Engine (SVE) that applies deterministic structural checks against parsed code, filters the merged finding set, with selected high-confidence findings further validated through symbolic execution and fuzz testing. We evaluate Chaintrix on EVMbench, the smart-contract security benchmark by OpenAI, Paradigm, OtterSec. Chaintrix detects 86 of 120 high-severity vulnerabilities (71.7% recall), with 25 audits scoring 100% recall, placing Chaintrix 26 percentage points above the strongest frontier-model baseline.

</details>


### [174] [The Wittgensteinian Representation Hypothesis: Is Language the Attractor of Multimodal Convergence?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09352)
*Zhaoyang Zhang,Run Shao,Dongyue Wu,Jiajie Teng,Chao Tao,Jingdong Chen,Haifeng Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces directional convergence analysis using cycle-kNN, an asymmetric alignment measure, and discovers that non-language modalities (point clouds, vision) consistently move toward language's representational structure more than vice versa. This asymmetry is traced to feature density and interpreted through the Information Bottleneck framework, formalized as the Wittgensteinian Representation Hypothesis: language's semantic structure is the asymptotic attractor of multimodal representation convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding why independently trained neural networks from different modalities converge toward shared representations remains an open question, and existing evidence relies on symmetric similarity measures that are blind to the direction of convergence.

Method: Introduced directional convergence analysis using cycle-kNN (an asymmetric alignment measure) applied across dozens of independently trained unimodal models spanning point clouds, vision, and language. Mechanistic analysis traced directionality to feature density asymmetry, interpreted via the Information Bottleneck framework.

Result: Discovered a consistent directional asymmetry: non-language modalities move toward language's neighborhood structure significantly more than the reverse. This pattern holds across all model families and scales but is invisible to symmetric measures. Language representations occupy the most compact regions of representational space.

Conclusion: The Information Bottleneck framework suggests optimization under compression drives representations toward discrete, compositional structures characteristic of language, formalized as the Wittgensteinian Representation Hypothesis: the semantic structure of language is the asymptotic attractor of multimodal representation convergence.

Abstract: Understanding why independently trained neural networks from different modalities converge toward shared representations, and where this convergence leads, remains an open question in representation learning. All existing evidence relies on symmetric similarity measures, which can detect convergence but are structurally blind to its direction. We introduce directional convergence analysis using cycle-kNN, an asymmetric alignment measure, applied across dozens of independently trained unimodal models spanning point clouds, vision, and language. We uncover a consistent directional asymmetry: non-language modalities move toward the neighborhood structure of language significantly more than the reverse, and this pattern holds across all model families and scales--yet is entirely invisible to symmetric measures. Mechanistic analysis traces the directionality to feature density asymmetry, whereby language representations occupy the most compact regions of representational space. The Information Bottleneck framework provides a principled interpretation: optimization under compression drives representations toward discrete, compositional structures characteristic of language. We formalize this as the Wittgensteinian Representation Hypothesis: the semantic structure of language is the asymptotic attractor of multimodal representation convergence.

</details>


### [175] [Position: Avoid Overstretching LLMs for every Enterprise Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09365)
*Kuldeep Singh,Anson Bastos,Isaiah Onando Mulang'*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that for enterprise workloads, LLMs should be used as interfaces for structured extraction rather than monolithic engines, with knowledge and computation externalized to dedicated components like knowledge bases and symbolic procedures for greater reliability, scalability, and transparency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise tasks are deterministic, structured, and knowledge-dependent, requiring strict cost, latency, and reliability guarantees. Current approaches (LLM deployment or distillation) are inefficient, unreliable, and misaligned with these task structures.

Method: The authors provide theoretical evidence that finite-capacity models cannot fully capture enterprise knowledge breadth. They propose a modular architecture where LLMs serve as structured extraction interfaces, while knowledge bases and symbolic procedures handle computation and storage.

Result: Formal demonstrations show that the proposed modular architectures are more reliable and maintainable than monolithic LLM frameworks for enterprise tasks.

Conclusion: Language models should be limited to structured extraction in deterministic enterprise workflows; modular architectures separating language interfaces from knowledge/computation components offer a sustainable foundation for enterprise AI systems.

Abstract: Enterprise workloads are dominated by deterministic, structured, and knowledge-dependent tasks operating under strict cost, latency, and reliability constraints. While these are often addressed through large language model (LLM) deployment or distillation into smaller models, we argue this is inefficient, unreliable, and misaligned with enterprise task structures. Instead, AI systems should treat language models as interfaces rather than monolithic engines, externalizing knowledge and computation into dedicated components for greater reliability, scalability, and transparency. Our theoretical evidences show that finite-capacity models cannot fully capture the breadth of knowledge required for enterprise tasks, creating inherent limits to efficiency and interpretability. Building on this, we take the position that language models should primarily be used for structured extraction in deterministic enterprise workflows, while computation and storage are delegated to knowledge bases and symbolic procedures. We formally demonstrate that such modular architectures are more reliable and maintainable than monolithic frameworks, offering a sustainable foundation for enterprise tasks.

</details>


### [176] [Towards a Virtual Neuroscientist: Autonomous Neuroimaging Analysis via Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09366)
*Keqi Han,Songlin Zhao,Yao Su,Lifang He,Carl Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NIAgent is a multi-agent system for autonomous end-to-end neuroimaging analysis that uses code-centric execution and hierarchical verification to dynamically adapt workflows, outperforming standard baselines on ADHD-200 and ADNI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transforming neuroimaging data into clinically actionable biomarkers is knowledge-intensive and labor-intensive. Existing standardized workflows like fMRIPrep are statically configured and cannot reason about downstream objectives, deliberate over alternatives, or close the loop between intermediate evidence and subsequent decisions, leaving experts trapped in manual trial-and-error cycles that limit scalability.

Method: NIAgent employs a multi-agent system with a code-centric execution paradigm where specialist agents collaboratively synthesize and optimize executable programs over composable domain-specific primitives. It also features a hierarchical verification framework for autonomous quality control, integrating cohort-level metric screening with agentic visual inspection for evidence-grounded workflow remediation.

Result: Experiments on ADHD-200 and ADNI demonstrate that NIAgent outperforms standard workflow-based baselines in predictive performance and exhibits sophisticated agentic behaviors including strategy exploration and adaptive refinement.

Conclusion: The closed-loop adaptive approach bridges the gap between static neuroimaging pipelines and human-like reasoning, enabling scalable clinical biomarker development through autonomous, dynamically adapting workflows.

Abstract: Transforming neuroimaging data into clinically actionable biomarkers is a knowledge-intensive and labor-intensive process. Standardized workflows such as fMRIPrep have improved robustness and efficiency, but they are statically configured and cannot reason about downstream objectives, deliberate over alternative strategies, or close the loop between intermediate evidence and subsequent decisions in the way a human researcher would. This lack of closed-loop adaptation often leaves domain experts trapped in a cycle of manual trial-and-error to tune parameters and remediate pipeline failures, severely constraining the scalability of clinical biomarker development. To bridge this gap, we introduce NIAgent, a multi-agent system for autonomous end-to-end neuroimaging analysis. Unlike conventional flat tool-calling agents, NIAgent adopts a code-centric execution paradigm where specialist agents collaboratively synthesize and optimize executable programs over composable domain-specific primitives. This design enables robust, long-horizon workflow construction that adapts dynamically to runtime observations. Furthermore, we propose a hierarchical verification framework for autonomous quality control, integrating cohort-level metric screening with agentic visual inspection to drive evidence-grounded workflow remediation. Experiments on ADHD-200 and ADNI demonstrate that NIAgent outperforms standard workflow-based baselines in predictive performance while exhibiting sophisticated agentic behaviors, including strategy exploration and adaptive refinement.

</details>


### [177] [Explainable Knowledge Tracing via Probabilistic Embeddings and Pattern-based Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09369)
*Siyu Wu,Cong Xu,Wei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PLKT is an interpretable knowledge tracing framework that uses Beta-distributed probabilistic embeddings and explicit logical operations to model knowledge states, achieving both high accuracy and transparency in predicting student performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep learning-based KT models use deterministic embeddings and opaque latent state transitions, which limit interpretability regarding how specific past behaviors influence predictions.

Method: PLKT formulates prediction as goal-conditioned evidence reasoning over historical behaviors, using robust Beta-distributed probabilistic embeddings to represent knowledge states and performing explicit logical operations (e.g., conjunction) to construct transparent reasoning paths.

Result: PLKT outperforms state-of-the-art KT methods while achieving superior interpretability.

Conclusion: Probabilistic embeddings with explicit logical reasoning enable both high predictive accuracy and interpretability in knowledge tracing, addressing the opacity of deterministic latent state models.

Abstract: Knowledge Tracing (KT) models students' knowledge states based on learning interactions to predict performance. While deep learning-based KT models have boosted predictive accuracy, most models rely on deterministic vector embeddings and opaque latent state transitions, limiting interpretability regarding how specific past behaviors influence predictions. To address this limitation, we propose Probabilistic Logical Knowledge Tracing (PLKT), an interpretable KT framework that formulates prediction as a goal-conditioned evidence reasoning process over historical learning behaviors. Instead of representing knowledge states as deterministic vector embeddings, PLKT employs robust Beta-distributed probabilistic embeddings to represent student knowledge states. This probabilistic foundation allows us to model the uncertainty of historical behaviors and perform explicit logical operations (e.g., conjunction), constructing transparent reasoning paths that reveal how specific past interactions contribute to the prediction. Extensive experiments show that PLKT outperforms state-of-the-art KT methods while achieving superior interpretability. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/PLKT-D3CE/.

</details>


### [178] [Do Linear Probes Generalize Better in Persona Coordinates?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09391)
*Prasad Mahadik,Adrians Skapars*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes using persona vectors and PCA to construct low-dimensional subspaces of model internals that capture harmful behaviors (deception, sycophancy) more robustly and transfer better across datasets than probes trained on raw activations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-only monitoring fails because models exhibit strategic deception and sandbagging during evaluation, while existing white-box monitors (linear probes) fail under distribution shift, limiting real-world usefulness.

Method: Construct persona axes for deception and sycophancy using contrastive persona prompts; apply unsupervised PCA on persona-specific vectors to extract low-dimensional subspaces; train probes on persona-PC projections.

Result: Across 10 evaluation datasets, persona-derived directions cleanly separate harmful/harmless personas; probes trained on persona-PC projections generalize better than those trained on raw activations; a unified axis combining multiple behaviors improves cross-behavior and cross-dataset generalization.

Conclusion: Persona vectors provide a useful inductive bias for building more transferable behavior probes for monitoring harmful behaviors in language models.

Abstract: It is becoming increasingly necessary to have monitors check for harmful behaviors during language model interactions, but text-only monitoring has not been sufficient. This is because models sometimes exhibit strategic deception and sandbagging, changing their behavior during evaluation. This motivates the use of white-box monitors like linear probes, which can read the model internals directly. Currently, such probes can fail under distribution shift, limiting their usefulness in real settings. We study whether there exists a low-dimensional subspace of the model internals that captures harmful behaviors more robustly, while leaving out spuriously correlative features. Inspired by the Assistant Axis and Persona Selection Model, we construct persona axes for deception and sycophancy using contrastive persona prompts. The first principal components, obtained by unsupervised PCA of the persona-specific vectors, cleanly separate harmful and harmless personas. Across 10 evaluation datasets, we show that persona-derived directions transfer non-trivially and probes trained on persona-PC projections generalize better than probes trained on raw activations. We also find that a unified axis consisting of multiple harmful and harmless behaviors improves generalization across behaviors and datasets. Overall, persona vectors provide a useful inductive bias for building more transferable behavior probes.

</details>


### [179] [Empowering VLMs for Few-Shot Multimodal Time Series Classification via Tailored Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09395)
*Lin Li,Jiawei Huang,Qihao Quan,Dan Li,Boxin Li,Xiao Zhang,Erli Meng,Wenjie Feng,Jian Lou,See-Kiong Ng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MarsTSC is the first VL agentic reasoning framework for few-shot multimodal Time Series Classification, featuring a self-evolving knowledge bank iteratively refined via three collaborative agents (Generator, Reflector, Modifier) and a test-time update strategy. It outperforms classical and foundation model baselines across 12 benchmarks with 6 VLM backbones, producing interpretable rationales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Few-shot multimodal time series classification suffers from limited labeled data and distribution shift; existing methods lack dynamic knowledge refinement and interpretable reasoning grounded in visual-language models.

Method: A three-agent VL reasoning framework: (1) Generator performs few-shot classification via reasoning; (2) Reflector diagnoses reasoning errors and identifies overlooked temporal features; (3) Modifier applies verified updates to a self-evolving knowledge bank. A test-time update strategy enables cautious, continuous knowledge refinement to mitigate few-shot bias and distribution shift.

Result: Consistent and substantial performance gains across 12 time series benchmarks using 6 VLM backbones, outperforming both classical and foundation model-based baselines under few-shot conditions, with human-readable interpretable rationales.

Conclusion: The proposed reflective agentic reasoning framework with a self-evolving knowledge bank effectively addresses few-shot bias and distribution shift in multimodal time series classification, while maintaining interpretability.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose the first VL$\underline{\textbf{M}}$ $\underline{\textbf{a}}$gentic $\underline{\textbf{r}}$easoning framework for few-$\underline{\textbf{s}}$hot multimodal $\underline{\textbf{T}}$ime $\underline{\textbf{S}}$eries $\underline{\textbf{C}}$lassification ($\textbf{MarsTSC}$), which introduces a self-evolving knowledge bank as a dynamic context iteratively refined via reflective agentic reasoning. The framework comprises three collaborative roles: i) Generator conducts reliable classification via reasoning; ii) Reflector diagnoses the root causes of reasoning errors to yield discriminative insights targeting the temporal features overlooked by Generator; iii) Modifier applies verified updates to the knowledge bank to prevent context collapse. We further introduce a test-time update strategy to enable cautious, continuous knowledge bank refinement to mitigate few-shot bias and distribution shift. Extensive experiments across 12 mainstream time series benchmarks demonstrate that $\textbf{MarsTSC}$ delivers substantial and consistent performance gains across 6 VLM backbones, outperforming both classical and foundation model-based time series baselines under few-shot conditions, while producing interpretable rationales that ground each classification decision in human-readable feature evidence.

</details>
