### [180] [Strategic commitments shape collective cybersecurity under AI inequality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09415)
*Adeela Bashir,Zia Ush Shamszaman,Zhao Song,The Anh Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper uses an evolutionary game-theoretic model to study how differential access to AI-enabled defence tools affects cybersecurity. It finds that costly high-capability defence leads to weak-defence equilibria, and that committed defenders alone cannot stabilize strong defence. However, subsidizing committed defenders significantly increases strong defence adoption, suppresses attacks, and improves system resilience.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When advanced AI-enabled defence tools are unevenly accessible, resource-limited defenders may fail to adopt effective protection, creating persistent vulnerabilities. The paper aims to understand this imbalance and find mechanisms to stabilize cybersecurity in AI-driven environments.

Method: The authors develop an evolutionary game-theoretic model in a finite population, incorporating defenders with low- and high-capability protection options, committed defenders who always adopt strong defence, social learning dynamics, and targeted subsidies to remove cost disadvantages.

Result: (1) Without intervention, costly high-capability defence drives the population toward weak-defence behaviour. (2) Committed defenders alone increase strong defence prevalence but cannot stabilize secure outcomes. (3) Subsidized commitment significantly increases strong defence adoption, suppresses successful attacks, and improves overall system resilience. (4) Social-welfare analysis shows improved defender outcomes with low attacker gains. (5) Simulations across a broad parameter space confirm subsidies consistently outperform commitment alone.

Conclusion: Targeted support for key defenders (subsidies) is an effective mechanism for stabilizing cybersecurity in AI-driven environments, providing a theoretical bridge between cybersecurity policy, AI governance, and strategic allocation of defensive AI capabilities.

Abstract: The growing integration of AI into cybersecurity is reshaping the balance between attackers and defenders. When access to advanced AI-enabled defence tools is uneven, resource-limited defenders may be unable to adopt effective protection, creating persistent system vulnerabilities. We study the impact of differential AI access using an evolutionary game-theoretic model in a finite population. We first show that when high-capability defence is costly, the population is driven toward low-cost, weak-defence behaviour, sustaining attacks and weakening long-run security. To address this problem, we introduce differential access to AI defence tools by allowing defenders to choose between low- and high-capability protection based on their resources. We then examine the role of a small group of committed defenders who always adopt strong defence and influence others through social learning. Although commitment increases the prevalence of strong defence, it alone cannot stabilise secure outcomes due to high defence costs. We therefore incorporate a targeted subsidy to remove the cost disadvantage from committed defenders. Our analysis shows that subsidised commitment significantly increases strong defence adoption, suppresses successful attacks, and improves overall system resilience. Simulations across a broad parameter space confirm that subsidies consistently outperform commitment alone. In addition, social-welfare analysis shows improved defender outcomes while keeping attacker gains low. These findings suggest that targeted support for key defenders can be an effective mechanism for stabilising cybersecurity in AI-driven environments and provide a theoretical bridge between cybersecurity policy, AI governance, and strategic allocation of defensive AI capabilities.

</details>


### [181] [From Passive Reuse to Active Reasoning: Grounding Large Language Models for Neuro-Symbolic Experience Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09419)
*Yanan Xiao,Yixiang Tang,Zechen Feng,Lu Jiang,Minghao Yin,Pengyang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NSER transforms standard experience replay from passive sample reuse into active knowledge construction by using LLMs to induce behavioral rules, grounding them into differentiable logic, and dynamically reweighting replay distribution to improve sample efficiency and convergence speed in RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard experience replay methods treat the buffer as a passive memory system prioritizing samples based on numerical prediction errors rather than semantic significance, contrasting with human learning that abstracts fragmented experiences into behavioral rules.

Method: NSER uses a neuro-symbolic grounding pipeline: (1) LLMs induce candidate behavioral rules from trajectories in a zero-shot manner, (2) these rules are grounded into differentiable first-order logic representations, and (3) the symbolic structures dynamically reweight the replay distribution to shape policy optimization.

Result: NSER achieves consistent superior sample efficiency and convergence speed across reactive, rule-based, and procedural benchmarks.

Conclusion: By allowing abstract knowledge to directly shape policy optimization, NSER bridges the gap between linguistic reasoning and numerical optimization, transforming experience replay into an active engine for knowledge construction.

Abstract: While experience replay is essential for data efficiency in reinforcement learning (RL), standard methods treat the replay buffer as a passive memory system, prioritizing samples based on numerical prediction errors rather than their semantic significance. This approach stands in contrast to human learning, which accelerates mastery by actively abstracting fragmented experiences into behavioral rules. To bridge this gap, we propose Neuro-Symbolic Experience Replay (NSER), a framework that transforms experience replay from a passive sample reuse mechanism into an active engine for knowledge construction. Specifically, NSER addresses the incompatibility between linguistic reasoning and numerical optimization through a novel neuro-symbolic grounding pipeline. It leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) in a zero-shot manner to induce candidate behavioral rules from accumulated trajectories, grounds these insights into differentiable first-order logic representations, and utilizes the resulting symbolic structures to dynamically reweight the replay distribution. By allowing abstract knowledge to directly shape policy optimization, NSER achieves consistent superior sample efficiency and convergence speed across reactive, rule-based, and procedural benchmarks.

</details>


### [182] [SimWorld Studio: Automatic Environment Generation with Evolving Coding Agent for Embodied Agent Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09423)
*Haoqiang Kang,Xiaokang Ye,Yuhan Liu,Siddhant Hitesh Mantri,Lingjun Mao,James Fleming,Drishti Regmi,Lianhui Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SimWorld Studio is an open-source Unreal Engine 5 platform that uses SimCoder, a tool/skill-augmented coding agent, to autonomously generate evolving 3D embodied learning environments from language/image instructions. SimCoder self-evolves through verifier feedback and supports co-evolution with embodied agents, generating adaptive curricula. Case studies on navigation show self-evolution improves generation reliability, and co-evolution yields up to 40-point success-rate gains over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embodied agents lack abundant, diverse, and automatically generated 3D environments for interactive learning, unlike LLM/VLM-based digital agents which benefit from rich sandboxes. Existing simulators rely on manual crafting or procedural templates, and LLM-based 3D generation typically produces static scenes without verifiable tasks or standard learning interfaces.

Method: The platform is built on Unreal Engine 5 with SimCoder, a tool/skill-augmented coding agent that writes and executes engine-level code to construct 3D worlds from language/image inputs. SimCoder self-evolves using multi-source verifier feedback (compilation errors, physics checks, VLM critiques) to revise environments and autonomously add reusable tools/skills. Generated worlds are exported as Gym-style environments. Co-evolution uses agent performance feedback to guide SimCoder in generating adaptive curricula near the learner's capability frontier.

Result: Three case studies on embodied navigation show: (1) self-evolution improves generation reliability; (2) generated environments substantially improve embodied agent performance that generalizes to unseen benchmarks; (3) co-evolution yields an 18-point success-rate gain over fixed-environment learning and a 40-point gain over an untrained agent.

Conclusion: SimWorld Studio enables scalable, automatic generation of evolving embodied learning environments with verifiable tasks, and supports co-evolution between environment generation and embodied agent learning, significantly improving agent performance and generalization.

Abstract: LLM/VLM-based digital agents have advanced rapidly thanks to scalable sandboxes for coding, web navigation, and computer use, which provide rich interactive training grounds. In contrast, embodied agents still lack abundant, diverse, and automatically generated 3D environments for interactive learning. Existing embodied simulators rely on manually crafted scenes or procedural templates, while recent LLM-based 3D generation systems mainly produce static scenes rather than deployable environments with verifiable tasks and standard learning interfaces. We introduce SimWorld Studio, an open-source platform built on Unreal Engine 5 for generating evolving embodied learning environments. At its core is SimCoder, a tool/skill-augmented coding agent that writes and executes engine-level code to construct physically grounded 3D worlds from language/image instructions. SimCoder self-evolves by using verifier feedback (e.g., compilation errors, physics checks, VLM critiques) to revise environments and autonomously add reusable tools and skills to its library. Generated worlds are exported as Gym-style environments for embodied agent learning. SimWorld Studio further enables co-evolution between environment generation and embodied learning: agent performance feedback guides SimCoder to generate adaptive curricula near the learner's capability frontier, so that environments become increasingly challenging as the embodied agent improves. Three case studies on embodied navigation show that self-evolution improves generation reliability, generated environments substantially improve embodied agent performance that generalizes to unseen benchmarks, and co-evolution yields an 18-point success-rate gain over fixed-environment learning and a 40-point gain over an untrained agent.

</details>


### [183] [VulTriage: Triple-Path Context Augmentation for LLM-Based Vulnerability Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09461)
*Wenxin Tang,Xiang Zhang,Junliang Liu,Jingyu Xiao,Xi Xiao,Jinlong Yang,Yuehe Ma,Zhenyu Liu,Zhengheng Li,Zicheng Wang,Wang Luo,Qing Li,Lei Wang,Peng Xiangli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VulTriage is a triple-path context augmentation framework for LLM-based vulnerability detection that enhances LLM input with control flow, knowledge, and semantic paths, achieving state-of-the-art performance on PrimeVul and showing generalization on Kotlin datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing learning-based vulnerability detection methods struggle to capture structural dependencies, domain-specific vulnerability knowledge, and complex program semantics. LLMs prompted with raw source code often miss vulnerabilities or produce false alarms, especially when vulnerable and benign functions differ only in subtle semantic details.

Method: VulTriage enhances LLM input through three complementary paths: (1) Control Path - extracts and verbalizes AST, CFG, and DFG information; (2) Knowledge Path - retrieves CWE-derived vulnerability patterns via hybrid dense-sparse retrieval; (3) Semantic Path - summarizes functional behavior before final judgment. These contexts are integrated into a unified instruction for the LLM.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on the PrimeVul pair test set, outperforming existing deep learning and LLM-based baselines on key pair-wise and classification metrics. Ablation studies verify each path's effectiveness, and experiments on Kotlin dataset demonstrate generalization under low-resource and class-imbalanced settings.

Conclusion: The triple-path context augmentation framework effectively addresses the limitations of raw-source-code prompting for LLMs, improving vulnerability detection accuracy and generalizing to low-resource settings.

Abstract: Automated vulnerability detection is a fundamental task in software security, yet existing learning-based methods still struggle to capture the structural dependencies, domain-specific vulnerability knowledge, and complex program semantics required for accurate detection. Recent Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong code understanding ability, but directly prompting them with raw source code often leads to missed vulnerabilities or false alarms, especially when vulnerable and benign functions differ only in subtle semantic details. To address this, we propose VulTriage, a triple-path context augmentation framework for LLM-based vulnerability detection. VulTriage enhances the LLM input through three complementary paths: a Control Path that extracts and verbalizes AST, CFG, and DFG information to expose control and data dependencies; a Knowledge Path that retrieves relevant CWE-derived vulnerability patterns and examples through hybrid dense--sparse retrieval; and a Semantic Path that summarizes the functional behavior of the code before the final judgment. These contexts are integrated into a unified instruction to guide the LLM toward more reliable vulnerability reasoning. Experiments on the PrimeVul pair test set show that VulTriage achieves state-of-the-art performance, outperforming existing deep learning and LLM-based baselines on key pair-wise and classification metrics. Further ablation studies verify the effectiveness of each path, and additional experiments on the Kotlin dataset demonstrate the generalization ability of VulTriage under low-resource and class-imbalanced settings. Our code is available at https://github.com/vinsontang1/VulTriage

</details>


### [184] [Don't Click That: Teaching Web Agents to Resist Deceptive Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09497)
*Yilin Zhang,Yingkai Hua,Chunyu Wei,Xin Wang,Yueguo Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DUDE is a two-stage framework that uses hybrid-reward learning, asymmetric penalties, and experience summarization to defend web agents against deceptive UI elements, reducing deception susceptibility by 53.8% while maintaining task performance, validated on a new 1,407-scenario benchmark RUC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLM-based web agents are vulnerable to deceptive interface elements during autonomous GUI interaction. Existing work either detects deception without integrating into task execution or documents attacks without proposing defenses, leaving a gap in practical, task-aware defense mechanisms.

Method: DUDE (Deceptive UI Detector & Evaluator) is a two-stage framework combining (1) hybrid-reward learning with asymmetric penalties to handle imbalanced deception signals, and (2) experience summarization that distills failure patterns into transferable guidance for robust decision-making.

Result: On the RUC benchmark (1,407 scenarios across four domains and deception categories), DUDE reduces deception susceptibility by 53.8% while maintaining task performance compared to baselines.

Conclusion: DUDE provides an effective foundation for robust web agent deployment by formalizing deception-aware defense, demonstrating significant vulnerability reduction without compromising utility, and introducing a standardized benchmark (RUC) for future research.

Abstract: Vision-language model (VLM) based web agents demonstrate impressive autonomous GUI interaction but remain vulnerable to deceptive interface elements. Existing approaches either detect deception without task integration or document attacks without proposing defenses. We formalize deception-aware web agent defense and propose DUDE (Deceptive UI Detector & Evaluator), a two-stage framework combining hybrid-reward learning with asymmetric penalties and experience summarization to distill failure patterns into transferable guidance. We introduce RUC (Real UI Clickboxes), a benchmark of 1,407 scenarios spanning four domains and deception categories. Experiments show DUDE reduces deception susceptibility by 53.8% while maintaining task performance, establishing an effective foundation for robust web agent deployment.

</details>


### [185] [EpiGraph: A Knowledge Graph and Benchmark for Evidence-Intensive Reasoning in Epilepsy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09505)
*Yuyang Dai,Zheng Chen,Jathurshan Pradeepkumar,Yasuko Matsubara,Jimeng Sun,Yasushi Sakurai,Yushun Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EpiGraph is a large-scale epilepsy knowledge graph integrating 48,166 papers and 7 clinical resources into 24,324 entities and 32,009 triplets across five clinical layers. The companion EpiBench benchmark defines five clinically motivated tasks. Integrating EpiGraph consistently improves LLM performance across all tasks, with pharmacogenomic reasoning gaining +30-41%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Epilepsy diagnosis and treatment require evidence-intensive reasoning across heterogeneous clinical knowledge (biosignal patterns, genetics, pharmacogenomics, treatment strategies, patient outcomes), but existing LLMs lack structured knowledge integration for such complex neurological reasoning.

Method: Construct EpiGraph by integrating 48,166 peer-reviewed papers and seven clinical resources into a heterogeneous graph with 24,324 entities and 32,009 evidence-grounded triplets across five clinical layers. Define EpiBench with five tasks: clinical decision-making, EEG report generation, pharmacogenomic precision medicine, treatment recommendation, deep research planning. Evaluate six LLMs under standard and Graph-RAG settings.

Result: Integrating EpiGraph consistently improves performance across all five tasks. The largest gains are observed in pharmacogenomic reasoning (+30-41%).

Conclusion: Structured epilepsy knowledge substantially enhances evidence-grounded clinical reasoning, and EpiGraph/EpiBench provide a practical framework for evaluating knowledge-augmented LLMs in real-world neurological settings.

Abstract: Epilepsy diagnosis and treatment require evidence-intensive reasoning across heterogeneous clinical knowledge, including biosignal patterns, genetic mechanisms, pharmacogenomics, treatment strategies, and patient outcomes. In this work, we present \textsc{EpiGraph}, a large-scale epilepsy knowledge graph and benchmark for evaluating knowledge-augmented clinical reasoning. \textsc{EpiGraph} integrates 48,166 peer-reviewed papers and seven clinical resources into a heterogeneous graph containing 24,324 entities and 32,009 evidence-grounded triplets across five clinical layers. Built upon this graph, \textsc{EpiBench} defines five clinically motivated tasks spanning clinical decision-making, EEG report generation, pharmacogenomic precision medicine, treatment recommendation, and deep research planning. We evaluate six LLMs under both standard and Graph-RAG settings. Results show that integrating \textsc{EpiGraph} consistently improves performance across all tasks, with the largest gains observed in pharmacogenomic reasoning (+30--41\%). Our findings demonstrate that structured epilepsy knowledge substantially enhances evidence-grounded clinical reasoning and provides a practical benchmark framework for evaluating knowledge-augmented LLMs in real-world neurological settings. Our code is available at: https://github.com/LabRAI/EEG-KG.

</details>


### [186] [WindINR: Latent-State INR for Fast Local Wind Query and Correction in Complex Terrain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09511)
*Yi Xiao,Qilong Jia,Hang Fan,Pascal Fua,Robert Jenssen,Xiaosong Ma,Wei Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WindINR is a latent-state implicit neural representation framework that enables continuous high-resolution local wind queries and sparse-observation correction, achieving ~2.6× speedup over full-network fine-tuning by updating only a compact latent state during inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many downstream decisions in complex terrain require fast wind estimates at user-specified locations and heights for given forecast times, rather than dense fixed-grid fields. Existing methods lack the ability to efficiently correct predictions using sparse local observations at inference time.

Method: WindINR uses a latent-conditioned decoder mapping terrain descriptors, low-resolution background fields, and continuous query coordinates to high-resolution wind states. It separates representation learning from latent-state correction: a privileged encoder learns from high-resolution supervision, a deployable predictor estimates initial latent states, and discrepancies form a Gaussian prior. At inference, only the latent state is updated via regularized correction using sparse observations.

Result: In OSSEs over the Senja region (UAV-aided and random-observation robustness tests), WindINR improves local high-resolution wind estimates by updating only the latent state. It achieves ~2.6× online-correction speedup over full-network fine-tuning on CPU.

Conclusion: WindINR provides a practical interface between kilometer-scale background products, sparse local observations, and wind queries in complex terrain, enabling efficient and continuously queryable high-resolution wind estimation.

Abstract: Many downstream decisions in complex terrain require fast wind estimates at a small number of user-specified locations and heights for a given forecast valid time, rather than another dense forecast field on a fixed grid. We present WindINR, a latent-state implicit neural representation framework for continuous high-resolution local wind query and sparse-observation correction. WindINR maps static terrain descriptors, a low-resolution background field, and continuous query coordinates to a high-resolution wind state through a latent-conditioned decoder. To enable rapid inference-time correction, WindINR separates reusable representation learning from sample-specific latent-state correction. During training, a privileged encoder infers a reference latent state from high-resolution supervision, a deployable latent predictor estimates an initial latent state from inference-time inputs alone, and their discrepancies are summarized into a dataset-adaptive Gaussian prior over latent corrections. At inference time, within the WindINR module, network weights remain fixed and only the latent state is updated by minimizing a regularized correction objective using sparse observations and their uncertainty. In controlled OSSEs over the Senja region, including a UAV-aided approach scenario and random-observation robustness tests, WindINR improves local high-resolution wind estimates by updating only a compact latent state rather than the full network. The corrected representation remains continuously queryable at arbitrary coordinates and, in our CPU benchmark, yields about a $2.6\times$ online-correction speedup over full-network fine-tuning, suggesting a practical interface between kilometer-scale background products, sparse local observations, and wind queries in complex terrain.

</details>


### [187] [Weighted Rules under the Stable Model Semantics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09519)
*Joohyung Lee,Yi Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces weighted rules under stable model semantics, inspired by Markov Logic's log-linear models, to add probabilistic and ranking capabilities to Answer Set Programming, and compares it with related formalisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the deterministic nature of stable model semantics by incorporating probabilistic and weighted reasoning, enabling inconsistency resolution, model ranking, and probabilistic inference in answer set programs.

Method: The method introduces weighted rules under stable model semantics following log-linear models from Markov Logic, allowing weights to be assigned to rules and using statistical inference to compute weighted stable models.

Result: The approach enables resolving inconsistencies in ASP, ranking stable models, associating probabilities with stable models, and applying statistical inference to compute weighted stable models.

Conclusion: The paper presents formal comparisons with answer set programs, Markov Logic, ProbLog, and P-log, showing the versatility and expressiveness of the proposed weighted rule framework under stable model semantics.

Abstract: We introduce the concept of weighted rules under the stable model semantics following the log-linear models of Markov Logic. This provides versatile methods to overcome the deterministic nature of the stable model semantics, such as resolving inconsistencies in answer set programs, ranking stable models, associating probability to stable models, and applying statistical inference to computing weighted stable models. We also present formal comparisons with related formalisms, such as answer set programs, Markov Logic, ProbLog, and P-log.

</details>


### [188] [Cplus2ASP: Computing Action Language C+ in Answer Set Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09528)
*Joseph Babb,Joohyung Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Cplus2ASP Version 2 is a significantly faster implementation of the definite fragment of action language C+ that uses modern answer set solving techniques, incremental execution via iclingo, multi-modal translations for other action languages, and features like Lua-based external atoms and interactive mode.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve the performance of the Causal Calculator (CCALC) by leveraging modern answer set solving techniques while maintaining full compatibility with its input language.

Method: The system implements a translation pipeline (f2lp, clingo, iclingo, as2transition) based on recent theoretical results, uses incremental grounding via iclingo justified by a module theorem extended to nested expressions, and supports external atoms through Lua calls.

Result: The new system is significantly faster than the original Causal Calculator Version 2 while remaining fully compatible with its input language.

Conclusion: Cplus2ASP Version 2 offers a high-performance, extensible tool for action language C+ and other action languages (B, BC) with modern ASP solving and user-friendly features.

Abstract: We present Version 2 of system Cplus2ASP, which implements the definite fragment of action language C+. Its input language is fully compatible with the language of the Causal Calculator Version 2, but the new system is significantly faster thanks to modern answer set solving techniques. The translation implemented in the system is a composition of several recent theoretical results. The system orchestrates a tool chain, consisting of f2lp, clingo, iclingo, and as2transition. Under the incremental execution mode, the system translates a C+ description into the input language of iclingo, exploiting its incremental grounding mechanism. The correctness of this execution is justified by the module theorem extended to programs with nested expressions. In addition, the input language of the system has many useful features, such as external atoms by means of Lua calls and the user interactive mode. The system supports extensible multi-modal translations for other action languages, such as B and BC, as well.

</details>


### [189] [LLM-Guided Monte Carlo Tree Search over Knowledge Graphs: Composing Mechanistic Explanations for Drug-Disease Pairs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09542)
*Rishabh Jakhar,Michel Dumontier,Remzi Celebi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TESSERA is a neuro-symbolic framework combining LLMs (for local discriminative judgment as prior policy and reward signal provider) with knowledge graphs (for hard structural constraints) and MCTS (for long-horizon search with credit assignment), applied to multi-step explanation extraction for drug mechanism elucidation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-step explanation extraction from knowledge graphs is combinatorially challenging, requiring heuristic guidance and credit assignment. Frontier LLMs offer heuristics but lack guarantees and their compositional performance degrades over long chains.

Method: A 3-part neuro-symbolic framework: (1) LLMs provide local discriminative judgment (prior policy for exploration bias and comparative state evaluator for reward signals); (2) Knowledge graph defines hypothesis space with hard structural constraints; (3) MCTS coordinates long-horizon search with principled credit assignment via backpropagation.

Result: Evaluation on drug mechanism elucidation across two knowledge graphs demonstrates fidelity to curated biology and coherent alternative mechanisms; ablations confirm discriminative contribution from both LLM components.

Conclusion: TESSERA offers a general paradigm for compositional reasoning over structured knowledge, applicable beyond its current drug mechanism elucidation use case.

Abstract: Extracting multi-step explanations from knowledge graphs poses a combinatorial challenge requiring both heuristic guidance (as candidates proliferate with depth) and credit assignment (as path quality emerges over extended sequences). Frontier LLMs, strong on knowledge/reasoning benchmarks, offer a compelling source of such heuristics, yet their knowledge comes sans guarantees and compositional performance degrades as chains lengthen. We thus present TESSERA, a 3-part neuro-symbolic framework that uses LLMs in a circumscribed role: for local discriminative judgement rather than autonomous multi-step generation; the knowledge graph then defines the hypothesis space enforcing hard structural constraints, and MCTS coordinates the long-horizon search with principled credit assignment via backpropagation. LLMs perform dual roles as a prior policy biasing exploration and a comparative state evaluator supplying reward signals. Evaluation on drug mechanism elucidation across two complementary knowledge graphs demonstrates fidelity to curated biology while surfacing coherent alternative mechanisms, with ablations confirming discriminative contribution from both LLM components. Beyond its current application, our framework offers a general paradigm for compositional reasoning over structured knowledge.

</details>


### [190] [TIDE-Bench: Task-Aware and Diagnostic Evaluation of Tool-Integrated Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09544)
*Yize Li,Junzhi Li,Jason Song,Chuxiong Sun,Rui Wang,Changwen Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TIDE-Bench is a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating tool-integrated reasoning in LLMs, featuring diverse tasks (including two newly designed ones), a task-aware evaluation protocol measuring multiple dimensions, and high-quality discriminative evaluation sets that reduce cost. Experiments reveal persistent bottlenecks in tool grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The field lacks a high-quality, unified evaluation benchmark for tool-integrated reasoning (TIR). Existing evaluations suffer from limited dataset quality, insufficient task diversity, lack of diagnostic comprehensiveness, and poor evaluation efficiency.

Method: TIDE-Bench introduces: (1) diverse task settings combining existing reasoning/Q&A tasks with two new tasks (tool-grounded experimental design and dynamic interactive tasks); (2) a comprehensive task-aware evaluation protocol measuring answer quality, process reliability, tool-use efficiency, and inference cost; (3) high-quality discriminative evaluation sets built by filtering low-discrimination instances from existing datasets.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple foundation models and TIR methods show persistent bottlenecks in tool grounding. The benchmark reduces evaluation cost while focusing on more challenging samples.

Conclusion: TIDE-Bench provides a holistic and efficient evaluation benchmark that reveals ongoing challenges in tool grounding for TIR, offering insights for future research directions in tool-integrated reasoning for LLMs.

Abstract: Tool-integrated reasoning has emerged as a promising paradigm for enhancing large language models with external computation, retrieval, and execution capabilities. However, the field still lacks a high-quality and unified evaluation benchmark, and existing TIR evaluations remain limited in dataset quality, task diversity, diagnostic comprehensiveness, and evaluation efficiency. In this work, we introduce TIDE-Bench, a holistic and efficient benchmark for evaluating TIR methods, featuring three key advantages. First, it provides diverse task settings, combining widely used mathematical reasoning and knowledge-intensive QA tasks with two newly designed tasks, namely the tool-grounded experimental design task and the dynamic interactive task, to probe models' abilities in complex tool invocation and multi-tool coordination. Second, TIDE-Bench adopts a comprehensive yet task-aware evaluation protocol, jointly measuring final answer quality, process reliability, tool-use efficiency, and inference cost across heterogeneous task settings. Third, TIDE-Bench constructs high-quality and discriminative evaluation sets by filtering low-discrimination instances from existing datasets, substantially reducing evaluation cost while focusing on more challenging samples. Extensive experiments on multiple foundation models and TIR methods reveal persistent bottlenecks in tool grounding, offering insights for future TIR research.

</details>


### [191] [PDEAgent-Bench: A Multi-Metric, Multi-Library Benchmark for PDE Solver Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09636)
*Zhen Hang,Yushan Yashengjiang,Junhui Li,Huanshuo Dong,Yang Wei,Zhezheng Hao,Jiangtao Ma,Songlin Bai,Haozhong Kai,Xihang Yue,Gangzong Si,Dongming Jiang,Chao Yao,Zhanhua Hu,Jiangqing Zhang,Pengwei Liu,Yaomin Shen,Xingyu Ren,Lei Liu,Zikang Xu,Han Li,Qingsong Yao,Hande Dong,Hong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PDEAgent-Bench is the first multi-metric, multi-library benchmark for PDE-to-solver code generation, containing 645 instances across 6 mathematical categories and 11 PDE families. It evaluates generated solvers on executability, accuracy, and efficiency using DOLFINx, Firedrake, and deal.II. Experiments show LLMs often produce runnable code but struggle with accuracy and efficiency requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks evaluate syntactic correctness or success on predefined test cases but lack benchmarks specifically for PDE-to-solver code generation. General-purpose benchmarks do not capture challenges like solver accuracy, efficiency, and compatibility with professional FEM libraries.

Method: PDEAgent-Bench contains 645 instances spanning 6 mathematical categories and 11 PDE families, supporting FEM libraries DOLFINx, Firedrake, and deal.II. It uses a staged evaluation framework: generated solvers must pass executability, numerical accuracy, and computational efficiency checks sequentially. Each instance includes a problem specification, reference solution on a prescribed grid, and case-specific accuracy/runtime targets.

Result: Representative LLMs and code agents often produce runnable code, but pass rates drop substantially when accuracy and efficiency requirements are enforced, indicating current agents are limited in producing numerically reliable and efficient PDE solvers.

Conclusion: PDEAgent-Bench provides a reproducible testbed grounded in practical requirements of numerical PDE solving, revealing that current code generation agents remain limited in producing numerically reliable and efficient solutions.

Abstract: PDE-to-solver code generation aims to automatically synthesize executable numerical solvers from partial differential equation (PDE) specifications. This task requires not only understanding the mathematical structure of PDEs, but also selecting appropriate discretization schemes and solver configurations, and correctly implementing the resulting formulations in finite-element method (FEM) libraries. Existing code generation benchmarks mainly evaluate syntactic correctness, or success on predefined test cases. To our knowledge, there is currently no publicly available benchmark specifically for PDE-to-solver code generation, and general-purpose code benchmarks do not fully capture the unique challenges of numerical PDE solution, such as ensuring solver accuracy, efficiency, and compatibility with professional FEM libraries. We introduce PDEAgent-Bench, to the best of our knowledge, the first multi-metric, multi-library benchmark for PDE-to-solver code generation. PDEAgent-Bench contains 645 instances across 6 mathematical categories and 11 PDE families, with common FEM libraries for DOLFINx, Firedrake, and deal.II. Each instance provides an agent-facing problem specification, a reference solution on a prescribed evaluation grid, and case-specific accuracy and runtime targets. PDEAgent-Bench adopts a staged evaluation framework in which generated solvers must sequentially pass executability, numerical accuracy, and computational efficiency checks. Experiments with representative LLMs and code agents show that models can often produce runnable code, but their pass rate drops substantially once accuracy and efficiency requirements are enforced. These results indicate that current agents remain limited in producing numerically reliable and efficient PDE solvers, and that PDEAgent-Bench provides a reproducible testbed grounded in the practical requirements of numerical PDE solving.

</details>


### [192] [Workspace Optimization: How to Train Your Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09650)
*Elad Sarafian,Gal Kaplun,Ron Banner,Daniel Soudry,Boris Ginsburg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces workspace optimization—adapting an agent's external structured workspace (rather than its weights) to improve performance—and presents DreamTeam, a multi-agent system that implements this idea, achieving a new SOTA on ARC-AGI-3 (38.4% vs. 36%) with 31% fewer environment actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier language models used as agents often cannot adapt their weights during inference. The authors ask what remains trainable in such settings, motivating the need for a learning paradigm that operates on the agent's external environment (workspace) rather than internal parameters.

Method: The authors propose workspace optimization, which mirrors weight-space training: artifacts = parameters, evidence = data, counterexamples = losses, textual feedback = gradients. They instantiate this in DreamTeam, a multi-agent harness with distinct roles (world model builder, planner, hypothesizer, prober, strategist, failure router) that evolves the workspace through interaction in multi-turn environments.

Result: On the 25-game ARC-AGI-3 public set under official scoring (averaged over two independent runs), DreamTeam improves the prior SOTA protocol-matched agent from 36% to 38.4%, while using 31% fewer environment actions per game.

Conclusion: Workspace optimization is a viable paradigm for improving frontier model agents in hard multi-turn tasks where weight adaptation is unavailable, and DreamTeam demonstrates its practical effectiveness on ARC-AGI-3.

Abstract: Modern agents built on frontier language models often cannot adapt their weights. What, then, remains trainable? We argue it is the agent's \emph{workspace}, the structured external substrate it reads, writes, and tests; we call its evolution workspace optimization. Workspace optimization targets hard multi-turn environments where a frontier model has strong priors but cannot solve the task in a single shot, so the agent must learn through interaction. We propose a principled way to evolve the workspace, mirroring the structure of weight-space training: artifacts in place of parameters, evidence in place of data, counterexamples in place of losses, and textual feedback in place of gradients. We instantiate the idea in DreamTeam, a multi-agent harness for ARC-AGI-3 whose roles build an executable world model, plan, hypothesize, probe, strategize, and route failures. On the current 25-game ARC-AGI-3 public set under the official scoring protocol and averaged over two independent runs, DreamTeam improves the SOTA protocol-matched agent's score from 36% to 38.4%, while using 31% fewer environment actions per game.

</details>


### [193] [CodeClinic: Evaluating Automation of Coding Skills for Clinical Reasoning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09675)
*Timothy Ossowski,Xinchi Liu,Danyal Maqbool,Vaibhav Dhanuka,Sheng Zhang,Hoifung Poon,Majid Afshar,Tyler Bradshaw,Junjie Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CodeClinic is a benchmark for evaluating LLM agents on synthesizing reusable clinical skills from EHR data, featuring longitudinal ICU monitoring and compositional information-seeking tasks. It includes an offline autoformalization pipeline that converts clinical guidelines into verified Python libraries, improving consistency and reducing token usage by up to 40% compared to zero-shot code generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based clinical reasoning systems rely on manually curated tool libraries, which require substantial expert effort to maintain. Zero-shot approaches produce inefficient and unreliable reasoning chains, especially under institution-specific policies. The authors aim to evaluate whether LLM agents can synthesize and compose reusable clinical skills autonomously.

Method: The authors introduce CodeClinic, built on MIMIC-IV, with two tasks: (1) longitudinal ICU surveillance with structured decisions every four hours across 25 findings and 8 clinical families, and (2) compositional information seeking with 63k instances across 259 tasks in 9 domains stratified by compositional dependency depth. They propose an offline autoformalization pipeline that converts natural-language clinical guidelines into reusable, verified Python skill libraries through iterative LLM refinement.

Result: The autoformalized skill libraries improve consistency over zero-shot code generation while reducing per-query token usage by up to 40%.

Conclusion: CodeClinic provides a structured benchmark for evaluating clinical reasoning agents, and the proposed autoformalization pipeline enables more efficient and reliable skill composition without relying on fixed, manually curated toolboxes.

Abstract: Clinical reasoning agents based on large language models (LLMs) aim to automate tasks such as intensive care unit (ICU) monitoring and patient state tracking from electronic health records (EHRs). Existing systems typically rely on manually curated clinical tools or skills for concepts such as sepsis detection and organ failure assessment. However, maintaining these tool libraries requires substantial expert effort, while zero-shot querying or code generation often produces inefficient and unreliable reasoning chains, especially under institution-specific clinical policies. We introduce CodeClinic, a benchmark built on MIMIC-IV for evaluating whether LLM agents can synthesize and compose reusable clinical skills instead of relying on fixed toolboxes. The benchmark contains two complementary tasks: longitudinal ICU surveillance and compositional information seeking. The longitudinal setting simulates monitoring patient trajectories with structured decisions every four hours across 25 findings and eight clinical families, while the compositional setting spans 63k instances across 259 tasks in nine domains and is stratified by compositional dependency depth to evaluate increasingly complex multi-step reasoning. We further propose an offline autoformalization pipeline that converts natural-language clinical guidelines into reusable and verified Python skill libraries through iterative LLM refinement. Compared with zero-shot code generation, the resulting libraries improve consistency while reducing per-query token usage by up to 40%.

</details>


### [194] [Absurd World: A Simple Yet Powerful Method to Absurdify the Real-world for Probing LLM Reasoning Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09678)
*Ryan Albright,Golam Md Muktadir,Zarif Ikram,S M Jubaer,Mehrab Hossain,Dianbo Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes Absurd World, a benchmarking framework that tests LLMs on logically coherent but altered scenarios to evaluate whether their reasoning is robust or merely relies on real-world pattern matching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous research focuses on breaking LLM reasoning with increasingly complex problems, but whether LLMs are robust in simple logical reasoning remains underexplored.

Method: Absurd World breaks real-world models into symbols, actions, sequences, and events, then automatically alters them to create absurd but logically coherent worlds where the solution logic remains unchanged.

Result: Evaluated a large collection of models with simple and advanced prompting techniques, proving Absurd World is an effective tool for determining LLMs' ability to think logically while ignoring real-world learned patterns.

Conclusion: The framework can be used to extensively test an LLM against real-world problem variations to verify the robustness of its reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: While extremely powerful and versatile at various tasks, the thinking capabilities of large language models (LLMs) are often put under scrutiny as they sometimes fail to solve problems that humans can systematically solve. However, recent literature focuses on breaking LLM reasoning with increasingly complex problems, and whether an LLM is robust in simple logical reasoning remains underexplored. This paper proposes Absurd World, a benchmarking framework, to test LLMs against altered realism, where scenarios are logically coherent, and humans can easily solve the tasks. Absurd World breaks a real-world model into symbols, actions, sequences, and events, which are automatically altered to create absurd worlds where the logic to solve the tasks remains the same. It evaluates a large collection of models with simple and advanced prompting techniques, and proves that it is an effective tool to determine LLMs' ability to think logically, ignoring the patterns learned from the real world. One can use this framework to extensively test an LLM against a real-world problem to verify whether the LLM's reasoning capability is robust against variations of the task.

</details>


### [195] [Unpredictability dissociates from structured control in language agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09692)
*Jia Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper tests whether stochastic sampling can replace structured control mechanisms (reasoning, memory, self-state, inhibition) in language agents. Across extensive ablation studies, lesion analyses, and controls (74K+ calls, 27K+ generations, 58K+ scored records), structured control consistently outperformed stochastic and other baselines in action-field coupling and behavioral metrics. Open-weight replications across multiple model sizes and families confirmed these findings. The study concludes that stochastic unpredictability cannot reproduce structured, action-coupled control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether stochastic sampling can substitute for structured mechanisms (reasoning, memory, self-state, inhibition) in action selection for language agents, since unpredictable behavior is often conflated with control but they need not coincide.

Method: Large-scale lesion/ablation experiments on a language-agent implementation (74,352 calls across 7 datasets), matched-interface control (26,946 generations), primary behavioral test (57,816 scored records), and open-weight replications (Qwen2.5 7B/14B/32B, Mistral-7B) across 20 task families and three scaffolds. Included blinded three-annotator audit (1,200 overlap items). Compared structured control against high-stochasticity, post-hoc, scrambled, verbosity, no-fields, scrambled-context, and distribution-matched controls.

Result: High-stochasticity comparator more unpredictable than structured control in 7/7 datasets; reason/veto lesions reduced structured-control profiles in 7/7 datasets each. Structured agent maintained stronger action-field coupling than all controls across every dataset. Structured control exceeded high-stochasticity and lesions in 7/7 datasets for all behavioral components. Open-weight replications confirmed: no-fields, scrambled-context, and distribution-matched controls failed to recover structured action control. Entropy matching, token/compute matching, and counterfactual-flip stress test did not meet their gates.

Conclusion: Stochastic unpredictability did not reproduce structured, action-coupled control in this implemented agent family, suggesting that structured mechanisms are necessary for proper action control in language agents.

Abstract: Unpredictable behavior is often taken as evidence of control, yet stochastic dispersion and structured action control need not coincide. This paper tests whether stochastic sampling can substitute for structured mechanisms that couple reasons, memory, self-state and inhibition to action selection in a language-agent implementation whose control components can be selectively disabled. In a seven-dataset baseline lesion matrix comprising 74,352 calls, the high-stochasticity comparator was more unpredictable than the structured-control variant in 7/7 datasets, whereas targeted reason and veto lesions reduced the expected structured-control profiles in 7/7 datasets each. In a matched-interface control spanning 26,946 generations, the structured agent maintained stronger action-field coupling than all stochastic, post-hoc, scrambled and verbosity controls across every dataset. The primary behavioral test removed free-form trace wording from the evaluation: 57,816 scored records showed the structured-control variant exceeding the high-stochasticity comparator or the reason/veto lesions in 7/7 datasets for all predefined behavioral components. Later open-weight runs extended the no-context controls to Qwen2.5 7B, 14B and 32B and to an independent Mistral-7B family across 20 task families and three agent scaffolds; no-fields, scrambled-context and distribution-matched controls failed to recover structured action control. A three-annotator blinded audit over 1,200 overlap items preserved high agreement. Strict entropy matching, strict token/compute matching and a formal counterfactual-flip stress test did not meet their gates and are treated as limitations. Stochastic unpredictability did not reproduce structured, action-coupled control in this implemented agent family.

</details>


### [196] [Ambig-DS: A Benchmark for Task-Framing Ambiguity in Data-Science Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09698)
*Josefa Lia Stoisser,Marc Boubnovski Martell,Sidsel Boldsen,Kaspar Märtens,Robert Kitchen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Ambig-DS introduces two diagnostic suites (51 target-ambiguity tasks on DSBench, 61 objective-ambiguity tasks on MLE-bench) to test whether data-science agents detect underspecification. Results show agents silently commit to wrong framings rather than fail execution; asking one clarifying question recovers most loss, but agents cannot reliably decide when to ask.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Silent misframing is a critical but unmeasured failure mode as agents shift from co-pilots to auto-pilots. Existing benchmarks only evaluate pipeline execution, ignoring whether agents recognize underspecified task framings that lead to clean but incorrect outputs.

Method: Two diagnostic suites: Ambig-DS-Target (51 tasks, prediction-target ambiguity on DSBench tabular modeling) and Ambig-DS-Objective (61 tasks, evaluation-objective ambiguity on MLE-bench Kaggle competitions). Each task pairs a fully specified version with an ambiguous variant via controlled edits; human-and-LLM verification confirms multiple plausible interpretations. Tested across five agents from efficient to frontier-class models.

Result: (i) Failures are silent commitments—wrong-target submissions or wrong-metric/baseline submissions—not execution errors. (ii) One clarifying question recovers most loss under ideal conditions. (iii) Agents cannot reliably decide when to ask: permissive prompts cause over-asking on clear tasks, conservative prompts cause silent defaulting on ambiguous ones.

Conclusion: Recognizing target and objective underspecification, not pipeline execution, is the bottleneck missing from standard DS-agent evaluations. Clarification capabilities are insufficient without reliable meta-cognition about when clarification is needed.

Abstract: As data-science agents shift from co-pilots to auto-pilots, silent misframing becomes a critical failure mode. Agents quietly commit to plausible but unintended task framings, producing clean, executable artifacts that hide their incorrect assessment of the task. Existing benchmarks score whether the pipeline runs, ignoring whether the agent recognized the task was underspecified. We introduce Ambig-DS, two diagnostic suites: one for prediction-target ambiguity (Ambig-DS-Target, 51 tasks built on DSBench, a tabular modeling benchmark) and one for evaluation-objective ambiguity (Ambig-DS-Objective, 61 tasks built on MLE-bench, a Kaggle-style ML competition benchmark), constructed so that scoring uses each source benchmark's original evaluator. For every task we pair the original, fully specified version with an ambiguous variant produced by controlled edits; a human-and-LLM verification pipeline confirms each variant admits multiple plausible interpretations with decision-relevant consequences. The suites are analyzed independently and ambiguity lowers performance in both. Across five agents spanning efficient to frontier-class models, we find in our controlled diagnostic setting: (i) failures are silent commitments: wrong-target submissions on Target, wrong-metric or non-committal baseline submissions on Objective, rather than execution errors; (ii) allowing the agent to ask one clarifying question recovers much of the loss under idealized conditions, suggesting missing framing information drives a substantial part of the observed degradation; but (iii) agents cannot reliably tell when to use it: permissive prompts induce over-asking on clear tasks, while conservative prompts induce silent defaulting on ambiguous ones. Recognizing target and objective underspecification, not pipeline execution, is the bottleneck missing from standard DS-agent evaluations.

</details>


### [197] [Medical Model Synthesis Architectures: A Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09716)
*Katherine M. Collins,Marlene Berke,Ilia Sucholutsky,Ayman Ali,Adrian Weller,Timothy J. O'Donnell,Tyler Brooke-Wilson,Lionel Wong,Joshua B. Tenenbaum*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MedMSA is a framework combining language models for knowledge retrieval with formal probabilistic models to produce calibrated, transparent clinical predictions under uncertainty, demonstrated for differential diagnosis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medicine involves high-stakes uncertainty in clinical judgments, and current AI systems lack calibrated reasoning under uncertainty and transparency in their reasoning processes.

Method: The MedMSA framework uses language models to retrieve relevant prior knowledge, then constructs a formal probabilistic model to support calibrated and verifiable inferences under uncertainty.

Result: A proof-of-concept demonstrates the framework for differential diagnosis, producing an uncertainty-weighted list of potential diagnoses explaining a patient's symptoms.

Conclusion: The framework enables practically useful yet formally transparent clinical predictions and shows promise for safe clinical collaborations, with future directions for broader application.

Abstract: Medicine is rife with high-stakes uncertainty. Doctors routinely make clinical judgments and decisions that juggle many fundamental unknowns, like predictions about what might be causing a patients' symptoms or decisions about what treatment to try next. Despite increasing interest in developing AI systems that aid or even replace doctors in clinical settings, current systems struggle with calibrated reasoning under uncertainty, and are often deeply opaque about their reasoning. We propose a framework for AI systems that can make practically useful but formally transparent clinical predictions under uncertainty. Given a clinical situation, our framework (MedMSA) uses language models to retrieve relevant prior knowledge, but constructs a formal probabilistic model to support calibrated and verifiable inferences under uncertainty. We show how an initial proof-of-concept of this framework can be used for differential diagnosis, producing an uncertainty-weighted list of potential diagnoses that could explain a patients' symptoms, and discuss future applications and directions for applying this framework more generally for safe clinical collaborations.

</details>


### [198] [Primal-Dual Guided Decoding for Constrained Discrete Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09749)
*Federico Tomasi,Dmitrii Moor,Alice Wang,Mounia Lalmas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes primal-dual guided decoding, an inference-time method for enforcing global constraints in discrete diffusion models via adaptive Lagrangian multipliers and KL-regularized optimization, achieving improved constraint satisfaction without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enforcing global property constraints during generation with discrete diffusion models remains an open challenge, as existing methods lack a principled way to incorporate constraints at inference time without retraining.

Method: Formulates constrained generation as a KL-regularized optimization problem, solved online via adaptive Lagrangian multipliers. Token logits are modified by an additive constraint-dependent bias derived from optimal KL-regularized projection, with multipliers updated by mirror descent based on constraint violation.

Result: Evaluated on topical text generation, molecular design, and music playlist generation; shows improved constraint satisfaction while preserving domain-specific quality metrics, with support for multiple simultaneous constraints and formal violation bounds.

Conclusion: Primal-dual guided decoding provides an effective, retraining-free inference-time method for constrained generation in discrete diffusion models, applicable across diverse domains with a single algorithmic framework.

Abstract: Discrete diffusion models generate structured sequences by progressively unmasking tokens, but enforcing global property constraints during generation remains an open challenge. We propose primal-dual guided decoding, an inference-time method that formulates constrained generation as a KL-regularised optimisation problem and solves it online via adaptive Lagrangian multipliers. At each denoising step, the method modifies token logits through an additive, constraint-dependent bias, with multipliers updated by mirror descent based on constraint violation. The bias arises as the optimal KL-regularised projection of the constraint, so the constrained distribution remains as close as possible to the model's unconstrained distribution while still satisfying the constraint. The method requires no retraining and no additional model evaluations beyond standard sampling, supports multiple simultaneous constraints, and provides formal bounds on constraint violation. We evaluate our approach on topical text generation, molecular design, and music playlist generation, showing that a single algorithm instantiated via domain-specific scoring functions improves constraint satisfaction while preserving relevant domain-specific quality metrics.

</details>


### [199] [UTS at PsyDefDetect: Multi-Agent Councils and Absence-Based Reasoning for Defense Mechanism Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09769)
*Dima Galat,Marian-Andrei Rizoiu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-phase deliberative council of Gemini 2.5 agents with prompt-level clinical rules and targeted override ensembles from fine-tuned Qwen3.5 models classifies psychological defense mechanisms using DMRS, achieving second place (F1 0.406) among 64 teams.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Defense mechanisms in emotional support dialogues are defined by what is absent (missing affect, blocked cognition, denied reality), posing unique classification challenges. Accurate detection is crucial for clinical assessment but existing methods struggle with these absence-based definitions and minority class bias.

Method: 1) Affect-cognition integration spectrum encoded as prompt-level clinical rules (+11.4pp F1). 2) Multi-phase deliberative council of Gemini 2.5 agents where class-specific advocates rate evidence strength (F1 0.382, top-5). 3) Targeted override ensemble from three fine-tuned Qwen3.5 models with 16 overrides (+2.4pp), selected by a structured multi-agent system (builder, critic, regression guard) that outperformed 8 prior attempts combined in one iteration.

Result: Overall F1 0.406, second place among 64 teams. The council alone achieved F1 0.382 (top-5). Prompt-level rules contributed the largest single gain (+11.4pp). However, the council exhibited a systematic "L7 attractor" bias: 59-80% of stable minority predictions were incorrect due to emotional content defaulting to the majority class.

Conclusion: Multi-agent deliberative architectures with clinically-informed prompt rules are effective for absence-based classification tasks, but systematic majority-class bias requires targeted override mechanisms. The structured multi-agent override selection system proved highly efficient, achieving more improvement in one iteration than 8 prior attempts combined.

Abstract: This paper describes our system for classifying psychological defense mechanisms in emotional support dialogues using the Defense Mechanism Rating Scales (DMRS), placing second (F1 0.406) among 64 teams.1 A central insight is that defense mechanisms are defined by what is absent: missing affect, blocked cognition, denied reality. We encode this as an affect-cognition integration spectrum in prompt-level clinical rules, which account for the largest single gain (+11.4pp F1). Our architecture is a multi-phase deliberative council of Gemini 2.5 agents where class-specific advocates rate evidence strength rather than voting, achieving F1 0.382 with no fine-tuning - a top-5 result on its own. We find, however, that the council is confidently wrong about minority classes: 59-80% of stable minority predictions are incorrect, driven by a systematic "L7 attractor" in which emotional content defaults to the majority class. A targeted override ensemble from three fine-tuned Qwen3.5 models applies 16 overrides (+2.4pp), selected by a structured multi-agent system (builder, critic, regression guard) that produced a larger F1 gain in one iteration than 8 prior attempts combined.

</details>


### [200] [Marrying Generative Model of Healthcare Events with Digital Twin of Social Determinants of Health for Disease Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09771)
*Ziquan Wei,Tingting Dan,Guorong Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a conditioned latent diffusion framework that integrates ICD-coded proxies of social determinants of health (SDoH) with multi-organ sensor data (brain, heart, liver, kidney imaging traits) for in silico disease modeling. The model introduces a geometric diffusion model for brain networks alongside diffusion models for tabular organ data, achieving SOTA results on UK Biobank data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative models for disease prediction rely on event-level representations from hospital/registry data and lack explicit modeling of social determinants of health (SDoH), limiting personalized disease modeling and clinical decision support.

Method: A conditioned latent diffusion framework with (1) a novel geometric diffusion model for temporal evolution of brain networks (graph-structured connectivity), (2) parallel diffusion models for tabular organ data, and (3) integration with ICD-coded SDoH proxies (chapters Z and V-Y in ICD-10) for simulated intervention and reasoning of future disease trajectories.

Result: Significant improvements over SOTA human disease autoregressive models and imaging trait generative baselines on UK Biobank data (44,834 brain, 23,987 heart, 28,722 liver, 32,155 kidney imaging samples, and ~500k medical history sequences, ages 25-89).

Conclusion: Incorporating SDoH proxies into a multi-organ conditioned latent diffusion framework enhances disease trajectory modeling and personalized clinical decision support.

Abstract: Despite the central role of sensor-derived measurements such as imaging traits and plasma biomarkers in biomedical research and clinical practice, existing generative models for disease prediction largely depend on event-level representations from hospital and registry data. Given the multi-factorial nature of human disease, the absence of explicit modeling of social determinants of health (SDoH), even in the limited form of ICD-coded proxies (chapters Z and V--Y in ICD-10), limits the capacity for personalized disease modeling and clinical decision support. To address this limitation, we propose a generative model with ICD-coded proxies of SDoH for \textit{in silico} modeling of disease reasoning, a conditioned latent diffusion framework that establishes the connection between multi-organ sensor data with tokenized healthcare events. Specifically, we introduce a novel geometric diffusion model to characterize the temporal evolution of complex data representation such as brain networks (region-to-region connectivity encoded in a graph), in parallel with diffusion models for tabular data from other organ systems. Together, we integrate the generative model with digitalized SDoH proxies (coined \modelname{}) for simulated intervention and reasoning of future disease trajectories. We conduct extensive experiments on the UK Biobank (UKB) dataset, which contains organ-specific imaging traits, including brain (44,834), heart (23,987), liver (28,722), and kidney (32,155), along with nearly 500k medical history sequences (age range: 25$\sim$89 years). Our \modelname{} achieves significant improvements over state-of-the-art human disease autoregressive models and imaging trait generative baselines.

</details>


### [201] [Attribution-based Explanations for Markov Decision Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09780)
*Paul Kobialka,Andrea Pferscher,Francesco Leofante,Erika Ábrahám,Silvia Lizeth Tapia Tarifa,Einar Broch Johnsen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces attribution-based explanation techniques for Markov Decision Processes (MDPs), providing formal characterizations of attributions for states and execution paths, and leveraging strategy synthesis for efficient computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing attribution techniques focus on static input features at single points in time and fail to generalize to sequential decision-making settings. This paper aims to fill this gap by developing attribution methods for MDPs.

Method: The authors provide a formal characterization of what attributions should represent in MDPs, focusing on importance scores for individual states and execution paths. They leverage strategy synthesis techniques to efficiently compute these scores despite the non-determinism inherent in MDPs.

Result: The approach is evaluated on five case-studies, demonstrating its utility in providing interpretable insights into the logic of sequential decision-making agents.

Conclusion: Attribution-based explanations can be effectively extended to sequential decision-making in MDPs by formally characterizing state and path attributions and using strategy synthesis for efficient computation.

Abstract: Attribution techniques explain the outcome of an AI model by assigning a numerical score to its inputs. So far, these techniques have mainly focused on attributing importance to static input features at a single point in time, and thus fail to generalize to sequential decision-making settings. This paper fills this gap by introducing techniques to generate attribution-based explanations for Markov Decision Processes (MDPs). We give a formal characterization of what attributions should represent in MDPs, focusing on explanations that assign importance scores to both individual states and execution paths. We show how importance scores can be computed by leveraging techniques for strategy synthesis, enabling the efficient computation of these scores despite the non-determinism inherent in an MDP. We evaluate our approach on five case-studies, demonstrating its utility in providing interpretable insights into the logic of sequential decision-making agents.

</details>


### [202] [EnactToM: An Evolving Benchmark for Functional Theory of Mind in Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09826)
*Gurusha Juneja,Dylan Lu,Saaket Agashe,Parth Diwane,Edward Gunn,Jayanth Srinivasa,Gaowen Liu,William Yang Wang,Yali Du,Xin Eric Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EnactToM is a benchmark of 300 embodied multi-agent tasks testing functional Theory of Mind (acting on implicit beliefs) in 3D household environments. Frontier models score 0% on functional tasks vs 45% on literal belief questions, with 93% of failures due to epistemic coordination breakdowns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ToM benchmarks test literal belief tracking via direct questions, not the ability to act optimally on implicit beliefs in embodied multi-agent settings (functional ToM).

Method: Introduces EnactToM, a benchmark of 300 formally verified 3D embodied multi-agent tasks with partial observability, private information, constrained communication, and an evolving task generation mechanism.

Result: All seven frontier models score 0.0% Pass^3 on the hard split for functional task completion, averaging 45.0% on literal belief probes; 93% of failures stem from epistemic coordination breakdowns.

Conclusion: Current AI models lack functional ToM in embodied settings despite some literal ToM capability, highlighting a critical gap requiring targeted improvement in epistemic coordination.

Abstract: Theory of Mind (ToM), the ability to track others epistemic state, makes humans efficient collaborators. AI agents need the same capacity in multi agent settings, yet existing benchmarks mostly test literal ToM by asking direct belief questions. The ability act optimally on implicit beliefs in embodied environments, called functional ToM, remains largely untested. We introduce EnactToM, an evolving benchmark of 300 embodied multi-agent tasks set in a 3D household with partial observability, private information, and constrained communication. Each task is formally verified for solvability and required epistemic depth, and new tasks are generated increase difficulty as models improve. On the hard split, all seven evaluated frontier models score 0.0% Pass^3 on functional task completion, while averaging 45.0% on literal belief probes. Manual analysis traces 93% of sampled failures to epistemic coordination breakdowns such as withheld information, ignored partner constraints, and misallocated messages, providing a concrete target for future work.

</details>


### [203] [Yield Curve Forecasting using Machine Learning and Econometrics: A Comparative Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09842)
*Aman Singh,Tokunbo Ogunfunmi,Sanjiv Das*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper compares econometric/time-series models, classical ML, and deep learning methods for forecasting U.S. Treasury yield curves over 47 years of daily data. ARIMA and naive econometric models outperform most other methods overall, though TimeGPT, LGBM, and RNNs perform best among ML approaches. The study also examines stationary vs. nonstationary data as input for deep learning models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite ML's success in NLP and computer vision, its impact on time-series forecasting (especially in finance) remains disputed. Treasury yield curves are critical for bond markets (larger than equity markets), yet many modern methods (particularly deep learning) have not been thoroughly tested on yield curve forecasting.

Method: The authors compare ARIMA and its extensions, naive benchmarks, ensemble methods, RNNs, and multiple transformers on daily U.S. Treasury yield curve data spanning 47 years. They also investigate the impact of stationary vs. nonstationary data inputs for deep learning models.

Result: ARIMA and naive econometric models outperform other methods overall, except in one time block. Among ML methods, TimeGPT, LGBM, and RNNs perform best.

Conclusion: Traditional econometric and time-series methods remain competitive and can outperform sophisticated deep learning approaches for yield curve forecasting, though certain ML methods (TimeGPT, LGBM, RNNs) show promise. Data stationarity is an important consideration for deep learning input.

Abstract: While machine learning has revolutionized many fields such as natural language processing (NLP) and computer vision, its impact on time-series forecasting is still widely disputed, especially in the finance domain. This paper compares forecasting performance on U.S. Treasury yield curve data across econometrics/time-series analysis, classical machine learning, and deep learning methods, using daily data over 47 years. The Treasury yield curve is important because it is widely used by every participant in the bond markets, which are larger than equity markets. We examine a variety of methods that have not been tested on yield curve forecasting, especially deep learning algorithms. The algorithms include the Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average (ARIMA) model and its extensions, naive benchmarks, ensemble methods, Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs), and multiple transformers built for forecasting. ARIMA and naive econometric models outperform other models overall, except in one time block. Of the machine learning methods, TimeGPT, LGBM and RNNs perform the best. Furthermore, the paper explores whether stationary or nonstationary data are more appropriate as input to deep learning models.

</details>


### [204] [The Metacognitive Probe: Five Behavioural Calibration Diagnostics for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09844)
*Rafael C. T. Oliveira*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Metacognitive Probe, a five-task diagnostic that decomposes LLM confidence behavior into distinct dimensions, revealing that frontier models like Gemini 2.5 Flash can show high calibration in one task but poor cross-task difficulty prediction, highlighting deficiencies masked by composite benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Composite benchmarks (MMLU, BIG-Bench, HELM, GPQA) only evaluate whether a model produces correct responses, but do not assess whether the model knows when its response is wrong. Models can score high on these benchmarks yet be wildly overconfident in specific narrow domains that aggregate metrics cannot surface. The paper aims to fill this gap.

Method: The authors develop the Metacognitive Probe, a 5-task, 15-slot diagnostic that decomposes LLM confidence behavior into five behaviorally-distinct dimensions: confidence calibration (T1-CC), epistemic vigilance (T2-EV), knowledge boundary (T3-KB), calibration range (T4-CR), and reasoning-chain validation (T5-RCV). The instrument is inspired by Flavell (1979) and Nelson and Narens (1990) but focuses on observable confidence-correctness alignment. It was evaluated on 8 frontier models and 69 humans.

Result: A headline finding is a 47-point within-model dissociation in Gemini 2.5 Flash: it achieved panel-best within-task calibration (T1-CC = 88; Spearman rho = +0.551) but panel-worst cross-task difficulty prediction (T4-CR = 41; sigma_conf = 1.4 across twelve factoids). The pre-specified human developmental hypothesis was falsified.

Conclusion: The Metacognitive Probe successfully surfaces confidence-related deficiencies that composite benchmarks fail to detect, demonstrating that frontier models can exhibit high calibration in one dimension while being severely miscalibrated in another. This suggests the need for multi-dimensional evaluation of model confidence beyond simple accuracy metrics.

Abstract: The Metacognitive Probe is an exploratory five-task, 15-slot diagnostic that decomposes an LLM's confidence behaviour into five behaviourally-distinct dimensions: confidence calibration (T1-CC), epistemic vigilance (T2-EV), knowledge boundary (T3-KB), calibration range (T4-CR), and reasoning-chain validation (T5-RCV). It is evaluated on N=8 frontier models and N=69 humans. The instrument is motivated by Flavell (1979) and Nelson and Narens (1990) but operates on observable confidence-correctness alignment; it is not a validated cross-species metacognition scale, and the pre-specified human developmental hypothesis was falsified.
  Composite benchmarks (MMLU, BIG-Bench, HELM, GPQA) ask whether a model produces a correct response. They are silent on whether the model knows when its response is wrong. A model can score 80 on a composite calibration benchmark and still be wildly overconfident in narrow pockets the aggregate cannot surface. The Metacognitive Probe surfaces those pockets.
  Our headline is a 47-point within-model dissociation in Gemini 2.5 Flash: panel-best within-task calibration (T1-CC = 88; Spearman rho = +0.551, 95% CI [+0.14, +0.80], p = 0.005) and panel-worst cross-task difficulty prediction (T4-CR = 41; sigma_conf = 1.4 across twelve factoids).

</details>


### [205] [Fairness of Explanations in Artificial Intelligence (AI): A Unifying Framework, Axioms, and Future Direction toward Responsible AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09852)
*Gideon Popoola,John Sheppard*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies a blind spot between algorithmic fairness and explainable AI (XAI): a model can produce fair outcomes while having unfair reasoning processes (procedural bias). It provides the first unified review of explanation fairness, proposes a conditional invariance framework formalizing explanation fairness, introduces a seven-dimensional taxonomy, identifies three generative mechanisms of explanation inequity, and proposes a six-step evaluation workflow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
